Munger's Verdict

MUNGER SYNTHESIS — TRULIEVE CANNABIS CORP. (NYSE: TRLV)

Date: 2026-08-26 · Price: $11.08 (24-Aug) / $11.32 [CALC] · Market cap: ~$2.13B · Shares: 192.57M Revision 6 — FINAL. Four headline claims from Revision 5 are withdrawn. The correction moves the estimate up and the width out.

0. THIS TIME MY ERRORS DID NOT CANCEL — THEY ALL LEANED THE SAME WAY

Passes 3 and 4 found errors pointing away from each other. I mistook that for convergence, was corrected, and stopped claiming it. Pass 5 found something different and worse: six of seven priceable errors running in one direction, and that direction was harsh.

I anchored the discount rate to a bond coupon that already prices the IRS claim, then charged the claim again in full. I read an accounting recognition threshold as a forecast of cash. I took maintenance capital from a half-year that still contained a business I no longer own. I discounted the Level 3 asset for being a management estimate and took the Level 3 liability at face. Each is defensible alone. End to end they are worth $0.85–$1.55 a share, all one way.

That is not a pattern of error. It is a temperament, and I can name where it came from: I was told four times that my bear case was too harsh at the floor, and I learned to be harsh in ways that are hard to argue with. A discount rate anchored to a real bond price is difficult to challenge. A probability borrowed from the company's own accountants sounds like humility. Neither is, and the tell is that they both move the same way.

Corrected: band $3.13–$9.45, central $5.84. Price $11.08.

1. WITHDRAWN — THE 250bp "COST OF MONEY" FINDING

Revision 5's proudest discovery, in its thesis sentence: "the 10.5% notes replaced 8.0% paper redeemed the same month — this company's own senior secured cost of money rose 250 basis points while the regulatory news was improving." It is wrong four ways, and all four are in the paragraph I quoted from.

(i) It was not a refinancing. The 8.0% notes were redeemed for cash on 5 December 2025 ($373.0M paid on $368.0M principal). The 10.5% notes mature 17 December 2030 and are non-call until 17 December 2027, dating the issue to twelve days after the redemption — and the filing states the proceeds are for "capital expenditures and general corporate purposes." New money did not retire old debt.

(ii) The company deleveraged. $368.0M of notes plus $15.8M of Blue Ridge mortgage = $383.8M retired against $200M raised — a $183.8M net reduction, termed out to 2030. A borrower the market has just repriced 250bp wider does not halve his funded debt in the same month. This is a capital-allocation decision I would praise in any other company, and I read the same paragraph and concluded the opposite.

(iii) It is a term and vintage artefact, not a spread. The retired notes were due 2026 — months from maturity on 5 December. The new paper runs five years; the 1-to-5-year credit curve for a sub-investment-grade issuer routinely carries 150–300bp. And the 8.0% coupon was struck years earlier, in the Harvest era, when the risk-free rate was ~1%:

8.0%  − ~1.0% risk-free (issue vintage)  =  ~700bp credit spread
10.5% − ~4.0% risk-free (Dec-2025)       =  ~650bp credit spread

On a spread basis this borrower's cost of money did not rise. It tightened. The 250bp is mostly the move in the risk-free rate between two issue dates four years apart — the identical level-versus-basis error I convicted myself of one page earlier when I withdrew the §280E-era ROIC.

(iv) Public versus private. The retired notes traded on the CSE; the new ones are an unlisted private placement to undisclosed buyers, non-call for two years. An illiquidity and structure premium of 50–150bp is conventional.

What survives: the 10.5% coupon is a real, current, entity-specific price for a five-year senior secured claim, and using it as the base of a cost-of-equity build is right — it is a large improvement on the $970 thousand note it replaced. What is deleted is the story I read into it. It is an informative price. It is not informative about the thing I said it was.

2. THE DISCOUNT RATE — THE TAX CLAIM WAS IN IT ALL ALONG

Revision 5 asserted the rate was "built from one observed price plus one generic spread, so it does not re-charge the tax claim, the non-disclosure, Harvest or the licence formula." Two of those four are false.

Decompose it: 10.5% = ~4.0% risk-free + ~650bp credit spread. Now ask what a senior secured lender was pricing in December 2025. Uncertain tax positions stood at $668.4M at 31-Dec-2025 — larger than the $598.2M I charge — against $255.5M of cash. §381.986 was in force and the licence formula was public. It is not credible that a lender demanding 10.5% at par attributed zero basis points to a government claim two and a half times the company's cash.

(The other two hold: medical-only cost-line non-disclosure did not exist until Q2'26, and Harvest did not exist until June 2026. Neither could be in a December 2025 coupon.)

And it is worse than an ordinary double charge. I then set p = 100% — I charged the crystallisation as a certainty. Once you have booked the disaster in full, the only remaining surprises are favourable, so paying a risk premium on top of it is arithmetic run backwards.

I strip 100bp for the overlap: r = 13–16%, central 14%. I take the conservative end of the 100–200bp the evidence supports, because attributing more of a 650bp spread to any single item is guesswork, and I say plainly that the exact attribution is unknowable.

3. THE TAX CLAIM — A THRESHOLD IS NOT A FORECAST

Revision 5: "Under ASC 740-10 that is management's own judgment... I have no basis for assuming a more favourable outcome than management's own accounting. I adopt p = 100%." The first clause is right and the second does not follow from it.

ASC 740-10-25-6 recognises a tax position only when it is more likely than not to be sustained upon examination on the technical merits, presuming (25-7) an examiner with full knowledge. ASC 740-10-30-7 then requires a full reserve when the threshold fails. So a full reserve means exactly one thing: management concluded P(sustained) ≤ 50%. It is a binary bookkeeping switch, deliberately blind to the practical reality that large tax disputes settle. It says nothing about what gets written on the cheque, and it is built that way on purpose.

That bounds p to (50%, 100%]. I took the endpoint and called it deference. It is not deference — it is my own bear corner, installed as the central case on the input carrying a fifth of my range, while six other inputs sat near their midpoints.

I adopt p = 75%, the neutral midpoint, and publish the sensitivity.

And I now charge the interest I was citing as evidence. Revision 5 argued the claim was severe because "interest compounds at $15.2M/quarter" — and then charged $598.2M flat over five years, accreting nothing. Roughly $60M a year appeared in neither the cash-flow build nor the claim's present value. Corrected:

Principal at p = 75%:        0.75 × $598.2M = $448.65M
Accreting at ~10% (IRS underpayment rate), five level instalments:
  payment = $448.65M × 0.263797 = $118.35M/yr
  PV at r = 14%: $118.35M × 3.4331 = $406.3M

One further correction to my own evidence: the UTP balance fell from $668.4M to $598.2M over six months. That is the Harvest deconsolidation carrying its own positions out — I established the $131M reconciliation myself in Revision 2 — not a shrinking liability. But Revision 5 printed the compounding trend without mentioning that the balance it charges is $70.2M lower than six months earlier. Both facts belong on the page.

4. THE REBUILD

Central Range
Revenue ($222M × 4) $888M
Gross profit @ 63% $559M
plus D&A-in-COGS [CALC] +$50M (5.6% of revenue) 5–7%
less SG&A (incl. SBC) −$332M (37.4%) $315–345M
EBITDA $277M $258–306M
Total medical D&A (opex $77M + COGS $50M) $127M
less gross cash interest −$45M flagged unverified
less cash tax @26% × (277 − 127 − 45) −$27M
less maintenance capex −$60M $50–80M
Operating owner earnings to equity $145M

The EBITDA restatement stands, confirmed a third way. FY2025 pre-tax income $91.5M + total other expense $52.0M = EBIT $143.5M; against GAAP EBITDA of $330M that implies total D&A of $186.5M, less ~$117.6M of operating-line D&A, leaving $68.9M = 5.8% of revenue inside cost of goods sold. Under ASC 330 the depreciation of a 750,000 sq ft indoor cultivation facility is capitalised into inventory and released through COGS, above the gross-profit line where I could not see it for four revisions. My 5.6% central is, if anything, conservative.

Maintenance capex, corrected for basis. Revision 5 annualised H1'26 capex of $34.5M to $70M — and H1'26 is consolidated, with five months of Harvest inside it, which I wrote in the same sentence and adjusted by nothing. That is the exact basis mismatch I have convicted three other parties of. Carving Harvest's ~25% share gives medical-only H1 of ~$27.5M, annualising to ~$55M. The two quarters also disagree badly — Q1 annualises to $54M, Q2 to $84M. $60M central, range $50–80M.

⚠️ The FY2026 figure of "up to $95M" — a ceiling, and the sole basis for every growth-capex term — remains [MEDIA], an unverified X summary of the Q2 call naming Texas, a state this company does not operate in. The central grid is immune to it (at R = r the growth term cancels identically), but both corners depend on it.

Harvest and the tax claim, now treated symmetrically. Revision 5 took a 13.6% reliability haircut off Harvest for being an unaudited Level 3 management estimate — and took the $598.2M uncertain tax position, an unaudited Level 3 management estimate in the same filing, at face. Sceptical about the asset, credulous about the liability, both in the same direction. I now handle estimate uncertainty on both through the range rather than a haircut on one: Harvest $170M central (range $150–188.5M); the claim through p ∈ (50%, 100%]. Carrying value at 30 June is $185.2M after the $3.3M equity-method loss.

On SG&A, the input I call Load-Bearing Factor #2 — the number I did not cite. Management stated on the Q2 call: "Second quarter SG&A was $102 million, or 38% of revenue. Adjusted SG&A at 32% of revenue was comparable to last year." My $332M (37.4%) is anchored to two GAAP quarters, one of which contains a deconsolidation, a $403.3M loss and an NYSE uplist. Removing SBC (~2.5 points, which I am right to charge) leaves roughly 3.5 points — ~$31M annualised — of genuinely restructuring-related cost inside my anchor. My counter is real and I keep it: this company runs ~$97M/yr of "non-recurring" charges that recur every year. But that is a reason to distrust the adjusted figure, not a reason to leave it uncited, and it is why the bull end of my SG&A range is $315M.

The grid — equity-side; Equity = OE ÷ r + cash $325.4M + Harvest − PV of the tax claim

r = 16% r = 14% r = 13%
Value/share $5.27 $5.84 $6.20

Buy Zone: $3.15–$5.85. Fair value limit is the central estimate; the floor is the bear corner.

Honest range, all seven inputs at their corners: $3.13 – $9.45 (3.02x). Bear: SG&A $345M · D&A-in-COGS 5% · capex $80M · r 16% · Harvest $150M · R 10% · p 100%. Bull: SG&A $315M · D&A-in-COGS 7% · capex $50M · r 13% · Harvest $188.5M · R 18% · p 50%.

p-sensitivity at r = 14%: p = 0% → $7.95 · p = 50% → $6.55 · p = 75% → $5.84 · p = 100% → $5.14.

5. WITHDRAWN — "NO COHERENT CONSTRUCTION REACHES $11.08"

Revision 5 graded conviction High on that sentence. My own EBITDA correction falsified it in the same draft, and I hardened it instead of checking it.

EV at $11.08 = mkt cap $2,133.7 + debt $485.3 − cash $325.4 − Harvest $170.0  =  $2,123.6M
÷ restated EBITDA $277M                                     =  7.67x  ex-claim
including the claim at my own PV $406.3M                    =  9.13x

Against my own dossier's peer table: Green Thumb 7.3x, Curaleaf 9.6x, Tilray 20.0x. Run it backwards at Curaleaf's multiple, holding my own debt stack, my own Harvest mark and my own tax charge: $11.75 — above the price. At Revision 4's $227M the same computation gave $9.23 and the claim was true. I fixed the metric in the buyer's favour by $50M and did not trace the consequence to the sentence the fix had just killed — the same failure as Revision 4's convergence claim, in a new location.

What is true instead: no discounted-cash-flow construction in this record reaches $11.08. A peer-multiple construction does. And I reject that frame by argument rather than by pretending it is absent:

  • EV/EBITDA ignores capital intensity. $60M of maintenance capex is 22% of $277M of EBITDA, and $127M of depreciation is 46% of it. The buyer is paying for cash that the grow house consumes before it arrives.
  • The peer table is unusable. Its real vintage is October 2022 — four years stale, disclosed nowhere in the dossier — and the current source (Motley Fool, June 2026) gives Green Thumb at ~3.5x and Curaleaf at ~15.5x, a range that implies $2.95 to $20.21. A frame that brackets everything settles nothing.
  • §280E is a tax provision and EBITDA is pre-tax, so Schedule III relief cannot lift EBITDA by a dollar — which is what broke Lynch's bridge and applies equally to anyone re-rating this on a multiple.

But I concede in §9 that this buyer "is not wrong about the multiple, he is wrong about the metric." A man with a defensible multiple applied to my own corrected metric arrives at the price. That does not make him right about the business. It makes the sentence wrong, and it is why conviction is Moderate.

And the companion claim goes too. Revision 5 said the bull corner with the claim erased was $9.44, "still 17% below the price." Corrected it is $10.89 — within 2% of $11.08.

6. STRESS TEST, MOAT, AND THE COUNCIL

Stress test, recomputed from the restated base (the dossier has none; item 10 is ❌). Contribution 68.6c per revenue dollar (variable COGS 31.4% after removing D&A-in-COGS), less variable SG&A 13.1c → pre-tax drag $0.555, post-tax $0.411. Buffer $145M.

Naive:      145 / 0.411                                    =  39.7%
Piecewise:  pre-tax income $105M / 0.555 = 21.3% (shield exhausts; OE still +$67M)
            then 67 / 0.555 = a further 13.6%              =  34.9%

FCF turns negative at a ~35% revenue decline; ~23% if the claim is instead settled on a $60M/yr cash instalment plan. The drag rises when the shield exhausts, so breakeven arrives sooner than the naive figure — the sign is right, after four consecutive revisions in which it was not. This is a scenario, not the base case: like-for-like revenue is −2.2% (roughly 8 of the headline 10.3 points are a deconsolidation artefact, which Lynch alone spotted) and medical-only is +4% sequential.

Moat Tribunal: 1/5 SEVERE (Psychologist). 4/5 MODERATE (Buffett, Biologist, Historian, Anthropologist). 0 MINOR/NONE. The moat is regulatory scarcity, not consumer preference: 22% of Florida doors converting to 35% flower share, 78% medical retention, 2x the next competitor's oil volume. But §381.986 Fla. Stat. mints four new MMTCs per 100,000 new patients; 22 are queued; 28 operators become ~50. A moat granted by a licence board is un-granted by a licence board. 60/50/40% off your first three visits, into three straight years of falling flower prices with 24.4% of operators profitable, is a coupon with a licence attached — cost position, not pricing power. No franchise premium; no floor adjustment. The Ohio Attorney General's antitrust action naming Trulieve among nine MSOs attacks precisely the shelf access scale was supposed to buy.

Growth. The reinvestment identity g = b × R with the base at R = r, which is algebraically identical to the maintenance build regardless of b — so the central grid does not depend on the [MEDIA] capex ceiling. The case for R ≈ r: 28 operators becoming ~50, falling flower prices, and units +8% against revenue +4% (price falling). The case for R > r, which I under-weighted for four revisions: Georgia SB 220, enacted 12 May 2026, authorising medical flower and vaporisation for patients 21+ and removing the end-stage requirement — a legislated demand expansion in a limited-licence state where Trulieve already cultivates everything it sells. Hence corners at 10% and 18%.

Lynch's BUY does not survive, on two grounds: his $350–450M "medical-only" EBITDA is the consolidated Q1 figure containing Harvest (Q1 ended 31 March; the deconsolidation was 3 June), and §280E is a tax while EBITDA is pre-tax, which removes his bridge entirely. His catalyst is spent. And the direct test nobody ran: adjusted net income was $20M in Q1'26 with zero days of §280E relief and $20M in Q2'26 with ~70 days of it. He was nonetheless right about the deconsolidation artefact, and no one else caught it.

Buffett's "$283–392M bull case" is $2,180M × 13%/18% — the price back-solved, not a forecast. That catch survives all five passes.

Bear facts the council omitted: the cash cushion is rented, not earned (cash +$69.9M in H1 while private placement notes rose $59.1M, at 10.5% senior secured); FY2025 adjusted EBITDA $427M against GAAP $330M; book equity $765.9M [CALC, by subtraction, includes NCI] = $3.98/share, P/B ≥ 2.79x; $177M into two failed ballot initiatives; CEO 0 purchases, 7 sales, 1,699,007 shares, ~$14.7M.

I weight the bull case at 40% and the bear at 60% — moved from 30/70, because six one-directional corrections have made the bull materially more respectable than five revisions of mine allowed.

7. FINAL DECISION

Decision: WAIT.

Moat Tribunal Result: Uncertain, eroding on a legislated schedule. Four MODERATE, one SEVERE, none arguing durability. Ceiling adjustment: NONE. Floor adjustment: NONE — ~94% individual retail, no backlog, no recurring revenue, one-click switching across ~50 soon-to-exist doors.

The thesis. I can value this within a factor of three — $3.13 to $9.45, central $5.84 — and the price is $11.08. My previous central was too low, and this time not because two errors cancelled but because six of my seven leaned the same way. I anchored the discount rate to a coupon that already priced the tax claim and then charged the claim again in full; I read an ASC 740 reserve as a forecast when the standard only says a bookkeeping test failed; and I took maintenance capital from a half-year that still had a deconsolidated business inside it. I also owe a retraction on the finding I liked best: the 10.5% notes did not replace the 8.0% notes — those were redeemed for cash twelve days earlier, the company cut its borrowings by $184M in the same month, and once you adjust for a five-year maturity against paper months from due and for four points of risk-free rate between two issue dates, this borrower's credit spread did not widen at all. And I must say plainly what my own EBITDA correction did: at $277M this stock trades near nine times enterprise value to EBITDA, which is Curaleaf's multiple in my own dossier, so a peer-multiple construction does reach the price and I reject that frame by argument rather than by pretending it is absent.

What keeps this a WAIT is the only thing that has not moved in six revisions: the whole range rests on a medical-only selling-expense line no human being has ever printed — and the Q3 10-Q is mechanically the first document that will.

The back-solve. Justifying $11.08 requires $286M of owner earnings at r = 14% (1.97x my central $145M), falling to about 1.2x at the bull corner. Corner-dependent, as it must be — narrower than the 1.4x–2.3x I published last time, because the corrections moved the price closer to reach.

Why is it priced here? Complexity, novelty, and a marginal buyer pricing EV/EBITDA and takeout optionality on the first plant-touching NYSE listing. He is not wrong about the multiple; he is wrong about the metric.

Moat Half-Life: 3–5 years. The moat is the licence count, and the statute that created the scarcity contains the formula that dissolves it.

On Burry's 1% short: I decline, and it is a judgment, not a calculation. No borrow cost, no short interest, no days-to-cover in the record. And after six revisions producing six different values for one company, I should not be shorting the seventh.

On the TOO UNCERTAIN tripwires — and this is closer than any previous draft admitted. At 3.02x, tripwire #4 is at the line rather than comfortably inside it, and Revision 5's claim that "2.45x is well inside a factor of three" is withdrawn along with the band that produced it. I argue out on one narrow ground: this is non-disclosure with a mechanical resolution date, not unknowability. The deconsolidation closed 3 June and Harvest is equity-method, so the Q3 income statement is the medical-only income statement by construction, roughly ten weeks out. TOO UNCERTAIN means don't buy, don't short, don't even watch — here the correct instruction is precisely to watch, on a known date, for three named line items. And the decision does not require resolving the range: every corrected central across six revisions — $4.45, $5.26, $5.62, $5.11, $5.84 — is under 55% of the price. A reader who files this in the Too Hard pile is applying the same discipline to the same facts and reaching a defensible answer.

Trigger Price: $3.15–$5.85

Position Size: 0% — no long, no short. A 2% position becomes defensible when medical-only SG&A is disclosed for the first time in the Q3 10-Q, two further quarters confirm the $222M base and 63% margin against the new licence cohort, and the IRS position is capped materially below the company's full reserve.

And then I stop. Not because six revisions feels like enough, but because what a seventh would argue about is no longer arithmetic — the grid, both corners, the width table, the p-sensitivity and the stress test all reproduce — but what a bond price means. That is the sort of thing an honest man names rather than settles wrongly for a seventh time.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY (distilled from synthesis above)

Decision: WAIT Trigger: Buy anywhere ≤ $5.85 (absurdly cheap floor $3.15 | fair value limit $5.85). Central estimate $5.84; full range across all seven inputs $3.13–$9.45 Conviction: Moderate. Downgraded from High: my own conceded frame — EV/EBITDA at the peer set — reaches the price on my own restated metric ($11.75 at Curaleaf's 9.6x), and the bull corner with the tax claim erased is $10.89 against a price of $11.08. Council Vote: 1 BUY, 3 WAIT, 7 PASS, 1 SELL Thesis in One Sentence: Corrected for a discount rate that double-charged the IRS claim, an ASC 740 reserve I misread as a forecast, and maintenance capital taken on a consolidated basis, Trulieve is worth $3.13–$9.45 with a central of $5.84 against a price of $11.08 — and the retraction I owe is that the 10.5% notes did not reprice this borrower 250bp wider, because they never replaced the 8.0% notes at all.

Load-Bearing Factors (ranked)

  1. The (discount rate, settlement fraction, reinvestment return) triple. Justifying $11.08 needs $286M of owner earnings at r = 14% against a central $145M — 1.97x, falling to ~1.2x at the bull corner. No filing settles these; r is now anchored to an observed bond price with the tax-claim overlap stripped, which is the only improvement available.
  2. Medical-only SG&A, never printed by anyone — the reason this is WAIT. My $332M is 37.4%, between two GAAP quarters (36.55%, 37.56%); management's own adjusted figure is 32%, and roughly $31M of the gap is genuinely restructuring-related. Q3'26 consolidated is medical-only by construction.
  3. Florida licence count (28 → ~50 MMTCs) — the moat is licence scarcity and §381.986 contains the dissolution formula. FCF turns negative at a ~35% revenue decline (~23% under a cash instalment plan) — a scenario, not the base case: like-for-like revenue is −2.2%, medical-only +4% sequential.

Primary Disagreement

Lynch (BUY, 55%) says the market reads consolidated revenue −10.3% and misses medical-only +4%, with a peer re-rate worth 30–100%. Buffett (PASS, 78%) says the core proxy P/E is ~24.9x against the sector cost of equity — though his "$283–392M bull case" is $2,180M × 13%/18%, the price back-solved rather than a forecast. The disagreement is about entity and about a tax provision: Lynch's EBITDA is the consolidated Q1 figure containing Harvest, and §280E is a tax while EBITDA is pre-tax, so relief cannot lift it. He was nonetheless the only expert to catch that the −10.3% headline is a deconsolidation artefact.

Evidence That Would Resolve This

  • The Q3'26 10-Q, ~10 weeks out — mechanically the first medical-only income statement, settling SG&A against management's 32% adjusted claim and confirming EBITDA near $277M.
  • A filed IRS settlement or Tax Court ruling with a stated dollar cap — worth $2.81/share between p = 100% and p = 0.
  • Florida DOH actually issuing the 22 new MMTC licences, and Trulieve's flower share and discounting behaviour three quarters after those doors open.

---SUMMARY--- VERDICT: BUY CONFIDENCE: 55 KEY METRIC: Medical-only Q2'26 revenue $222M (+4% sequential, 63% gross margin, traffic +6%, units +8%) [SEARCH] — the 280E-relieved, 94%-retail core the market is ignoring while it prices the consolidated headline "revenue −10.3% YoY." TRIGGER PRICE: Starter size at or below current ($11.32 [CALC] / $11.08 [MEDIA]); add meaningfully below $8.50 @ 15% required return (mid-point of the dossier's 13–18% local cost of equity) if the IRS uncertain-tax-position dispute either settles for materially less than $598–696M [SEC] or is resolved with a clear cash-payment cap. POSITION SIZE: 2% starter position — this is a "prove it for two more quarters" stake, not a full-conviction one, given the single clean quarter of medical-only data and the unresolved tax liability. KEY RISK: The $598–696M uncertain tax position [SEC] — more than a quarter of the $2.13B market cap — where the IRS has publicly stated Trulieve's $113M refund claims "are not valid" [SEARCH], against only $325.4M of cash [SEARCH]. BULL CASE: Schedule III 280E relief (effective April 22, 2026) plus the June 2026 NYSE uplist convert a shunned, Schedule-I-adjacent OTC stock into the first plant-touching NYSE name with the #1 share position in the largest US medical market — a re-rating catalyst the market hasn't priced because it's reading the consolidated revenue decline, not the medical-only growth underneath it. MOAT FLAG: MODERATE ---END SUMMARY---

TRLV — Peter Lynch, The Contrarian Optimist

Context up front: which entity am I pricing?

I am pricing the go-forward, post-restructuring, medical-only entity — Q1 2026 ($287M revenue, first positive net income to common in the trailing record) and Q2 2026 ($222M medical-only revenue, 63% gross margin) — not the pre-June-3 consolidated group whose FINANCIAL PHYSICS table (TTM owner earnings $0.05B, owner yield 2.5% [CALC]) reflects a business that no longer exists in that form. Where I cite the consolidated numbers it's to show what the market is reacting to, not what I think the company is worth today.

1. THE TEN-BAGGER TEST

This is not a fast-grower and I won't pretend it is. It's a turnaround dressed as a structural unlock: a company that spent its entire public life as an OTC/CSE-listed Schedule I outcast — no NYSE access, restricted banking, no bankruptcy protection — and in six weeks (April 22 to June 10, 2026) had its core medical business rescheduled to Schedule III, got 280E tax relief, and became the first plant-touching cannabis company on the NYSE [SEARCH per "Trulieve Announces Uplist to NYSE"].

What growth is the market pricing? At $11.32 and a $2.13B market cap [CALC/MEDIA], against Q1's adjusted EBITDA of $100M on $287M revenue (35% margin, largely pre-280E-relief) [SEARCH], the market is pricing something close to no growth and no multiple expansion — the stock trades on the "revenue −10.3% YoY" consolidated headline, not on the medical-only growth trend (traffic +6%, units +8%, revenue +4% sequentially) [SEARCH].

What's realistically achievable: if medical-only EBITDA sustains near a $350–400M annualized run rate as the 280E benefit flows through a full year (Q2 only captured ~10 weeks of it) and Florida/Georgia patient growth continues at the pace management describes as "accelerated recently" [SEARCH], a re-rate from today's ~5.5x EV/EBITDA (EV ≈ $2.13B mkt cap + ~$405M gross debt − $325.4M cash ≈ $2.21B, against a $400M run-rate) toward the peer range of 7.3x–9.6x (Green Thumb, Curaleaf, per the Roundhill table [MEDIA]) implies an EV of roughly $2.9–3.8B — 35–70% upside on the multiple alone, before compounding two to three years of patient growth on top. That's a realistic double over 2–3 years in the bull case, not a ten-bagger. I'd rather be honest about that than oversell it — the whole point of this exercise is to find what the bears are missing, not to manufacture a number.

2. THE "EVERYONE HATES IT" SIGNAL

Every bearish narrative in this dossier has a specific counter.

"Revenue fell 10.3% YoY in Q2, 7.0% in H1, FY2025 was flat, Q4 was −3% YoY." Counter: this conflates the deconsolidation with organic performance. Q2's $271.0M consolidated figure includes only ~5 weeks of Harvest before it left the group on June 3 [SEARCH]. The retained medical-only business — the thing you actually own going forward — grew revenue 4% sequentially with traffic +6% and units +8% [SEARCH]. The "shrinking company" story is an artifact of comparing a pre-restructuring quarter to a post-restructuring one.

"$403.3M (or $688M, per a conflicting 8-K headline) loss on deconsolidation." Counter: this is a non-cash accounting loss from marking the retained Harvest interest to fair value on exit, not a cash loss or an indicator of operating deterioration. Strip it out and Q2 adjusted net income was positive $20M ($0.11/share) [SEARCH] — the headline GAAP loss of $406M / −$2.10 per share is almost entirely this one line item.

"$598–696M uncertain tax position, IRS says the refund claims aren't valid." Counter (partial — this is the one bear case I take most seriously, see Key Risk): most of this liability ($655.6M of $696.4M) is Trulieve's own aggressive position that 280E didn't preclude ordinary deductions in prior years — it is a reserve against an optimistic claim TRLV made, not new money the IRS is billing on top of a clean balance sheet. Worst case, it's written off against past refund claims already booked, not a fresh $600M cash bill. That said, I can't prove the downside is capped at zero cash outflow — the record doesn't settle it either way.

"CEO Kim Rivers sold $14.66M of stock, zero purchases." Counter: this executes under an Automatic Securities Disposition Plan announced March 17, 2026 — roughly eleven weeks before the restructuring was announced — with sales beginning June 17 and continuing until 2.5M shares are sold [SEARCH]. That's a pre-scheduled 10b5-1-style program, not opportunistic selling timed to bad news she alone knew. It's a mildly negative signal (zero purchases means no vote of confidence either), not the "insider knows it's broken" signal a naive read would suggest.

"Harvest is a 90%-economic, 0%-control minority stake — management gave away the adult-use business." Counter: that structure is why TRLV could list on the NYSE at all — Schedule I adult-use assets can't sit in a listed entity's consolidated financials, full stop. Deconsolidating into a controlled-by-someone-else vehicle while retaining 90% of the economics is the mechanism that solved a problem no other MSO has solved. It's a structural unlock, not a giveaway — assuming Harvest performs, which it hasn't yet (see Section 5 caveat).

"Two failed Florida adult-use ballot campaigns, $177M+ spent for nothing." Counter: this is sunk cost and backward-looking. It doesn't touch the current, profitable, dominant medical footprint. What it should do is stop future campaign spending — a real management-discipline test going forward, not a reason to discount today's medical business.

Private-market value: at a $2.21B EV against a business generating $53.1M of quarterly operating cash flow even during a disruption quarter [SEARCH], holding 22% of Florida's dispensary doors and 2x the next competitor's oil volume [SEARCH], a strategic acquirer — a bigger MSO or a consumer/pharma entrant waiting for federal clarity — would very plausibly pay a premium to today's price for the #1 position in the largest medical cannabis market in the country, with the hard part (NYSE listing, Schedule III compliance) already done. I can't put a precise number on this without better data, but the qualitative case is real.

3. THE HIDDEN GROWTH

  • Medical-only growth is real and currently invisible in the headline numbers: +4% sequential revenue, +6% traffic, +8% units [SEARCH], in markets management says are "accelerating" (Florida, Georgia, Pennsylvania).
  • Georgia's SB220 ("Putting Georgia's Patients First Act"), signed May 12, 2026, adds lupus, HIV and IBD as qualifying conditions and removes the "end-stage" requirement [SEARCH] — a market-expansion tailwind that predates any of TRLV's own execution and shows up nowhere in the trailing financials.
  • The 280E unlock itself is the biggest under-priced item. The pipeline flagged a 228% effective tax rate on the old consolidated numbers — that was largely 280E's non-deductibility of ordinary business expenses. Q1 2026 (before the April 22 rescheduling) already produced the first positive net income to common ($2M) in the trailing record on operating improvement alone [SEARCH]; Q2 then captured roughly ten weeks of 280E relief on top. A full year of 280E-relieved medical revenue hasn't been reported yet — that's not priced in, it's not even fully knowable in the data available.
  • Optionality the market assigns zero value to: the DEA hearing on broader (adult-use) rescheduling began June 29, 2026 (Event D) [SEARCH]. If it extends 280E to adult-use activity, Harvest's economics improve and TRLV's 90% stake — carried at $188.5M, about 8.8% of market cap — becomes worth meaningfully more. The market is pricing Harvest near its carrying cost with no credit for this catalyst, and I'm not crediting much either (see Section 5 caveat that Harvest already posted a $3.3M equity-method loss) — but it's a real, uncapped call option that costs nothing extra to hold.

4. THE FCF REALITY CHECK

Hurdle discipline: the dossier's LOCAL COST OF EQUITY for this name is 13–18% — a sector premium is warranted given Schedule I history for adult-use, restricted banking, and a still-unresolved federal tax dispute. I am measuring every yield below against that band, not the US Treasury.

  • Trailing consolidated owner yield: 2.5% [CALC] — nowhere close to clearing a 13–18% hurdle. On this basis alone the stock is a clear pass.
  • Forward, post-restructuring basis: H1'26 FCF was $74M (Q1 $42M + Q2 $32M) [SEARCH]. Annualized crudely (×2, ignoring seasonality and the fact Q2 only had a partial 280E benefit), that's ~$148M FCF against a $2.13B market cap — a ~6.9% forward FCF yield. Still below the 13–18% hurdle, and I want to be explicit about that rather than round up to "cheap."
  • If the 280E benefit reaches a full-quarter run rate and patient growth continues, FCF could plausibly reach $150–180M/year — a 7–8.5% yield at today's price. That still doesn't clear even the low end of the 13–18% hurdle. The honest conclusion: this is not a stock you buy for current cash-flow yield. It clears the hurdle only if you also get the multiple re-rate described in Section 1, or meaningful growth beyond the current run rate.
  • No carry: zero dividend, and the dossier is explicit that "nothing pays you to wait." That raises the bar for holding through the tax-dispute uncertainty — there's no coupon compensating you while it resolves.
  • "Would I buy the whole company?" At $2.13B for the #1 medical operator in the country's largest medical market, with $325.4M of cash against ~$32M of debt due within 24 months (the $200M private placement doesn't mature until 2030) [SEC], the balance sheet gives me room to wait. But on cash flows alone, today, at a 13–18% hurdle — no, the yield doesn't make me happy yet. The bull case has to be earned through growth and re-rating, not collected as a coupon.

5. THE MANAGEMENT DECODER

  • The $177M+ spent on two failed Florida ballot campaigns looks like capital destruction in hindsight — both failed, the second on a signature-gathering technicality amid lawsuits [SEARCH]. I won't rescue this with pure hindsight bias: it was a legitimate asymmetric bet on the single largest addressable-market expansion available to the company, and it lost. The real test is whether management stops funding future campaigns now that the odds (60% supermajority requirement) haven't improved.
  • The Harvest deconsolidation reads on its face like giving away the adult-use crown jewels for a nine-figure GAAP loss. I read it instead as the specific mechanism that got a Schedule I-adjacent business onto the NYSE — something no cannabis MSO had achieved before. That is sophisticated, not reckless, capital allocation if Harvest's value holds up. It's a fair critique that Harvest already posted a $3.3M equity-method loss in its first stub period [SEC] and Trulieve has zero operational control to fix that — I flag this as a real weakness in the "smart move" read, not a reason to dismiss it.
  • CEO stock sales under a pre-announced 10b5-1 plan: neutral-to-mildly-cautious, not a red flag. Scheduled, non-discretionary selling is categorically different from a CEO dumping stock into good news. Zero purchases means I get no positive signal either — I'm not crediting this as bullish, just declining to treat it as the smoking gun a headline-only read would suggest.

6. THE "WHAT IF THEY'RE RIGHT?" SCENARIO

If (a) 280E relief flows through a full four quarters at something like Q2's run rate, (b) Florida (938,980 registered patients and growing) and Georgia (newly expanded eligibility) patient growth continues at the mid-to-high-single-digit sequential pace seen in Q2, (c) the IRS dispute resolves as a reserve write-down rather than a fresh cash bill, and (d) the broader-rescheduling hearing eventually extends 280E to Harvest —

Then: medical-only annualized EBITDA plausibly reaches $350–450M within two to three years, and a re-rate toward the peer range (7.3x–9.6x EV/EBITDA, treating the conflicting Motley Fool multiples as unresolved per the dossier) implies an EV of roughly $2.9–4.5B against today's ~$2.21B — 30–100% upside, concentrated in the multiple re-rate rather than in current cash yield. That is a realistic double-to-triple over 2–3 years if the bulls are right, timed to two catalysts with knowable dates: the next 2–4 quarters of medical-only reporting (proving the growth trend is durable) and the DEA hearing outcome on broader rescheduling (Event D, no fixed timeline given).

Bottom line

This is the textbook Lynch setup — a stock the market hates for a headline that isn't the real story, sitting on a structural catalyst (Schedule III + NYSE listing) most generalist investors haven't had time to price. But I'm not going to pretend the FCF yield clears the 13–18% hurdle today, because it doesn't — 6.9% forward, optimistically 7–8.5%, is a "wait for the re-rate" number, not a "back up the truck" number. And the largest single risk — a $598–696M uncertain tax position against $325.4M of cash — is exactly the kind of binary, government-decision risk that can wipe out a small position's thesis overnight. Small starter size, add on confirmation, size up only if the tax dispute resolves and a second clean medical-only quarter confirms the growth trend is durable rather than a one-quarter blip.

1. The Flywheel Check

Draw the loop

Trulieve has the physical ingredients of a flywheel in Florida:

More patient traffic → greater cultivation and store utilization → lower unit costs → broader availability and sharper pricing → higher retention → more traffic

The evidence is tangible:

  • 170 Florida dispensaries represent 22% of retail doors [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 investor deck].
  • Trulieve holds 35% flower share and 26% oil share [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 investor deck].
  • Stores sell 56% more flower and 20% more oil than the market average [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 investor deck].
  • The company’s 750,000-square-foot automated cultivation facility provides a plausible fixed-cost advantage [SEC].
  • Medical-market retention is 78%, versus 69% across the broader retail platform [SEARCH, TRLV 8-K].
  • Medical-only traffic grew 6%, units grew 8%, and revenue grew 4% sequentially in Q2 [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 FY2026 earnings-call transcript].

That is real scale. It is not yet proof of favorable flywheel physics, however. A healthy flywheel must translate volume leadership into superior cash economics or sustainably lower customer prices.

Velocity: currently negative

The consolidated numbers show negative operating leverage:

Metric Change
Q2 revenue −10.3% YoY [CALC from SEARCH]
Q2 gross profit −11.3% YoY [CALC from SEARCH]
Q2 SG&A +0.7% YoY [CALC from SEARCH]
H1 revenue −7.0% YoY [CALC from SEARCH]
H1 operating cash flow −20.7% YoY [CALC from SEARCH]

In Q2, revenue fell from $302.1M to $271.0M while SG&A increased from $101.1M to $101.8M [SEARCH, “Trulieve Reports Second Quarter 2026 Results”]. That is the opposite of operating leverage.

The medical-only data are better: $222M revenue, 63% gross margin and 4% sequential growth [SEARCH, Q2 earnings-call transcript]. But one quarter of gross-margin data cannot prove that the retained entity has operating leverage.

Flywheel or doom loop?

The most defensible conclusion is:

  • Store and production flywheel: credible.
  • Revenue flywheel: emerging in medical markets but not demonstrated over multiple periods.
  • Corporate-cost flywheel: presently absent.
  • Pricing flywheel: threatened by industry price compression and steep customer-acquisition discounts, including 60%/50%/40% off the first three visits [SEARCH, “Florida 4/20 Deals 2026”].

Additional Florida MMTC licenses could dilute Trulieve’s density advantage, while falling cannabis prices may transfer production savings to consumers rather than shareholders. The flywheel has torque, but it is not generating economic velocity yet.

2. The Shutdown Test

The identified incinerator: Harvest

Harvest is the obvious investment-phase operation:

  • Trulieve retains 90% of its economics but has zero operational control [SEC/CALC].
  • The retained interest is carried at $188.463M, approximately 8.8% of market capitalization [SEC/CALC].
  • Harvest produced a $3.3M equity-method loss in H1 2026 [SEC, Q2 10-Q].
  • Its separation generated a reported $403.3M deconsolidation loss [SEARCH, Q2 results], although an 8-K summary reports $688M; that conflict remains unresolved.
  • The investment cannot be freely exited and is not a free option.

The $403.3M charge is principally an accounting destruction of value, not evidence of equivalent current cash burn. The recurring economic drag visible in the dossier is the $3.3M H1 equity loss plus the opportunity cost of $188.5M of trapped capital.

Cost-dumping audit

There is no evidence that Trulieve has made the retained core look artificially profitable by placing shared costs in Harvest.

In fact, the opposite warning appears:

  • Medical-only management reporting emphasizes a flattering 63% gross margin [SEARCH, Q2 transcript].
  • Consolidated SG&A remained essentially flat at $101.8M despite the revenue decline and partial deconsolidation [SEARCH, Q2 results].
  • No segment-level SG&A, corporate-infrastructure or engineering allocation is provided.

Charging all Q2 consolidated SG&A and D&A to the medical core gives a conservative operating-profit bridge:

  • Medical gross profit: $140.0M [SEARCH, Q2 transcript]
  • Less consolidated SG&A: $101.8M [SEARCH, Q2 results]
  • Less consolidated D&A: $25.7M [SEARCH, Q2 results]
  • Conservative core operating-profit proxy: $12.5M quarterly [CALC]

This is deliberately harsh because some costs may relate to Harvest. But until allocations are disclosed, the 63% medical gross margin should not be mistaken for a 63% economic margin.

“True core profit”

Q2 adjusted net income was $20M, or $0.11 per share [SEARCH, “Trulieve Reports Second Quarter 2026 Results”]. Mechanically annualized, that is:

  • Adjusted profit: $80M
  • Adjusted EPS: $0.44
  • Adjusted core P/E at $11.32: 25.7x [CALC]

If the disclosed H1 Harvest equity loss is added back and annualized, the mechanical shutdown result is:

  • Q2 adjusted profit plus average quarterly Harvest loss: $20M + $1.65M
  • Annualized “true core” profit: approximately $86.6M
  • Core EPS: approximately $0.45
  • Core P/E: approximately 25.2x at $11.32 [CALC]

This is only a proxy: the dossier does not establish whether every Harvest-related item is included in adjusted income or provide a clean post-280E medical tax rate.

The resulting core earnings yield is approximately 4.0%. That is expensive against the 18% local cost-of-equity hurdle I use—the upper end of the dossier’s 13–18% range because of the IRS dispute, one-quarter medical history and lack of control over Harvest.

The reported headline P/E of −4.51 is economically meaningless because GAAP earnings are negative [MEDIA, stock-snapshot source]. Shutting Harvest does not reveal a cheap core. It reveals a profitable core trading at roughly six times the reciprocal of my required earnings yield.

3. Maintenance Versus Expansion

The consolidated decline and the medical-only improvement resemble two different businesses.

Evidence for healthy core funding expansion

  • Medical-only revenue grew 4% sequentially [SEARCH, Q2 transcript].
  • Medical traffic increased 6% and units increased 8% [SEARCH, Q2 transcript].
  • Medical gross margin was 63%, above the 60% consolidated margin [SEARCH, Q2 transcript and Q2 results].
  • Event A removed 280E prospectively from medical activity beginning April 22, 2026, which should improve retained-core after-tax economics.
  • Only $10M of the increased capex guidance is specifically earmarked for emerging Georgia and Texas medical markets [MEDIA, Finsee/X Q2 summary].

Evidence the core itself may be deteriorating

  • FY2025 consolidated revenue was flat; Q4 fell 3% YoY [SEARCH, Q4 FY2025 earnings summary].
  • H1 2026 revenue fell 7% and operating cash flow fell from $137.2M to $108.8M [SEARCH, Q2 results].
  • Q2 SG&A did not fall with revenue.
  • Industry price compression and discounting threaten the conversion of volume leadership into profit.
  • Inventory reached 188 days in 2025, versus 144 days in 2023 [SEC].

FY2026 capex guidance of up to $95M is close to Q2 annualized D&A of approximately $103M [MEDIA/SEARCH/CALC]. That makes current spending look predominantly like maintenance and replacement, not a large Day 1 expansion program.

Classification: this is a potentially healthy medical core funding a limited expansion, but it has not yet proved that thesis. Consolidated cash deterioration and flat SG&A prevent me from declaring that the weakness resides only in Harvest.

4. Cash Flow Truth

Historical consolidated cash flow improved materially before the structural break:

Period FCF Owner earnings
2022 −$170M −$180M
2023 $150M $20M
2024 $120M $80M
2025 $210M $80M

[CALC, pre-restructuring consolidated basis]

Those figures belong to the pre-June-3 business and should not be applied directly to today’s medical-only entity.

The cleaner post-restructuring indicators are:

  • H1 FCF: $74M [SEARCH, Q2 results].
  • Q2 FCF: $32M [SEARCH, Q2 results].
  • Q2 annualized FCF: $128M [CALC].
  • Q2 annualized FCF per share: approximately $0.665, using 192.57M shares [CALC from SEARCH].
  • FCF yield at $11.32: approximately 5.9% [CALC].
  • Equivalent P/FCF: approximately 17.0x [CALC].

That 5.9% FCF yield is inadequate against my 18% local cost-of-equity hurdle. Even the more generous annualized H1 result—$148M, or roughly a 6.8% yield—does not clear the dossier’s lowest 13% hurdle [CALC].

At an 18% required FCF yield, Q2 annualized FCF supports approximately:

[ \$0.665 \div 18\% = \$3.69\text{ per share} ]

Hence the $3.70 trigger price. It provides no separate credit for a favorable IRS resolution or Harvest upside; both are too uncertain to capitalize at face value.

The cash balance of $325.4M comfortably covers approximately $32M due within 24 months [SEARCH/SEC]. But the $598M uncertain tax-position liability is nearly twice the cash balance and approximately 4.7 times annualized Q2 FCF [SEARCH/CALC]. Event C, not store operations, therefore dominates equity value.

There is no dividend, so carry is 0.0% [CALC]. Shares increased from 181M to 192M between 2021 and 2025, or 1.6% annual dilution [CALC]. Investors receive neither cash carry nor shrinking share count while awaiting regulatory resolution.

Verdict on Flywheel Health

Trulieve owns the strongest pieces of a cannabis flywheel—Florida density, above-market store productivity, automated cultivation, high medical retention and improving medical traffic—but the financial flywheel has not engaged: consolidated revenue and cash flow are declining, SG&A is not flexing, and the current 5.9% annualized FCF yield falls far short of the 18% local cost-of-equity hurdle appropriate to its tax and regulatory risks. Event A may make the retained medical core substantially better, but Event C’s $598M liability and Event B’s trapped $188.5M Harvest stake absorb that promise. This is not yet a doom loop, but at $11.32 investors are paying for a healthy flywheel before the cash-flow physics have proved it.
