The “No” Test — Focus

The restructuring has finally given Trulieve a comprehensible product architecture:

  • One core customer: the medical cannabis patient.
  • Four retained markets: Florida, Georgia, Pennsylvania and West Virginia [SEC].
  • Approximately 94% retail revenue [SEARCH per “Trulieve Reports Second Quarter 2026 Results”].
  • In Florida and Georgia, Trulieve cultivates, processes, manufactures and retails all its cannabis products [SEC].

That is much cleaner than the pre-restructuring collection of 233 dispensaries across nine states [SEC]. Event B—the June 2026 deconsolidation—was strategically sound because it separated Schedule I adult-use operations from the Schedule III medical business.

But the cleanup is incomplete. Trulieve retains 90% of Harvest’s economics while possessing zero operational control. The $188.5M carrying value represents roughly 8.8% of market capitalization [SEC/CALC], and Harvest already produced a $3.3M H1 equity loss [SEC per Q2 10-Q]. That is the “open box” problem hiding inside the new simplicity: meaningful capital committed to a product line management cannot control.

Focus verdict: Much improved, but not pure.

The Love Metric — Loved, Not Yet Beloved

The best evidence is 78% retention in medical-only markets, compared with 69% across the broader retail platform [SEARCH per TRLV 8-K]. Because there are no contracts or backlog and almost all customers are individuals, that retention is not enterprise lock-in.

The operating evidence reinforces genuine preference:

  • Medical-only traffic increased 6% and units increased 8% [SEARCH per Trulieve Q2 FY2026 earnings-call transcript].
  • Medical-only revenue reached $222M, up 4% sequentially [SEARCH per the same transcript].
  • Florida stores sold 56% more flower and 20% more oil per location than the market average [SEARCH per TRLV 8-K/Q2 investor deck].
  • Trulieve holds 35% of Florida flower sales and 26% of oil sales while operating 22% of retail doors [SEARCH per TRLV 8-K/Q2 investor deck].

That is strong product-market fit. Customers demonstrably choose Trulieve more often than its store footprint alone would predict.

Still, this is not proof of love at Apple intensity. There is no NPS, disclosed cohort churn, basket size or patient spending data. The 60%/50%/40% discounts offered on customers’ first three visits also make it difficult to separate brand pull from promotional acquisition [SEARCH per “Florida 4/20 Deals 2026”].

Love verdict: Customers appear satisfied and voluntarily retained, but “beloved” remains unproven.

The Integration — Landlord With a Leased-Out Wing

Trulieve owns the whole medical widget in its strongest states. Florida and Georgia operations span cultivation through retail, including a 750,000-square-foot automated Florida cultivation facility [SEC]. That vertical integration supports product consistency, availability and cost control.

Its productivity suggests the integration works: Trulieve sold 680,000 ounces and 1.5 billion milligrams of oil across the platform—more than twice the next-highest competitor [SEARCH per TRLV 8-K/Q2 investor deck].

But Event B made it a tenant in adult-use cannabis. Trulieve retained 90% of Harvest’s economics but surrendered operational control. This is the worst ownership combination: capital exposure without authority.

The medical moat is also politically rented. Florida may add 22 MMTC licenses, potentially increasing the total from approximately 28 to around 50 [SEARCH per “Florida Cannabis MMTC License | 2026 Application Guide” and “22 New Cannabis Licenses Coming to Florida”]. Price compression and automation accessible to competitors threaten both regulatory scarcity and production advantages.

Integration verdict: Landlord in medical; powerless investor in adult-use.

The Next Big Thing — Regulatory Beneficiary, Not Market Creator

Trulieve is not creating a new consumer category. It is executing extremely well inside a market created and constrained by government.

Event A—medical cannabis moving to Schedule III—removes §280E prospectively from April 22, 2026. That could transform after-tax cash generation, but the government created that opportunity.

Event E is more revealing. Trulieve spent more than $145M on Florida’s failed 2024 adult-use campaign and more than $32.4M on the failed 2026 effort [SEARCH per “Florida 2026 Cannabis Legalization Campaign Falls Short on Signatures Amid Lawsuits”]. More than $177M did not create the new market.

Event D, broader adult-use rescheduling, would improve Harvest economics, but it remains an unresolved political option—not product innovation. The company reports $0.00B of R&D [SEC], further suggesting operational optimization rather than invention.

Innovation verdict: Excellent execution of the existing model, not invention of the future.

Price Versus the Hurdle

The pipeline’s 2.5% owner yield at $11.32 is based on the pre-June-3 consolidated company [CALC]. It is inadequate against the dossier’s 13–18% local cost of equity, which incorporates the approximately 4% US risk-free rate, roughly 5% equity premium and a cannabis-sector premium.

H1 2026 FCF was $74M [SEARCH per “Trulieve Reports Second Quarter 2026 Results”], or $148M annualized mechanically. That period is predominantly pre-deconsolidation and is therefore not a clean medical-only run rate. Nevertheless, capitalizing $148M at the required 13–18% return produces an indicative equity price of approximately $4.25–$5.90 per share [CALC], before separately resolving the tax liability and Harvest’s value.

At the approximately $2.13B market capitalization reported at $11.08 [MEDIA per stock-snapshot source], Trulieve would need roughly $277–383M of sustainable annual medical-only FCF to deliver a 13–18% equity yield [CALC]. The dossier does not yet demonstrate that earning power.

The decisive overhang is Event C: the unresolved uncertain-tax-position liability is $598M in Q2 [SEARCH per Q2 release], after reaching $696.4M in Q1 [SEARCH per TCNNF Q1 2026 10-Q summary]. That is not a product problem management can design its way out of.

Soul Verdict

Trulieve has a real product heartbeat: high medical retention, exceptional Florida productivity and meaningful control of its medical supply chain. But it lacks demonstrated customer devotion, owns a large uncontrolled adult-use investment and depends on regulators for both its tax economics and its next market.

Soul verdict: Sugared Water.

---SUMMARY--- VERDICT: PASS CONFIDENCE: 78 KEY METRIC: Core proxy P/E is ~24.9x versus 5.6–7.7x justified by the 18–13% local cost of equity. TRIGGER PRICE: $3.30–$4.20 @ 18–13% required return, including Harvest at its full $188.5M carrying value. POSITION SIZE: ZERO KEY RISK: The $598–696M disputed tax position could overwhelm otherwise adequate liquidity while the Florida regulatory moat is opening to new licenses. BULL CASE: Schedule III tax relief converts the dominant medical franchise into $280M–$390M of annual owner earnings while Harvest retains meaningful value. MOAT FLAG: MODERATE ---END SUMMARY---

1. The Anti-Fragile Moat

The actual moat

Trulieve has a real operating advantage in Florida:

  • 170 dispensaries represent 22% of Florida retail doors, but Trulieve commands 35% of flower and 26% of oil sales [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 investor deck].
  • Flower volume per store is 56% above the market average; oil volume is 20% above average [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 investor deck].
  • Medical-market retention is 78% [SEARCH, TRLV 8-K].
  • Platform oil volume is more than twice that of the next-highest competitor [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 investor deck].
  • Its vertically integrated system includes a 750,000-square-foot automated Florida cultivation facility [SEC].

That is evidence of scale, locations, purchasing convenience, and operating execution. It is not merely management calling itself a market leader.

But this is a narrow regional moat, not an inevitable fortress.

The Chaos Test: mixed at best

There is no evidence that recessions make Trulieve stronger. Cannabis may retain demand, but consumers can trade down, seek discounts, reduce dosage, or use illicit substitutes.

More importantly, medical-only traffic rose 6% and units rose 8%, while revenue increased only 4% sequentially [SEARCH, Q2 FY2026 earnings-call sources]. On a comparable directional basis, revenue lagging unit volume suggests price or mix pressure [CALC]. That is the opposite of anti-fragility.

Competitor failures could consolidate traffic around Trulieve, but cannabis oversupply and falling prices can damage every operator simultaneously.

The $1 billion competitor test: Trulieve fails

A rival could not reproduce 170 Florida locations overnight. Licenses, cultivation, compliance, and local density create meaningful delay and expense.

Nevertheless, $1 billion would be enough to acquire licenses, fund cultivation, discount heavily, and buy customer traffic. The threat is becoming more concrete: 22 additional Florida MMTC licenses were reportedly being prepared, potentially increasing the field from roughly 28 to around 50 [SEARCH, “Florida Cannabis MMTC License | 2026 Application Guide” and “22 New Cannabis Licenses Coming to Florida”].

There are no contracts, enterprise integrations, or meaningful switching costs. The customer can visit another dispensary on the next purchase. Retention reflects convenience, product availability, and habit—not captivity.

Pricing power: absent

The strongest moat test is whether prices can rise without damaging volume. Trulieve does not pass:

  • Retail flower prices have declined across most legal markets [SEARCH, “Cannabis Pricing Trends 2026”].
  • Trulieve offers 60%, 50%, and 40% discounts on a customer’s first three visits [SEARCH, “Florida 4/20 Deals 2026”].
  • Industry operators use discounts reaching 60–75% during promotional periods [SEARCH, same].
  • Only 24.4% of industry operators are reportedly profitable [SEARCH, “Cannabis Industry Statistics 2026”].

The 63% medical-only gross margin is impressive [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 FY2026 transcript], but it demonstrates cost position and regulatory economics—not pricing power. I would expect a unilateral price increase to lose volume.

2. Strategic Evolution

Feature absorption

AI is not an existential replacement for cannabis production or retail. The more relevant technological threat is automation.

Trulieve has partially absorbed that threat through its automated Florida cultivation facility [SEC]. Yet automation is increasingly available to competitors, with cited systems reducing labor cost dramatically [SEARCH, “Cannabis Automation: A Comprehensive Guide [2026]”]. Automation therefore improves Trulieve’s economics while simultaneously commoditizing part of its production advantage.

This is competent adaptation, not a proprietary technology ecosystem.

Commoditizing the complement

There is no evidence that Trulieve is deliberately making an adjacent technology cheaper to expand demand for a protected core.

Its closest strategic effort was political: more than $177M was contributed across two failed Florida adult-use campaigns—over $145M for 2024 and more than $32.4M during 2025 [SEARCH, “Florida 2026 Cannabis Legalization Campaign Falls Short…”]. That would have expanded the addressable market, but it was expensive, unsuccessful, and dependent upon achieving a 60% supermajority.

Possible restrictions on intoxicating hemp could reduce competition, but that is government action, not an ecosystem Trulieve controls.

3. Maintenance Versus Expansion

The structural break prevents casual comparison.

June’s restructuring deconsolidated Harvest, leaving Trulieve with 90% of its economics but no operational control. The retained investment is carried at $188.5M, and Harvest already contributed a $3.3M H1 equity loss [SEC]. The historical financials are consolidated; the go-forward company is medical-only.

The evidence for a healthy core is:

  • Medical-only revenue was $222M and increased 4% sequentially [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 FY2026 transcript].
  • Medical gross margin was 63%, versus 60% consolidated [SEARCH, same].
  • Medical traffic increased 6% and units increased 8% [SEARCH, earnings-call transcript].
  • Event A removed 280E prospectively from medical operations effective April 22, 2026 [SEARCH, DEA/DOJ rescheduling sources].

The contrary evidence is:

  • Q2 consolidated revenue declined 10.3% year over year and H1 revenue declined 7.0% [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 2026 results].
  • H1 operating cash flow fell from $137.2M to $108.8M [SEARCH, same].
  • FY2025 consolidated revenue was flat, with Q4 down 3% year over year [SEARCH, Q4 FY2025 call summary].
  • FY2026 capex guidance is $95M, close to annualized Q2 D&A of approximately $103M [MEDIA/SEARCH/CALC]. Only an incremental $10M was identified for emerging-market expansion [MEDIA, Finsee/X call summary].

My interpretation: this is not yet proven to be a healthy core funding a large expansion. Expansion spending is too small to explain the operating-cash-flow deterioration. The medical core looks healthy at the gross-profit and volume levels, but its post-280E net earnings have not been disclosed cleanly. Until that record exists, I underwrite it as an unproven core rather than granting the favorable “investment phase” explanation.

Core P/E versus headline P/E

The reported headline P/E of −4.51 is economically meaningless because Q2 contained approximately $407M of restructuring and Harvest-related losses [MEDIA, stock-snapshot source; SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 results].

A more useful—but still provisional—calculation is:

  • Q1 adjusted net income: $20M [SEARCH, Trulieve Q1 2026 results].
  • Q2 adjusted net income: $20M [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 2026 results].
  • Annualized adjusted earnings proxy: $80M [CALC].
  • Equity value at $11.32 and 192.57M shares: approximately $2.18B [CALC].
  • Normalized headline P/E: approximately 27.3x, or a 3.7% earnings yield [CALC].
  • After crediting Harvest’s full $188.5M carrying value, implied value paid for the core is approximately $1.99B, producing a core proxy P/E of 24.9x and a 4.0% yield [CALC].

A 4.0% core earnings yield is inadequate against the dossier’s 13–18% local cost of equity. That hurdle includes the US base rate, ordinary equity risk, and the cannabis sector’s regulatory and financing premium. A business carrying these risks should trade around 5.6–7.7 times sustainable earnings, not roughly 25 times.

The calculation is generous because the $80M run rate still contains a partially blended quarter, while Harvest receives full carrying value despite zero control and no freely available exit.

4. The Capital Allocation Machine

The Cannibal: no

Shares increased from 181M in 2021 to 192M in 2025, total dilution of 6.5%, or approximately 1.6% annually [CALC]. No buyback data was retrieved, so repurchases cannot be credited.

SBC was $6.9M in Q2, approximately 13% of operating cash flow [SEC/CALC]. Trulieve is feeding management and employees shares rather than reliably shrinking the denominator.

ROIC: fails the franchise standard

Consolidated ROIC was:

  • 1.6% in 2022
  • −8.0% in 2023
  • 4.5% in 2024
  • 8.3% in 2025
  • −20.9% TTM [CALC, pre-restructuring consolidated basis]

It has never approached the required 15% franchise threshold in the disclosed period. The TTM figure is distorted by restructuring, but the cleaner 2025 result still fails.

Capital allocation has also produced:

  • More than $177M spent supporting two failed Florida legalization campaigns [SEARCH].
  • A $188.5M non-controlled Harvest investment already producing equity losses [SEC].
  • New private placement issuance, increasing its net carrying value to $195.8M [SEC].
  • A disputed tax position of $598–696M, with interest accrual reaching $15.2M in Q1 [SEARCH, Q1/Q2 filings and releases].

Cash of $325.4M and only about $32M of debt due within 24 months provide near-term liquidity [SEARCH/SEC]. They do not neutralize a tax exposure roughly twice the cash balance.

5. The Yellow Pages Test

Trulieve is not a pure Yellow Pages analogue. Its moat is not primarily information scarcity. Physical cultivation, regulated licenses, compliance infrastructure, and location density are real assets.

The decay pattern is nevertheless recognizable:

  • Volume is rising faster than revenue in the medical segment [SEARCH/CALC].
  • Industry pricing is compressing [SEARCH].
  • Customer switching costs are low.
  • New license issuance can dilute scarcity.
  • Automation allows competitors to reproduce production efficiencies.
  • The company’s most important protection comes from regulation that government can rewrite—as Events A and B demonstrated in a matter of weeks.

The durable portion is scale and convenient physical distribution. The temporary portion is restricted licensing and state-level supply control. The consumer brand appears useful, but deep discounting indicates it does not support luxury-like pricing.

This is therefore a moat whose volume advantage may survive while its economics erode.

6. Regulatory Regime Being Priced

My valuation assumes:

  • Event A remains enacted and medical 280E relief applies prospectively.
  • Event B remains in place, with Harvest deconsolidated.
  • Event C produces no retroactive refund windfall; the disputed liability remains a genuine claim.
  • Event D, broader adult-use rescheduling, receives no value.
  • Event E, Florida adult-use legalization, receives no value after two failed campaigns.
  • Event F, hemp restrictions, receives no valuation premium.

The bull case needs Event A to lift medical-only annual earnings to approximately $283–392M at the current $11.32 price to clear the 13–18% required return [CALC]. That is 3.5–4.9 times the provisional $80M adjusted earnings run rate. One quarter of medical-only disclosure does not support that leap.

7. Valuation and Cigar-Butt Price

The pipeline’s $12.34 standard-FCF value appears to imply approximately a 10% discount rate, while its $2.62 owner-earnings value implies roughly 12%, assuming the stated 2% perpetual growth and approximately 192M shares [CALC]. Both are below the mandatory 13–18% local cost of equity, and both use pre-restructuring consolidated cash flows. They are unsuitable as current fair-value anchors.

Using the provisional $80M annual earnings:

  • At an 18% hurdle, earnings are worth approximately $444M.
  • At a 13% hurdle, they are worth approximately $615M.
  • Adding Harvest at its entire $188.5M carrying value produces equity value of approximately $633–804M, or $3.29–$4.17 per share [CALC].

That is the cigar-butt range, not a wonderful-company valuation. With no dividend, the carry is 0%, which does not clear a 13–18% required return. Nothing pays shareholders to wait.

Definitive Verdict

Trulieve possesses a narrow but meaningful Florida scale and regulatory moat. It does not possess strong pricing power, contractual lock-in, consistently superior ROIC, or an ecosystem that a well-funded competitor cannot challenge.

This is currently a mediocre-to-improving company at an expensive price, complicated by an unresolved government liability. It is not an inevitable fortress, and the current price does not provide cigar-butt protection.

Moat quality: narrow, operationally real, but vulnerable to license expansion, price compression, and regulatory change. Verdict: PASS with a zero position.

---SUMMARY--- VERDICT: PASS CONFIDENCE: 78 KEY METRIC: FY2025 inventory stood at 188 DIO, up from 144 days in FY2023 while revenue subsequently flattened [SEC] TRIGGER PRICE: $4.40-$5.10 @ 15% required return, contingent on normalized post-restructuring FCF of $128-$148M and containment of the tax liability POSITION SIZE: ZERO KEY RISK: The $598M uncertain tax-position liability exceeds cash and can overwhelm otherwise sound medical-market operations [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 2026 release] BULL CASE: The retained medical business sustains its 63% gross margin while converting superior store productivity and Schedule III tax relief into normalized FCF above $300M MOAT FLAG: MODERATE ---END SUMMARY---

Operating verdict

Trulieve has a capable production-and-retail machine, but it is not yet running cleanly enough to justify the current valuation. Vertical integration, strong medical-market margins, and exceptional Florida throughput are offset by slowing inventory velocity, weak overhead leverage, falling consolidated revenue, and an unresolved tax liability larger than the cash balance.

The June 2026 restructuring makes historical comparisons hazardous:

  • FY2022–FY2025 figures describe the pre-restructuring consolidated group.
  • Q2 2026 includes two months of Harvest and one month without it.
  • The only clean go-forward operating snapshot is medical-only revenue of $222M and gross profit of $140M, a 63% margin [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 FY2026 earnings-call transcript].

That is not enough history to certify the new machine.

1. Inventory velocity: deteriorating

Period Inventory Revenue DIO Implied turns
FY2023 $210M [SEC] $1.129B [SEC] 144 days [SEC] 2.53x [CALC]
FY2024 $230M [SEC] $1.186B [SEC] 179 days [SEC] 2.04x [CALC]
FY2025 $240M [SEC] $1.181B [SEC] 188 days [SEC] 1.94x [CALC]

The machine peaked operationally in FY2023 and then slowed. From FY2024 to FY2025:

  • Inventory increased approximately 4.3% [CALC from SEC].
  • Revenue declined approximately 0.4% [CALC from SEC].
  • COGS remained approximately $470M [SEC].
  • DIO deteriorated by nine days to 188 [SEC].

That is the freshness-test red flag: more capital sat in inventory even though sales did not grow. At roughly two inventory turns annually, Trulieve lacks the velocity expected from a highly disciplined retail operation.

Inventory fell to $186.9M at June 30, 2026 from $242.3M at year-end [SEARCH, “Trulieve Reports Second Quarter 2026 Results”]. But Event B—the Harvest deconsolidation—removed assets from the reporting perimeter. Without post-deconsolidation COGS or DIO, that decline cannot be credited to better execution.

Inventory verdict: weak and not yet repaired.

2. Supplier power: strong internal control, concentrated external risk

Trulieve is not dependent on a disclosed single Chinese factory or contract manufacturer. In Florida and Georgia, it cultivates, processes, and manufactures all cannabis products sold through its dispensaries [SEC]. Its infrastructure includes:

  • A 750,000-square-foot automated Florida cultivation facility [SEC].
  • Approximately 3.5M square feet of registered production capacity [SEARCH, regulatory event materials].
  • 170 Florida dispensaries, representing 22% of retail doors [SEARCH, TRLV Q2 investor deck].

This is genuine vertical integration. It controls production, processing, distribution, and retail presentation. That reduces supplier bargaining power and should provide better quality control and production planning.

However, vertical integration relocates rather than eliminates concentration risk:

  • Approximately 82% of the cited 206–207 medical dispensaries are in Florida [CALC from SEARCH].
  • Florida may issue 22 additional MMTC licenses, potentially increasing the operator count toward approximately 50 [SEARCH, moat-threat register].
  • Cultivation assets carry energy, environmental, compliance, and capacity-utilization risks.
  • Automation is becoming widely available, reducing the durability of Trulieve’s production-cost advantage [SEARCH, cannabis automation study].

No named single-source supplier was disclosed in the dossier, so a conventional supplier-concentration finding cannot be made. The more important single-source dependency is Florida’s regulatory architecture.

Harvest creates another control problem: Trulieve owns 90% of its economics but has zero operational control, with $188.5M committed and a $3.3M H1 equity loss already recorded [SEC/CALC]. That is not supply-chain ownership; it is capital exposure without operating authority.

Supplier-power verdict: favorable inside the medical operation, but geographically and regulatorily concentrated.

3. Margin discipline: strong gross margin, poor overhead leverage

Gross margin

Using reported revenue and COGS, consolidated gross margins were approximately:

  • FY2023: 52.2% [CALC from SEC].
  • FY2024: 60.4% [CALC from SEC].
  • FY2025: 60.2% [CALC from SEC].

The improvement from FY2023 to FY2024 was substantial. But FY2025 did not deliver further scale benefits: revenue was essentially flat and gross margin held near 60% [SEC/CALC].

More recently:

  • Q2 2026 consolidated gross margin was 60%, down from 61% in Q2 2025 [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 release].
  • H1 2026 gross margin was approximately 59.6%, versus 61.0% in H1 2025 [CALC from SEARCH].
  • The retained medical business produced a 63% margin [SEARCH, Q2 earnings-call transcript].

The medical-only result is encouraging, but one quarter does not establish a durable post-restructuring margin.

Operating expenses

SG&A exposes the weakness beneath gross margin:

  • Q2 SG&A was $101.8M on $271.0M of revenue, versus $101.1M on $302.1M one year earlier [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 release].
  • SG&A therefore rose from approximately 33.5% to 37.6% of revenue [CALC from SEARCH].
  • H1 SG&A fell to $206.7M from $219.9M, but revenue fell faster; SG&A increased slightly from approximately 36.7% to 37.1% of revenue [CALC from SEARCH].

If all disclosed SBC is treated as part of SG&A, cash SG&A was approximately $94.9M in Q2 2026 versus $94.3M in Q2 2025 [CALC from SEARCH]. Cash overhead was therefore effectively flat despite a 10.3% revenue decline [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 release].

That is not operating leverage. Gross margins are disciplined; the overall cost structure is not yet adapting quickly enough.

Reported R&D was effectively zero across FY2022–FY2025 [SEC]. D&A was $25.7M in Q2 and $55.5M in H1 2026 [SEARCH, Trulieve Q2 release]. The FY2026 capex ceiling of $95M [MEDIA, Finsee/X call summary] is close to annualized Q2 D&A of approximately $103M [CALC], suggesting capital spending is broadly supporting the existing asset base plus selective Georgia and Texas expansion.

Margin verdict: good production economics, insufficient corporate leverage.

4. Product and store productivity

Operational throughput is Trulieve’s strongest evidence:

  • Florida flower volume per store was 56% above the market average [SEARCH, Q2 investor deck].
  • Oil volume per store was 20% above the market average [SEARCH].
  • The platform sold 680,000 ounces and 1.5B milligrams of oil—more than twice the next-highest competitor [SEARCH].
  • Medical-market retention was 78% [SEARCH].
  • Medical-only traffic increased 6%, units increased 8%, and revenue increased 4% sequentially [SEARCH, Q2 transcript].

This is real utilization advantage. The concern is monetization. The dossier provides no average ticket, basket size, price realization, or contribution margin per patient. Aggressive first-visit discounts of 60%, 50%, and 40% [SEARCH, Florida 4/20 deals] and industry price compression mean high unit volume does not automatically equal superior economic value.

5. Cash conversion and balance-sheet capacity

H1 operating cash flow fell to $108.8M from $137.2M [SEARCH, Q2 release], a decline of approximately 20.7% [CALC]. Q2 FCF was $32M and H1 FCF was $74M [SEARCH, same source].

Liquidity itself is adequate:

  • Cash was $325.4M [SEARCH, Q2 release].
  • Debt due within 24 months was approximately $32M [SEC/CALC].
  • The $200M private placement matures in 2030 [SEC].

But Event C dominates the balance sheet. The uncertain tax-position liability was $598M at Q2 [SEARCH, Q2 release], exceeding cash by approximately $273M [CALC]. Earlier reported exposure was $696.4M, with interest accrual reaching $15.2M in Q1 2026 [SEARCH, Q1 10-Q summary]. Operations cannot be graded independently of a liability capable of consuming years of free cash flow.

6. Regulatory event mapping

  • Event A—medical Schedule III: removes prospective §280E pressure from medical activity after April 22, 2026. It improves potential cash conversion, not gross margin.
  • Event B—Harvest deconsolidation: causes the inventory, asset, revenue, and earnings discontinuity. It also produced the reported $403.3M deconsolidation loss [SEARCH, Q2 release].
  • Event C—retroactive refund dispute: drives the $598–696M liability range and is the main reason the equity deserves a high required return [SEARCH].
  • Event D—adult-use rescheduling: determines whether Harvest eventually receives §280E relief; it should not be priced as assured.
  • Event E—failed Florida legalization: limits near-term expansion of Trulieve’s dominant Florida footprint despite more than $177M of disclosed campaign contributions [SEARCH/CALC].
  • Event F—hemp restrictions: could reduce substitute competition, but the direction and timing remain unsettled.

My base case prices enacted Events A and B, assumes no favorable resolution of Events C or D, and assigns no value to another Florida adult-use attempt.

Valuation discipline and trigger

At $11.32 [CALC], the pre-restructuring consolidated owner yield is 2.5% [CALC]. That is inadequate against the dossier’s 13–18% local cost of equity, derived from a roughly 4% US risk-free rate, approximately 5% equity-risk premium, and a substantial cannabis-sector premium. The historical owner yield is also the wrong entity basis, so it is only a warning—not a valuation anchor.

Annualizing Q2 and H1 transition-period FCF produces approximately $128–148M [CALC from SEARCH]. Against the cited $2.13B market capitalization [MEDIA, stock-snapshot source], that is only a roughly 6.0–6.9% FCF yield [CALC], again far below the 13–18% local cost-of-equity hurdle.

Using the 15% midpoint and approximately 192.57M shares [SEARCH], those transition FCF levels support roughly $4.40–$5.10 per share [CALC]. Even there, I would require confirmation that the cash flow is genuinely medical-only and that Event C will not consume it.

Operations health grade: C+

The core medical production system earns a B: vertically integrated supply, 63% gross margin, high retention, and excellent store throughput. Inventory discipline, overhead leverage, structural comparability, and uncontrolled tax exposure reduce the total operating grade to C+.

This is a productive machine with too much aging inventory, too little demonstrated overhead flexibility, and one enormous government-controlled liability. At the current price, the correct position is zero.

---SUMMARY--- VERDICT: PASS CONFIDENCE: 68 KEY METRIC: Uncertain tax position liability $598–696M [SEC] = 28–33% of the $2.13B market cap [MEDIA], sitting atop an owner yield of only 2.5% TTM [CALC] against a 13–18% local cost of equity hurdle [dossier] TRIGGER PRICE: $6–7 @ 15% required return (mid-point of the 13–18% hurdle) — that band prices in a realistic probability-weighted loss on the IRS dispute while still paying the medical-only entity's ~7–8% run-rate FCF yield [CALC from Q1'26 FCF $42M / Q2'26 FCF $32M annualized].
