NON-FATAL FINDINGS

Five. Not one changes a number, a conclusion, or a reader's decision. I list them because the brief asked what Revision 6 introduced, not because any of them should hold the report.

1. §5's third rejection argument does not do the work the other two do. "§280E is a tax provision and EBITDA is pre-tax, so Schedule III relief cannot lift EBITDA by a dollar — which is what broke Lynch's bridge and applies equally to anyone re-rating this on a multiple." Against Lynch this is decisive. Against the peer-multiple buyer it is weaker: that buyer's case is not that EBITDA rises, it is that the multiple should expand because after-tax cash improves — which EV/EBITDA is blind to in both directions, so the argument is neutral there rather than adverse. Immaterial: it is the third of three, arguments (i) capital intensity and (ii) the table brackets everything carry the rejection on their own, and the memo has already conceded the conclusion this argument would otherwise be defending.

2. The 40/60 weighting is a sentence, not an input. 0.4 × 9.45 + 0.6 × 3.13 = $5.66, which is not the published central of $5.84 — because the central is the r = 14% / p = 75% grid cell, as it should be. The same was true of Revision 5's 30/70 (= $4.91 against a published $5.11). The weighting has never driven the arithmetic in either draft, so moving it changes nothing. It landed as prose; it should not be read as having moved a number.

3. The bear-corner Harvest mark of $150M has no stated derivation. Pass 5 NF7 killed the old $130M for being twice the top of the memo's own 10–15% method and asked for $157M. The memo abandoned the haircut method entirely in favour of a range — so there is no longer a stated method for $150M to violate — but it also gives no basis for $150M specifically. Worth $7M ÷ 192.57 = $0.036/share at the bear corner only. Third revision running in which one printed figure lacks a derivation; it is now down to one figure and four cents.

4. Two cosmetic mismatches between the stated rule and the printed numbers. §4 says "Fair value limit is the central estimate; the floor is the bear corner" and then prints $3.15–$5.85 against a central of $5.84 and a bear corner of $3.13 — a one- and two-cent rounding-up in each direction. And §7's "every corrected central across six revisions — $4.45, $5.26, $5.62, $5.11, $5.84" lists five values for six revisions (the sequence is inherited verbatim from pass 5's own §(h) list, with $5.84 substituted for $5.95, and $5.62 in it is pass 4's corrected central rather than a memo central). The claim it supports is unaffected — 55% of $11.08 is $6.09, and every listed value plus every unlisted earlier one is below it.

5. One phrase is now closer to its own limit than the memo says. §7: "the whole range rests on a medical-only selling-expense line no human being has ever printed." Revision 6 itself now cites management's 32% adjusted SG&A. That figure is consolidated (Harvest until 3 June) and non-GAAP, so it is not a medical-only selling-expense line and the claim survives intact — but the memo would be more precise saying so at the point where it makes the absolute claim, as it already does two sections earlier.

WHAT I TRIED TO BREAK AND COULD NOT

  • A double-count between the accretion and the principal charge. There is none; I decomposed the payment stream and traced the $45M of gross interest to instruments. Clean.
  • A double-fix on the tax claim (stripping 100bp from r and reducing the explicit charge for overlap). The p = 75% move is separately grounded in ASC 740-10, not in the overlap. Clean.
  • The band being a third number pulled between pass 5's two restatements. It is a fresh computation from revised inputs and it reproduces to the cent on the memo's own printed EBITDA endpoints. Clean.
  • The October 2022 vintage. Confirmed at source, and confirmed non-load-bearing either way.
  • A refuted sentence still carrying weight. Eight supports enumerated, none refuted.
  • The maintenance capex change being backfilling. It was requested, it runs the other way from the accusation, and the memo implemented it more conservatively than requested.

ONE-LINE VERDICT ON THE VERDICT

No FATAL findings. All four of pass 5's FATAL corrections landed and landed correctly: the 250bp cost-of-money finding is withdrawn outright with nothing downstream depending on it; the 100bp strip is the conservative end of the red team's own attribution range, explicitly labelled as unknowable judgment rather than derivation, and r = 14% is used consistently in the claim PV, the grid, the sensitivity, the back-solve and the load-bearing factors with no stale 15% anywhere; p moves to 75% with the accreting interest now charged inside the claim's payment stream and nowhere else, so the $448.65M of principal and $143.10M of accrued interest each appear exactly once against a $45M owner-earnings interest line that is debt-only; and "no coherent construction reaches $11.08" is replaced by a worked falsification of itself, a corrected claim, three stated grounds for rejecting the peer frame, and a conviction downgrade — an argument that concedes rather than an assertion that restates. The October 2022 vintage claim verifies at raw_verify.txt:181 against a dossier that says no date is stated, and it is non-load-bearing either way. The band $3.13–$9.45 with a central of $5.84 is not a blend of pass 5's two restatements and should not be — it is a fresh computation from revised inputs that reproduces to the cent on the memo's own printed EBITDA endpoints, with the central inside pass 5's $5.41–$5.95 and the bull corner inside pass 5's $8.74–$9.95. The maintenance capex move to $60M was requested by pass 5's non-fatal 5, runs bullish rather than bearish, and was implemented $5M short of what was asked with a stated reason — the opposite of the pathology the question suspected. Every reproducible figure in the document reproduces: the grid, both corners, the p-sensitivity, the claim PV, the EV and peer back-run, the $10.89, the $2.81, the $286M back-solve and a stress test that ties for the second consecutive draft. Five non-fatal items remain — a third rejection argument in §5 that is neutral rather than adverse against the peer buyer, a 40/60 weighting that drives no number and never did, an undeviated $150M Harvest bear mark worth four cents, two cosmetic rounding mismatches and an off-by-one revision count that leaves its own claim intact, and one phrase that would be more precise with a qualifier it already supplies elsewhere. None of them changes a number, a conclusion, or a reader's decision. No sentence refuted by any prior pass survives in the document, and the headline verdict rests on none of them. PUBLISH.

ARCHIVE — PASS 1 (full report)

REALITY CHECK — RED TEAM · TRULIEVE CANNABIS CORP. (NYSE: TRLV)

Date: 2026-08-26 · Memo under review: Munger Synthesis, WAIT, trigger $2.00–$3.25, position 0% Reviewers: The Ghost of Charlie Munger · The Living Warren Buffett

HEADLINE FINDING (read this first)

The verdict survives. The arithmetic under it does not.

Every correction I found runs in the same direction — the memo is too harsh, not too generous — and none of them is large enough to change the answer. Corrected for two real errors, fair value is roughly $2.75–$4.50, not $2.00–$3.25. Against $11.08, that is still a refusal. WAIT / 0% stands. The published band is wrong by about 25–40% and must be restated.

Two of the four overcorrection hypotheses the parent asked me to test are confirmed. Two are rejected on the data — I ran them and they failed, and I say so below rather than manufacturing symmetry.

# Finding Grade Verdict impact
1 SBC double-counted (~$28M/yr) in the owner-earnings build CONFIRMED ERROR Band +25%
2 Stress test inherits the SBC error and uses consolidated revenue decline as evidence about a medical-only entity — the exact error Munger convicts Lynch of CONFIRMED ERROR (double standard) Kills a Load-Bearing Factor headline
3 UTP $696.4M → $598M "unexplained" FLAG MUST BE WITHDRAWN — I reconciled it to the cent Removes a red flag
4 "Killer cross-check, free of any tautology" is the valuation restated CHECK-0 VIOLATION Sentence must be rewritten
5 (a) 280E-timing category error in the $26M annualization REJECTED — the data runs the other way, and $26M is not load-bearing anyway None
6 (b) "Set the liability to zero" claim collapses if (a) holds REJECTED — (b) never rested on (a) None
7 Munger's rebuttal of Lynch's medical-only EBITDA VERIFIED CORRECT — and understated Lynch BUY case is not live
8 Bear points the memo missed Three, below Reinforces bear

CHECK 0 — TAUTOLOGY GATE

0.1 Munger's own tautology catch: VERIFIED, EXACT, and the best work in the memo

He claims Buffett's "$280–390M bull case" is the price back-solved. I derived it independently:

$11.32 × 192.57M shares = $2,180M
$2,180M × 13% = $283M
$2,180M × 18% = $392M

Buffett's stated range: $283–392M. Not approximately. Exactly. One witness in two hats — the hurdle wearing a forecast's clothes. Munger is right, the catch is material, and six experts would have been misled by it. Credit where due.

0.2 The echo test: VERIFIED, EXACT

Jobs, Psychologist and Anthropologist all published $4.25–$5.90. Derivation checks:

$148M ÷ 18% ÷ 192.57M = $4.27
$148M ÷ 13% ÷ 192.57M = $5.91

Identical to the cent, from one input, with no deduction for net debt and no credit for Harvest — cruder than Munger's own build. Three hats, one witness. The echo finding stands.

0.3 Munger's OWN tautology — CHECK-0 VIOLATION

He writes: "And the killer cross-check, free of any tautology: to justify $11.08 at a 15% hurdle requires $320M of owner earnings on $888M of revenue — a 36% owner-earnings margin."

Derive it:

Valuation:    V = OE / r  →  P_fair = OE / (r × N)
Cross-check:  OE_req = P_mkt × N × r  →  m* = (P_mkt × N × r) / Rev

Therefore:    P_fair / P_mkt  ≡  OE / OE_req

Check:  $2.90 (his midpoint) / $11.08 = 0.262
        $80M / $320M            = 0.250

Same statement. The "cross-check" is his valuation wearing a percent sign. It is not free of tautology, and calling it that is precisely the failure mode this gate exists to catch.

It is not fully empty — it substitutes an industry feasibility ceiling for the observed run rate, which would be a genuine second witness if the ceiling were sourced. It isn't. See 0.4.

Required fix: delete "free of any tautology." Restate as: "the same conclusion expressed as a required margin, tested against feasibility."

0.4 The Apple/Costco comparison (parent question c) — RHETORICAL ORNAMENT, STRIKE IT

"A 36% owner-earnings margin. Apple earns ~26%. Costco earns ~2%."

Three problems: 1. Definitional mismatch. Apple's ~26% is a net margin; the memo's 36% is an owner-earnings margin (post-capex, pre-D&A). Different numerators. Unsourced, undated. 2. It proves nothing by itself. Trulieve's gross margin is 63% — higher than Apple's ~46%. A naive reader can therefore reverse the comparison and conclude Trulieve is the more profitable structure. The sentence is worse than useless; it is exploitable by the bull. 3. It is unnecessary. The claim stands on Trulieve's own income statement without borrowing anyone's benchmark:

   Gross margin                63.0%
   less SG&amp;A ($305M / $888M)  −34.3%
   less capex ($95M / $888M)  −10.7%
   less cash interest         −3.9%
   ─────────────────────────────────
   Pre-tax owner margin        14.1%   →  ~11% post-tax

To reach 36% you must run this business with negative SG&A. That is arithmetically impossible, and it needs no Cupertino. The supporting evidence that does belong here is already in the dossier and Munger cites it: 24.4% of cannabis operators are profitable at all.

Answer to (c): the comparison is not a fair test and does not prove what it is used to prove. Strike the sentence. The underlying claim survives without it, and is stronger without it.

0.5 Assumption smuggling — DISCLOSURE GAP, not FATAL

Every headline number is computed at a fixed 13–18% hurdle with nothing else varied. The hurdle is dossier-prescribed, so it is not smuggled. But the memo never shows the sensitivity, and the sensitivity is large:

Hurdle Fair value/share (corrected OE $100M)
18% $2.73
15% $3.29
13% $4.05
10% $5.11
8% $6.35

At every rate the answer is "not at $11.08." The verdict is robust; the precision is not. Show the table.

THE REAL CHARLIE MUNGER'S TAKE

Well. Somebody did the hard work and then tripped over his own shoelaces on the way to the podium.

I'll say the good part first, because it's genuinely good. He caught Buffett back-solving the price and calling it a forecast. He refused to value a business on EBITDA and explained why in a sentence any fool can check — capex eats 35% of it before a nickel reaches an owner. He noticed that a 63% gross margin is a cost position, not pricing power, and he had the discounting evidence to prove it: a company handing out 60% off your first visit does not have pricing power, it has customers who will walk across the parking lot for a coupon. And he charged stock-based compensation as a real expense. That is more intellectual honesty than I get from most nine-figure sell-side departments.

Now the shoelaces.

FINDING 1 — He counted the rat poison twice

His bottom-up build:

Revenue $888M × 63% GM      =  $559M
less SG&amp;A                    − $305M
less capex                   −  $95M
less cash interest           −  $35M
less SBC                     −  $28M
─────────────────────────────────────
                             =  $96M pre-tax  →  ~$78M owner earnings

SBC is already inside SG&A. Proof from the company's own expense reconciliation, and the prior-year quarter settles it beyond argument:

Q2&#x27;25:  SG&amp;A 101.1 + D&amp;A 29.4          = 130.5   vs total expenses 130.3   ✓
        adding SBC 6.8                  = 137.3   overshoot 7.0            ✗

Q2&#x27;26:  SG&amp;A 101.8 + D&amp;A 25.7 + deconsol 403.3 = 530.8  vs total 532.8    ✓
        adding SBC 6.9                          = 537.7  overshoot 4.9    ✗

H1&#x27;25:  219.9 + 58.8 = 278.7  vs 280.2  ✓   H1&#x27;26: 206.7 + 55.5 + 403.3 = 665.5  vs 667.2  ✓

Share-based compensation is a memo disclosure inside SG&A, not an additive line. Deducting it again is a $28M/yr pre-tax double charge — a 26% understatement of owner earnings.

Corrected: pre-tax $124M, owner earnings ~$100M, not $78–80M.

$100M ÷ 18% = $556M  + Harvest $110M − net debt $140M = $526M →  $2.73/sh
$100M ÷ 13% = $769M  + Harvest $150M − net debt $140M = $779M →  $4.05/sh

His "most generous defensible" ceiling (SG&A $250M) goes from $4.33 to ~$5.85.

I tested a second suspected double-count — cultivation depreciation buried in COGS, charged again as capex — and it does not exist. The cash-flow D&A add-back ($25.7M Q2, $55.5M H1) equals the income-statement D&A line exactly, so D&A sits in operating expense, gross profit is pre-depreciation, and his frame is clean. One error, not two. I say so because a red team that only finds what it went looking for isn't a red team.

FINDING 2 — The stress test does to itself what it accuses Lynch of doing

Item 10 was missing from the pipeline and he built it himself. Two things are wrong with it.

(i) It inherits the SBC error. Buffer is $124M, not $96M:

Net drag per revenue dollar: 0.63 − (0.343 × 0.35) = $0.51   ✓ his figure, correct
Revenue at risk per 100% decline: 0.51 × $888M = $453M

FCF breakeven, no settlement:          $124M / $453M = 27%   (he published 22%)
FCF breakeven, $60M/yr IRS installment: $64M / $453M = 14%   (he published 8%)

"FCF turns negative at just an 8% revenue decline" is Load-Bearing Factor #2 in his Executive Summary. The correct number is ~14%. That is the difference between a cliff edge and a slope.

(ii) The double standard. He convicts Lynch — correctly — of using a consolidated EBITDA figure to describe a medical-only entity. He then writes: "Consolidated revenue already fell 10.3% YoY in Q2'26." That is a consolidated number offered as evidence that the medical-only entity is sliding toward his 8–22% thresholds. Same error, mirror image.

Q2&#x27;26 consolidated $271.0M vs Q2&#x27;25 $302.1M = −10.3%
But Harvest was inside Q2&#x27;26 for only ~2 of 3 months (deconsolidated 3-Jun).
Harvest stub revenue $49M over ~2 months → full-quarter equivalent ~$73.5M.
Like-for-like perimeter: $222M + $73.5M = $295.5M vs $302.1M = −2.2%

And the only medical-only datapoint that exists: Q1 ~$213.5M → Q2 $222M = +4% sequential,
traffic +6%, units +8%.

Roughly 8 of the 10.3 points are a deconsolidation artifact. The genuine underlying decline is ~2%, and H1 like-for-like is maybe −5%. A 14% decline from a base currently printing +4% sequential is not "the arithmetic of doubling the doors." It is a scenario, and it should be labelled one.

I want to be fair to the bear case here: one quarter of +4% against 22 incoming competitors and collapsing flower prices is not a trend, and the discounting evidence says that +6% traffic was bought. But you cannot prosecute Lynch for entity mismatch in Section 4 and commit it in Section 3.

FINDING 3 — The $98M "unexplained" UTP drop. I explained it. Withdraw the flag.

He calls it "a $98M reduction the record does not explain" and demands a full 10-Q reading before anyone underwrites the equity. The record explains it, in the forensic brief he was handed.

He compared a Q1 10-Q figure ("uncertain tax position liabilities, net, of $696.4M", explicitly excluding $82.9M of overpayment claims) against a Q2 balance-sheet line. Different basis, different date. The balance sheet's own comparative is right there:

Uncertain tax position liabilities:  $598.2M (30-Jun-26)  vs  $668.4M (31-Dec-25)

Now roll it forward using the cash-flow accrual line ($61.0M added in H1'26):

From Dec-25 basis:  668.4 + 61.0 − 598.2  =  $131.2M removed
From Mar-26 basis:  696.4 + 32.9 − 598.2  =  $131.1M removed

Both bases land on $131M, and it all happened in Q2 — the quarter of the deconsolidation. That is Harvest's share of the 280E exposure leaving the balance sheet with Harvest. Confirmation sits on the same page: deferred tax liabilities fell $178.0M → $57.2M, discontinued-ops liabilities went to zero, operating leases $107.9M → $85.8M, construction finance $133.8M → $120.2M. Every liability line fell, because a quarter of the company left.

Flagging one line as a forensic anomaly while ignoring the seven around it with the same cause is a failure of the null hypothesis. Recommendation: withdraw the red flag; replace with "reconcile in the 10-Q." It is no longer a reason not to underwrite the equity.

But the finding I'd substitute is worse for the bulls, and nobody on this council raised it. Under IRC §1502, every member of a consolidated federal return group is severally liable for the group's entire tax for years it was a member. Moving Harvest out in June 2026 removes $131M from the balance sheet; it does not remove Trulieve's several liability for pre-2026 tax years, which is what the whole dispute is about. And Trulieve retains 90% of Harvest's economics anyway. Off the books is not off the hook. Verify this in the 10-Q contingencies note — that is the question worth asking.

FINDING 4 — Three bear points he left on the table

(1) The cash build is borrowed. Cash went $255.5M → $325.4M, +$69.9M, and he cites the $325.4M as the liquidity that makes a $598M crystallization survivable. Bridge it:

OCF 108.8 − capex 34.8 − cash disposed with Harvest 58.4 + private placement increase 59.1 ≈ +74.7

Private placement notes went $136.7M → $195.8M. Without ~$59M of new debt, cash fell. The liquidity cushion he leans on is financed, not earned.

(2) $20M of adjusted net income in Q1 and $20M in Q2 — with Schedule III in between. Q1'26 had zero days of §280E relief. Q2'26 had ~70 days of it on the medical business. Adjusted net income was $20M in both. The rescheduling produced no visible earnings lift in the only period we can observe it. That is the single most direct test of the entire bull thesis available in this record, it is one line long, and no one ran it. (Caveat honestly: Q2 also lost a month of Harvest.)

(3) "Adjusted" has been a work of fiction before. FY2025 adjusted EBITDA $427M (36% of revenue) against a computed GAAP EBITDA of $330M (28%) — a $97M/yr gap of impairments, disposals and "non-recurring" charges that recur annually. And the $20M/qtr adjusted net income he annualizes to $80M follows a full-year 2025 adjusted net LOSS of $27M and a Q4'25 adjusted net loss of $3M. He is annualizing two good quarters out of a six-quarter series. That cuts bearish and strengthens him — he should have said it.

FINDING 5 — Too Hard. He refuses the pile. He is wrong to refuse it, and right about the answer.

"I checked, and I do not qualify — deliberately... When a variable you cannot calculate does not change the answer, you do not get to hide behind it."

I admire the move. It is a legitimate one: if the answer is identical at both ends of a binary, the binary isn't load-bearing. But it only works if the non-binary part is solid, and look at what the non-binary part rests on:

  • one quarter of medical-only revenue and gross margin, and nothing else — no medical-only SG&A, no medical-only EBITDA, no medical-only cash flow, ever reported
  • an SG&A allocation ($305M) with no disclosure basis whatsoever, on which the entire valuation pivots
  • a stress test the author built himself, now shown to be wrong by 6 points
  • a peer table that is [MEDIA], undated, and contradicted inside the same dossier (Green Thumb at 7.3x or ~3.5x)
  • Section M returned nothing; Section H returned an MLB team and a utility
  • two unresolved numerical conflicts in the company's own record
  • no verbatim management Q&A, despite a CONTROVERSY_DIALOGUE tag

Sit on your ass, gentlemen. You are permitted to.

The honest formulation is: "Too Hard to own. Not too hard to refuse." A 0% position is the Too Hard pile with better manners. What is not earned is the two-significant-figure band. Publishing $2.00–$3.25 on a business with one partial quarter of data, where two accounting corrections move the band 25–40%, is exactly the false precision this council has been told about before. Publish $2.75–$4.50 and call it a range of ignorance, not a range of estimate.

FINDING 6 — Internal inconsistencies a memo built on arithmetic discipline cannot afford

Three different numbers for the same thing:

Location Value
§0 Echo Test $2.15–$3.45
§2 Valuation (computed: $414M–$625M ÷ 192.57M) $2.15–$3.25 ✓
§5 Trigger / Executive Summary $2.00–$3.25 / $2.15–$3.45

$625M ÷ 192.57M = $3.246. The $3.45 is unexplained and appears in the Thesis-in-One-Sentence. The $2.00 floor is below his own computed $2.15 and is a judgment overlay labelled "absurdly cheap." Pick one and show it.

FINDING 7 — Where he was too generous, for completeness

  • Net debt excludes $85.8M of operating leases on a 170-store retailer, and excludes ~$26M of current portions. Including them lowers his band ~$0.45/share.
  • He quotes 6.8x against Lynch's peer range using the top of his own EBITDA range ($323M). At his low end ($254M) the stock is at 8.7x — above the peer midpoint. He published only the half that flattered Lynch.
  • He charges $95M of capex when H1 actual annualizes to $70M, and $10M of the guided increase is explicitly growth capex for Georgia and Texas. Buffett's own definition charges maintenance capex. A defensible maintenance figure is $70–85M — worth another $0.30–$0.80/share.
  • The stress test uses pre-tax FCF as the buffer while the valuation applies a 26% cash tax. Post-tax the thresholds are worse.

RAT POISON, BULLSHIT EARNINGS, PRICING POWER — the standing tests

  • SBC: charged, correctly, then charged again. He also never prices the dilution: 1.6%/yr on a $2.13B cap is $34M/yr of value transferred — more than the $27.6M expense.
  • EBITDA: explicitly refused, with a checkable reason (capex = 35% of it). ✓ Uses it only to explain the market's frame, and labels it as such. That is how it should be done.
  • Pricing power: correctly denied. 60/50/40% off the first three visits, national flower prices falling three straight years, 24.4% of operators profitable, and a state statute that mints four new competitors per 100,000 new patients. This is a commodity sold through a licence.

THE REAL WARREN BUFFETT'S TAKE

Charlie's done the hard arithmetic, so let me do the easy part, which is asking whether any of it was necessary.

On the circle of competence. In seven weeks this company changed its tax regime, its corporate structure, and its stock exchange. It now owns 90% of the economics of a business it has no control over whatsoever — someone else with 10% runs it, and it has already lost money. Its largest liability is a disputed government claim worth 28% of the market value, with no timeline, where the IRS has said in public that the refund claims "are not valid." Its moat is a count of licences that the Florida legislature has written a formula for doubling.

I don't know what this is worth. Neither does the memo, and I say that as its defender, because it produced two numbers for the same company on the same data — $2.15–$3.25 on owner earnings and $7.06–$9.87 on what a private buyer would pay. That is a three-fold gap between two honest frames. Charlie explains the gap well, and his explanation is checkable — $95M of capex is 35% of $270M of EBITDA, so the EBITDA buyer is paying for cash that never arrives. I think he's right. But when two frames disagree by 3x, the correct posture is humility about the number and firmness about the action.

On the toll bridge. This is not one. A toll bridge is something people must cross and cannot route around. Here the state issues the tokens, and §381.986 Florida Statutes says the state must issue four more for every 100,000 new patients. Twenty-two are queued. A moat granted by a licence board is un-granted by a licence board — Charlie's line, and it is the truest sentence in the document. The 78% retention is real and it is the best fact the bulls have; but retention measured across 22% of the doors tells you nothing about retention across 50 operators. And a business that leads with 60% off your first visit is buying that retention, not earning it.

On capital preservation — Rule Number One. Set aside every argument above. At $11.08 you are paying $2.13 billion for a business that, corrected in its own favor, earns about $100 million, carries a $598 million disputed government claim against $325 million of cash — most of which arrived by borrowing — and whose competitive protection is on a published statutory dissolution schedule. That is a 4.7% yield against a 13–18% required return, before the liability. You don't need the third decimal place to decline that.

On Mr. Lynch, who deserves the courtesy of a straight answer. I checked his rebuttal myself, because the parent asked me to and because Peter is nobody's fool.

Munger's correction: Lynch's $350–450M "medical-only" EBITDA is really the consolidated Q1 figure. Verified, and it is worse than Munger says.

  1. Q1'26 ended 31 March 2026. The deconsolidation was 3 June 2026. The $100M of Q1 adjusted EBITDA that Lynch annualizes to $400M is unambiguously consolidated and contains Harvest. Munger is right on the fact.
  2. Lynch's bridge is a category error. He argues EBITDA rises "as the 280E benefit flows through a full year." §280E is a tax provision. EBITDA is before tax. Schedule III relief cannot lift EBITDA by a single dollar. Neither Lynch nor Munger noticed this, and it removes Lynch's entire mechanism for getting from $400M to $450M.
  3. Cross-check on the level. Consolidated adjusted EBITDA has run 35–36% of revenue (FY2025 $427M/36%; Q4'25 $105M/36%; Q1'26 $100M/35%). Applied to $888M of medical revenue with 3 points of margin uplift, that is ~$320–340M — right at the top of Munger's $254–323M and clearly below Lynch's $350–450M. On GAAP EBITDA (FY2025 $330M, 28% of revenue) it is ~$275M, mid-range of Munger's.
  4. The peer anchor is unusable in both directions. Munger says 6.8x is "at the doorstep" of a 7.3–9.6x peer range — but that table is [MEDIA], undated, from a promotional piece, and the same dossier reports Green Thumb at ~3.5x from a different source. If 3.5x is right, TRLV at 6.8x is expensive, not cheap. Section M returned nothing. Building any part of a rebuttal on this table — even the part that helps the bull — is a discipline failure that the memo should disclose rather than paper over.
  5. The catalyst is spent. The uplist was 10 June. The stock is already $10.09 → $11.32 since 19 August.
  6. And the direct test. Adjusted net income was $20M in the quarter with no 280E relief and $20M in the quarter with 70 days of it.

Conclusion: Lynch's BUY is not live. Munger's rebuttal holds, and I'd have made it harder. What survives of Peter's case is the honest half he stated himself — "I'm not going to pretend the FCF yield clears the hurdle today, because it doesn't." A man who tells you his own thesis doesn't clear the hurdle has told you what to do.

I will note one thing in his favour that the memo buries: Lynch is the only expert who correctly identified that the −10.3% headline is a deconsolidation artifact. He was right about that, Munger repeats the artifact as evidence in his own stress test, and the memo gives him no credit for it.

On Mr. Burry's short, and whether the refusal was reasoned or asserted (parent question d). Munger declines a 1% short on "instrument risk," and names three mechanisms: a live DEA rescheduling hearing with an unknown decision date, a possible Florida ballot revival, and a $2.13B float in a retail-narrative sector.

That is stated reasoning, not bare assertion. It identifies a specific, correct asymmetry: an unhedgeable binary catalyst on an unknown clock. And it is the capital-preservation-consistent answer — Rule Number One applies to shorts twice over, because the loss is unbounded and the clock isn't yours.

But it is entirely unquantified, and he doesn't admit that. No borrow cost. No short interest. No days-to-cover. I searched the full record — the dossier contains none of these. He therefore could not quantify it. The defect is not the reasoning; it is failing to write the sentence "and the record contains no borrow or short-interest data, so this judgement is unverifiable." Say what you don't know.

There is also a tension he should own: he asserts the stock is ~3.5x fair value and then declines to act in either direction. That is defensible and it is what I would do — but the confident multiple and the refusal to act sit oddly on the same page, and the honest reconciliation is that the multiple is less certain than stated. Which brings us back to the band.

Would I buy this? No. Would I short it? No. Would I put it in the Too Hard pile? I already have. The memo reaches the right destination by a road with two potholes in it. Fix the potholes; keep the destination.

VERDICT ON THE PARENT'S SPECIFIC QUESTIONS

(a) Is the $26M annualization a category error that understates the business by a large multiple? — NO. HYPOTHESIS REJECTED.

The hypothesis: H1'26 straddles Schedule III (effective 22-Apr), so the $61.0M uncertain-tax-position add-back reflects a §280E regime that has largely stopped; stripping it and annualizing turns a pre-relief period into a post-relief run rate.

Elegant. The data kills it three ways.

(i) The accrual did not fall when relief arrived — it rose. Split the H1 line into quarters:

H1&#x27;26 add-back  $61.0M   |   Q2&#x27;26  $32.9M   →   Q1&#x27;26 = $28.1M
H1&#x27;25 add-back $114.4M   |   Q2&#x27;25  $58.7M   →   Q1&#x27;25 = $55.7M

Q1'26 was entirely pre-relief. Q2'26 was ~77% post-relief for medical activity. The accrual went UP 17% sequentially, $28.1M → $32.9M. If Schedule III were switching off new §280E disallowance, this is the number that would have collapsed. It didn't.

(ii) The year-over-year decline predates the relief entirely. Q1'25 $55.7M → Q1'26 $28.1M is a 50% fall, and every day of both quarters is pre-22-April. The H1 decline is not a Schedule III effect.

(iii) A large and growing slice of the add-back is interest that Schedule III does nothing about. The Q1 10-Q: "interest on uncertain tax positions totaling $15.2 million and $9.0 million for the three months ended March 31, 2026 and 2025... to the provision for income taxes." So of Q1'26's $28.1M, $15.2M is interest on the legacy dispute — up 69% YoY and compounding on $598M of principal. Rescheduling medical cannabis in April 2026 has no effect whatsoever on interest accruing against deductions taken in 2019–2025. The add-back does not go to zero prospectively.

(iv) And the mechanics run backwards from the hypothesis anyway. The add-back is a non-cash item that ADDS to operating cash flow. If it disappeared, reported OCF would go down, not up. Cash taxes cannot "fall" from a level that was never being paid — that is the entire point of an accrual. Relief lowers the expense; the cash was already being withheld.

(v) Most importantly: $26M is not load-bearing. Munger uses it once, in §0, to demolish three experts' $148M input. He does not use it in the valuation. His valuation is a bottom-up P&L build with a 26% cash tax rate — already fully post-Schedule-III. The alleged category error, even if real, never reaches the answer.

What IS wrong here is rhetorical, and should be fixed: the memo brandishes $26M to discredit the council, then quietly values on $80M, and never reconciles the two. A reader can easily leave thinking the business earns $26M. Add one sentence: "$26M is what the cash flow statement shows if every disputed dollar is eventually paid at the old tax expense; $80M is what the business earns paying normal taxes going forward. The forward number is the right one, and I use it."

Separately — a much cleaner attack on the $148M that Munger missed. H1'26 FCF of $74M is CONSOLIDATED: Harvest was inside the group for 5 of 6 months. Annualizing it to describe a medical-only entity is an entity mismatch, full stop — no tax argument needed. That single sentence demolishes the $4.25–$5.90 trigger more cleanly than the whole add-back argument. He had the better weapon and used the worse one.

(b) Does "set the liability to zero and it's still only worth $2.15–$3.45" collapse if (a) is right? — NO. THE PREMISE IS WRONG.

The parent's linkage assumes (b) rests on the $26M annualization. It does not. It rests on the ~$80M bottom-up build, which is independent of the tax add-back and already assumes full Schedule III relief. (a) and (b) are not connected, so (b) cannot collapse through (a).

(b) does, however, need restating for an unrelated reason — the SBC double-count. Corrected, the sentence becomes:

> Set the liability to zero — assume Trulieve wins outright, keeps the $113M, owes nothing — and the stock is still worth roughly $2.75–$4.05 against a price of $11.08.

The structural claim — the IRS is not the load-bearing variable — is the memo's single best insight and it survives every correction I made. Even at a 10% discount rate with zero tax liability and the generous SG&A assumption, I cannot construct a value above ~$5.85 on an owner-earnings basis. The stock is $11.08.

(c) The Apple/Costco comparison — RHETORICAL ORNAMENT. STRIKE IT. See §0.4.

(d) Burry's short — REASONED, BUT UNQUANTIFIED AND NOT ADMITTED AS SUCH. See Buffett's take.

The UTP flag — WITHDRAW IT. Reconciled to $131M in Finding 3. Replace with the §1502 several-liability question, which is the real one.
