Three sentences to add: 1. "The peer table is undated [MEDIA], Section M returned nothing, and the same dossier reports Green Thumb at both 7.3x and ~3.5x. No part of this analysis should rest on it, including the part that flatters the bull case." 2. "The record contains no borrow cost, short interest, or days-to-cover. My refusal of Burry's short is a judgement, not a calculation." 3. "Cash rose $69.9M in H1 while private placement notes rose $59.1M. The liquidity cushion is financed, not earned."

THE "OLD SCHOOL" FINAL SCORECARD

  • Circle of Competence: OUT. Tax regime, corporate structure and listing venue all rewritten in seven weeks. One quarter of go-forward revenue and gross margin — and nothing else medical-only ever reported. A liability worth 28% of market cap resolved by a government on no timetable. A moat with a statutory dissolution formula. Item 10 absent, Section M empty, Section H contaminated, no management Q&A, two unresolved numerical conflicts in the company's own record. This is outside the circle, and being outside the circle is not a failure — it's the whole point of drawing one.

  • Moat Integrity: NARROW AND ON A CLOCK — the licence, not the customer. The scale evidence is genuine (35% flower share on 22% of doors, 56% more flower per store, 2x the next competitor's oil volume, 78% medical retention). But it is cost position, not pricing power — proved by 60/50/40% off the first three visits, three straight years of falling flower prices, and 24.4% of operators profitable. §381.986 Fla. Stat. mandates four new MMTCs per 100,000 new patients; 22 are queued; 28 operators become ~50. Munger's line is the correct one: a moat granted by a licence board is un-granted by a licence board. Half-life 3–5 years is, if anything, generous.

  • Management Character: PROMOTERS, with one genuinely sophisticated act. $177M of shareholder money — 8% of market cap — into two failed ballot initiatives, one beaten 56% against a 60% bar, the second dead on signatures. Reporting on a "medical-only" basis that has no cost line beneath the gross margin. "Adjusted EBITDA" running $97M/yr above GAAP on charges that recur every year. CEO: 0 purchases, 7 sales, 1,699,007 shares, ~$14.7M, under a plan adopted eleven weeks before a restructuring the record cannot date the decision on. Against that: the Harvest deconsolidation genuinely solved a listing problem no MSO had solved. Clever is not the same as aligned. Zero purchases at any price is its own sentence.

  • Berkshire Buy? NO. Too Hard, and cheap wouldn't fix it. At $2.75 we would be buying a licence lottery ticket with a $598M government claim attached and no control over 90% of the economics we supposedly own. That is not a business we can hold for twenty years without reading a Federal Register notice every morning.

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the SECURITY: F. Negative earnings, ROE −47.7%, no dividend, no carry, 6.5% dilution, book $6.07 against $11.08, largest liability disputed and >25% of market cap, price 2.5–4x every defensible intrinsic estimate. Graham's margin of safety here is negative. Nothing in the corrections changes this grade.

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the MEMO'S WORK: B. It caught a genuine circular argument, ran a real echo test, refused EBITDA with a checkable reason, charged SBC, and reached the right decision through a data pipeline with four holes in it. It loses the A for a $28M double-count that moves the answer 26%, a stress test committing the exact error it prosecutes elsewhere, a red flag it had the data to withdraw, a "tautology-free cross-check" that is a tautology, and three different values for its own fair-value ceiling. Right answer, showing its work, with two of the steps wrong.

ONE-LINE VERDICT ON THE VERDICT

The memo has overcorrected — by roughly 25–40% on the band, on one Executive Summary headline, and on one forensic flag it should never have raised — but it has not overcorrected on the decision. WAIT, 0%, no long and no short, stands unchanged. Republish the band as $2.75–$4.50, delete the Apple sentence, withdraw the UTP flag, restate the 8% stress threshold as 14%, and stop pretending you know this business to the nearest 25 cents. And Lynch's BUY is dead: Munger's rebuttal is correct, and §280E cannot lift EBITDA at all.

ARCHIVE — PASS 2 (full report)

REALITY CHECK — RED TEAM, PASS 2 · TRULIEVE CANNABIS CORP. (NYSE: TRLV)

Date: 2026-08-26 · Memo under review: Munger Synthesis Revision 2, WAIT, trigger $2.75–$4.50, position 0% Price: $11.08 (24-Aug) / $11.32 [CALC] · Reviewers: The Ghost of Charlie Munger · The Living Warren Buffett Scope: the REWRITE only. Nothing pass 1 already corrected is re-litigated.

HEADLINE FINDING

FOUR FATAL findings, all of them inside the replacement reasoning. And — unlike pass 1 — they do not all run the same way.

Pass 1 found five errors and every one made the business look better. The memo took all five, moved the band up 35%, and kept the verdict. That is the pattern the parent asked me to be suspicious of, and the suspicion is warranted: the revision's error-correction process is not direction-neutral. Three times in this rewrite, a red-team finding forced a withdrawal and the memo immediately manufactured a same-or-worse replacement rather than accept a net reduction in the bear case:

Struck by pass 1 Substituted in Revision 2 Status after pass 2
"Killer cross-check, free of any tautology" "1.83x book … genuinely independent witness" New tautology. FATAL 1.
UTP $98M "unexplained" flag IRC §1502 several liability Legally true, predicate unestablished, over-promoted
$26M FCF annualization "H1 FCF $74M is CONSOLIDATED — full stop" Unquantified; when quantified it collapses. FATAL 3.

Three for three. A revision that replaces every withdrawn bear point with a fresh bear point of equal weight is not correcting; it is defending.

And the single most consequential finding runs the other way from every pass-1 correction. The memo's hurdle sensitivity table holds g = 0 in every cell — an assumption never stated anywhere in the document. Restore any positive growth and the memo's own 8% cell clears $11.08. The claim "at every rate from 8% to 18%, the answer is not at $11.08" is false.

My answer to the parent's hardest question (f), stated plainly: yes, $11.08 is defensible on a set of assumptions I would not call unreasonable. It requires a normal-taxpayer discount rate for a now-federally-legal NYSE-listed medical operator, maintenance capex at retail norms rather than the entire guided budget, and ~3% perpetual growth. Each is individually defensible; all three must hold at once. I still would not pay it. But the memo's assertion that no defensible build gets above $4.50 is wrong, and it is wrong because of an assumption the memo never wrote down.

The verdict I reach is the same as the memo's — WAIT, 0%. I reach it despite the memo's arithmetic, not because of it.

# Finding Grade Direction
1 "Independent witness" is stale, mis-stated, entity-mismatched and algebraically dependent on the discount rate FATAL Mixed
2 g = 0 smuggled into every cell of the sensitivity table; capex/growth pair internally contradictory FATAL Bull
3 New lead argument (consolidated FCF) asserted without sign or magnitude; quantified, it lands the "demolished" experts inside the memo's own band FATAL Bull
4 Headline Trigger Price does not follow from the memo's own division FATAL (headline only) Neutral
5–18 Fourteen non-fatal findings, seven bearish, five bullish, two neutral — Both

CHECK 0 — TAUTOLOGY GATE (run before anything else)

FATAL 1 — The "genuinely independent witness" fails on four counts, and one of them is the gate itself

The memo, §2.3:

> "The genuinely independent witness is the balance sheet and the return history. Book value is $6.07/share against $11.08 — 1.83x book … That test uses the balance sheet and historical returns, touches neither my forward earnings estimate nor the discount rate."

Four things are wrong with that sentence, and the fourth is a Check-0 violation of exactly the species pass 1 struck.

(i) The book value is one quarter stale. It is $3.98, not $6.07.

$6.07 comes from the FY2025 pipeline block — i.e. before the $403.3M deconsolidation loss booked in Q2'26. I rebuilt the current figure from the Q2'26 balance sheet in raw_verify.txt:

TOTAL ASSETS (30-Jun-26)                               $2,077.4M
Total current liabilities                                  95.1
Long-term:
  Notes payable, net                    89.4
  Private placement notes, net         195.8
  Operating lease liabilities           85.8
  Finance lease liabilities             59.8
  Construction finance liabilities     120.2
  Deferred tax liabilities              57.2
  Uncertain tax position liabilities   598.2
  Other long-term liabilities           10.0
  LT discontinued ops                    0.0
                                     ───────
                                      1,216.4              1,216.4
TOTAL LIABILITIES                                       $1,311.5M
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
TOTAL EQUITY                                              $765.9M
÷ 192.57M shares                              =            $3.98/share

Cross-check on 31-Dec-25 with the same method: assets $2,696.1M − liabilities $1,552.9M = $1,143.2M of equity. Divide by the FY2025 share count of 188.3M and you get $6.07 — to the cent. That is conclusive: the memo's $6.07 is the December-2025 number, and the December-2025 balance sheet still had Harvest in it.

Second cross-check: equity fell $1,143.2M → $765.9M = −$377.3M, against an H1'26 net loss attributable to common of −$403.6M plus roughly $26M of SBC and share issuance. It ties.

So the multiple is not 1.83x. It is $11.08 ÷ $3.98 = 2.79x (2.85x at $11.32). The memo understates the premium to book by 52%.

(Caveat I owe you: my equity figure is derived by subtraction and is total equity including any non-controlling interest. Confirm against the 10-Q equity statement. But the error bars do not save the sentence — even if I am off by $50M, BVPS is $3.72–$4.24 and P/B is 2.6x–3.0x. There is no version of this that gets back to 1.83x.)

(ii) The ROIC series is consolidated, §280E-burdened, and impairment-dominated — the exact entity mismatch the memo prosecutes twice

The five ROIC readings (−20.9%, 8.3%, 4.5%, −8.0%, 1.6%) come from the pipeline's FINANCIAL PHYSICS table, which the refined dossier labels "[CALC, consolidated basis]" and which the dossier's own STRUCTURAL DISCONTINUITY warning says describes an entity that no longer exists.

Worse: the same table carries the flag "🚨 HIGH TAX RATE: Effective rate 228%." Every one of those five years was earned under §280E — the tax regime that was repealed for this entity in April 2026. The memo is using a 228%-effective-tax-rate history as a witness about a 26%-effective-tax-rate future.

This is the third-generation commission of the error the memo convicts (a) Lynch of in §4, and (b) its own prior draft of in §3. It prosecutes the mismatch in the revenue line, prosecutes it in the EBITDA line, and then commits it in the return-on-capital line while calling the result "genuinely independent."

Two further defects in the series itself: the TTM −20.9% is dominated by the $403.3M non-cash deconsolidation write-off — an accounting event, not an operating return — and "five years on record" is really four fiscal years (2022–2025) plus a TTM window that overlaps 2025. It is four observations wearing five hats.

(iii) It is not independent of the discount rate. The discount rate is the whole sentence.

> "…its best year was 8.3% against a 13–18% hurdle."

Delete "13–18%" and the sentence says nothing. The claim that it "touches neither my forward earnings estimate nor the discount rate" is false on its face — the hurdle appears verbatim, and it is the only thing that turns 8.3% into a verdict.

(iv) And it is the memo's own valuation wearing a percent sign — a CHECK-0 VIOLATION

Derive it symbolically. The witness's implicit claim is the no-growth residual-income identity:

Warranted P/B  =  ROIC / r          →     V_witness  =  B × ROIC / r
Memo&#x27;s build:                             V_memo     =  OE / r
Ratio:         V_witness / V_memo   =  (B × ROIC) / OE        ← r CANCELS

The discount rate falls out of the comparison entirely. Two witnesses that agree no matter what r is are not two witnesses. And since B × ROIC ≈ E by construction, B × ROIC / r ≡ E / r — the witness is the memo's own capitalization with a trailing earnings number substituted for a forward one. One witness, two hats.

The numerical tell is decisive:

On the STALE book:      $1,143.2M × 8.3%  =  $94.9M   ≈  the memo&#x27;s $100M forward OE

The "independent" witness agrees with the earnings estimate to within 5% because the algebra forces it to. That is not corroboration; it is the same computation.

(v) And on the CORRECTED book, the witness reverses on the memo

Run it properly, on $765.9M of actual current equity:

Witness&#x27;s implied earnings:   $765.9M × 8.3%  =  $63.6M
Memo&#x27;s forward estimate:                          $100M

They now disagree by 57%. The memo cannot claim corroboration from a witness that only agrees when you feed it a superseded balance sheet.

And read the other way, the correction destroys the sentence's punchline:

Forward owner earnings $100M ÷ current book equity $765.9M  =  13.1%

The go-forward business earns 13.1% on its post-write-down book — which clears the bottom of the memo's own 13–18% hurdle. The sentence "It has never once earned its cost of capital" is a statement about a consolidated, §280E-burdened, pre-write-off entity. On the entity the memo is actually valuing, using the memo's own earnings number, it is not true.

Verdict on FATAL 1: strike the witness entirely. It is stale by one quarter, computed on the wrong entity under a repealed tax regime, explicitly dependent on the discount rate it claims not to touch, algebraically identical to the memo's own valuation, and self-contradictory once the balance sheet is updated. Pass 1 struck one tautology; the rewrite installed another in the same slot, with a bigger claim attached to it.

FATAL 2 — Assumption smuggling: g = 0 in every cell. The sensitivity table proves nothing.

Check 0 is explicit: "when a sensitivity varies one input, verify every other input was not held at a value that determines the answer."

The memo's table:

Hurdle 18% 15% 13% 10% 8%
Fair value/share @ OE $100M $2.73 $3.29 $4.05 $5.11 $6.35

> "At every rate from 8% to 18%, the answer is 'not at $11.08.' The verdict is robust; the number is not."

Every cell holds g = 0. The formula V = OE/r is a perpetuity with zero nominal growth forever — below inflation, i.e. a business shrinking ~2%/yr in real terms until the end of time. That assumption is never stated in the memo. It is not in §2.2, not in §2.3, not in the sensitivity table's caption, not in the Executive Summary. It is smuggled inside a division sign.

And it is not a neutral default. The dossier explicitly warns: "any expert who models positive growth must say explicitly why the trailing ROE is not the right base and what replaces it." The memo models zero growth and says nothing at all — the disclosure obligation runs in both directions.

Restore growth to the memo's own table, its own $100M, its own Harvest and net-debt figures:

g = 0 g = 2% g = 3% g = 3.4%
r = 13% $4.05 $4.68 $5.32 $5.63
r = 10% $5.11 $6.44 $7.64 $8.30
r = 8% $6.35 $8.61 $10.33 $11.21

The memo's own 8% cell clears $11.08 at g = 3.4%. The headline sentence "at every rate from 8% to 18%, the answer is not at $11.08" is arithmetically false, and it is false because of an unstated input, not a disputed one. This is precisely the KSPI failure the Check-0 gate was written to catch, reproduced in the section that publishes a sensitivity table in response to the gate.

The capex/growth pair is also internally contradictory — a double penalty of ~$1.00/share

The memo charges $85M of maintenance capex against a medical-only D&A base I compute at ~$77M (derivation in Non-Fatal 5). Capex above depreciation means the asset base is growing. The memo then credits zero growth.

You may charge growth capex and credit the growth it buys. You may charge maintenance capex and credit no growth. You may not charge growth capex and credit no growth. The excess is roughly $85M − $77M ≈ $8M of pure growth spend, and on a broader reading of what "maintenance" means for a flat-store-count retailer (see Non-Fatal 7) it is $25–40M. Capitalized at 13%, $25M/yr of investment charged at zero return destroys ~$192M of value, or ~$1.00/share, by assumption alone.

Note also that the memo's own §0 evidence contradicts g = 0: medical-only revenue is +4% sequential (~17% annualized), traffic +6%, units +8%, the Florida registry stands at 938,980 patients and growing, and Georgia SB 220 (signed 12-May-2026) just added flower and vape to a market Trulieve already cultivates in. There is a real bear argument for g ≤ 0 — 28 operators becoming ~50 — but it has to be made and priced, not buried in a division.

FATAL 3 — The new lead argument is asserted, never quantified; quantified, it collapses

This is the replacement for the withdrawn $26M annualization, and it is the memo's opening move — the thing that "changes what the memo is about."

> "H1'26 free cash flow of $74M is CONSOLIDATED. Harvest sat inside the group for five of the six months. Annualizing it to describe a medical-only entity is an entity mismatch, full stop."

The mismatch is real. The inference drawn from it is not established, and the memo never establishes the one thing that matters: the sign.

Harvest is the adult-use business. It remained Schedule I, it remained subject to §280E disallowance, and it was consuming capex in expansion markets. Removing a §280E-burdened, capital-hungry segment from a consolidated cash-flow figure can perfectly well raise the remaining entity's cash-flow rate. The memo asserts the correction runs downward and moves straight on. "Full stop" is doing work that arithmetic should be doing.

So I did the arithmetic. The record contains exactly the datapoints needed:

Q1&#x27;26 (Harvest inside, full quarter):   revenue $287M,  OCF $56.0M,  capex $14M,  FCF $42M
Q2&#x27;26 (Harvest inside ~2 of 3 months):  revenue $271M,  OCF $53.1M,  capex $21M,  FCF $32M

Harvest revenue lost between the quarters:
    Q1 Harvest (full qtr)     ≈ $73.5M      [memo&#x27;s own gross-up]
    Q2 Harvest (2-month stub) =  $49.0M     [271.0 − 222.0]
    Δ Harvest revenue         = −$24.5M
    Δ medical revenue         = +$8.5M      [213.5 → 222.0]
    Δ total                   = −$16.0M     ✓ ties to $287M → $271M

Δ operating cash flow for that same period: $56.0M → $53.1M  =  −$2.9M

Losing $24.5M of Harvest revenue cost $2.9M of operating cash flow. Harvest's marginal cash contribution margin is ≤12%, against a medical business running a 63% gross margin. Scale it:

Harvest annualized revenue     ≈ $294M
Harvest annualized OCF         ≈ $35M          (at ≤12%)
Harvest&#x27;s share of H1&#x27;26 FCF   ≈ 5/6 × $17.5M ≈ $14.6M
Medical-only H1&#x27;26 FCF         ≈ $74M − $14.6M = $59M
Medical-only annualized        ≈ $118M         (the three experts used $148M)

The entity mismatch is worth about 20%, not "full stop." And now correct the three experts for both errors the memo names them for — the entity basis and their failure to deduct net debt or credit Harvest:

$118M ÷ 18% = $656M  + Harvest $130M − net debt $155M = $631M → $3.28/share
$118M ÷ 13% = $908M  + Harvest $130M − net debt $155M = $883M → $4.58/share

$3.28–$4.58. The memo's own band is $2.75–$4.50. The three experts the memo demolishes in its opening section — the demolition that "reframes the whole council" — land inside the memo's own range once their error is properly quantified. They were not wrong about the answer. They were sloppy about the route.

And $118M sits at the top of the memo's own $95–115M range, which is corroboration the memo could have claimed and instead threw away in order to keep the demolition.

Why this is FATAL: §0 is the memo's structural argument, this is its load-bearing sentence, it replaced a struck one, and it has never been checked. A sentence ending in "full stop" that turns out to be worth 20% and to confirm rather than destroy the thing it attacks cannot stand as the lead.

FATAL 4 — The headline Trigger Price is not what the memo's own arithmetic produces

The memo, §2.2, computes and then converts:

> "Equity value: $498M–$895M = $2.75–$4.50/share"

$498M ÷ 192.57M = $2.586        the memo prints $2.75      (+6.3%)
$895M ÷ 192.57M = $4.647        the memo prints $4.50      (−3.2%)

Neither end follows, and the two errors run in opposite directions, so it is not a rounding convention. Meanwhile the hurdle sensitivity table on the very next page produces a third band — $2.73–$4.05 at OE $100M. Three fair-value ranges on two pages:

Location Band
§2.2, computed from $498M–$895M $2.59–$4.65
§2.2 / §5 / Executive Summary, as printed $2.75–$4.50
§2.2 sensitivity table @ OE $100M, 18%→13% $2.73–$4.05

The one that reaches the Trigger Price, the Executive Summary and the Thesis-in-One-Sentence matches neither computation. It matches pass 1's recommendation — the memo appears to have adopted the red team's headline figure without re-deriving it, then published its own inconsistent build underneath.

Pass 1 raised this exact defect as its FINDING 6 ("three different numbers for the same thing"). The rewrite reproduced it with new numbers. Everything downstream inherits the error: the settlement-adjusted band ($1.24–$2.99 = printed band − $1.51) and the operating-lease alternative ($2.30–$4.05 = printed band − $0.45) are both anchored to a figure that does not exist in the arithmetic.

It does not change the verdict — nothing at this scale could, against $11.08. It is FATAL as a headline: a memo whose entire claim to authority is arithmetic discipline may not publish a trigger price that its own division does not produce.

NON-FATAL FINDINGS

Seven run bearish, five bullish, two neutral. I am listing them in both directions deliberately, because pass 1 ran one way and that is exactly what should make a second pass suspicious.

Bearish — the revision was too generous here

5. Medical-only D&A is ~$77M, not the consolidated $103M — the tax shield is overstated. The memo's tax line is 26% × (254 − 103 D&amp;A − 35 int) = $30M. The $103M is Q2'26 D&A of $25.7M annualized — a consolidated figure covering a quarter with Harvest inside for two of three months, and Harvest took $476.3M of intangibles and $158.3M of goodwill with it (50% of the combined pre-deconsolidation balance). Solving for the medical-only rate:

Reported Q2 D&amp;A = medical + (2/3 × Harvest).  Harvest ≈ 33% of full-perimeter D&amp;A.
25.7 = 0.67D + 0.22D = 0.89D  →  D = $28.9M/qtr full perimeter
Medical-only ≈ 0.67 × 28.9 × 4 ≈ $77M/yr

Corrected: 26% × (254 − 78 − 35) = $36.7M, and owner earnings fall from $104M to $97.3M. Worth ~$0.25/share.

6. The $305M medical-only SG&A is a $29M/yr gift with no disclosure basis. Consolidated Q2'26 SG&A ratio is 101.8 / 271.0 = 37.6%. The memo assumes medical-only runs at 34.3% — 3.3 points better. At the consolidated ratio, SG&A is $334M, EBITDA is $225M and owner earnings drop to ~$76M, a ~15% cut to the band. The memo does flag that this line has "no disclosure basis" — credit for that — but it does not flag that its chosen value is the favourable end of the range, nor show the sensitivity. Given that medical revenue is 82% of consolidated revenue while it is allocated only 75% of consolidated SG&A, the direction of the assumption should have been named.

7. Net debt still omits ~$15M of current portions — a pass-1 finding the rewrite silently dropped. Pass 1: "excludes $85.8M of operating leases, and excludes ~$26M of current portions." Revision 2 wrote a methodological note about the operating leases and said nothing at all about the current portions.

89.4 + 4.2 + 195.8 + 120.2 + 0.6 + 59.8 + 10.1 = $480.1M gross
less cash $325.4M                              = $154.7M net debt   (memo: $140M)

$0.08/share. Trivial in size; not trivial that a correction was neither adopted nor refused.

8. Harvest is valued off the very frame the memo spends a page rejecting. Harvest enters the band at $110–150M, being the $188.463M carrying value less a 20–30% haircut. But the 10-Q states that carrying value was "determined using a combination of the income approach and market approach, including consideration of discounted cash flow analyses, market multiples of comparable publicly traded companies." The memo rejects EV/EBITDA for the business it controls — "He is not wrong about the multiple. He is wrong about the metric" — and then accepts, at 70–80 cents on the dollar, a management mark built partly on that same metric, for a business it does not control, that is losing money (−$3.3M in a stub period), that remains Schedule I and §280E-burdened, and that it cannot force to distribute or sell. Apply the memo's own owner-earnings discipline to Harvest and the credit is materially lower. Worth $0.25–$0.50/share.

9. Roughly half the Q2'26 uncertain-tax accrual is unexplained, in a mostly-post-relief quarter — and the 26% cash tax rate has no disclosure basis. The memo: "$15.2M and rising of it is interest on the legacy dispute." $15.2M is the Q1 figure. Applied to Q2'26's $32.9M accrual, even generously grossed up, it leaves ~$17M of new uncertain tax position in a quarter that was ~77% post-relief for medical activity. Part is surely Harvest (still Schedule I, present for two of three months) — but the memo never says so, and never reconciles the balance. The dossier is explicit: "The FY2026 tax rate under Schedule III has not been disclosed in the retrieved record." The memo flags the SG&A assumption as unsupported and does not flag the tax rate, which is equally unsupported and equally load-bearing. At a 40% effective cash rate, owner earnings fall to ~$88M and the band to ~$2.4–$4.0.

10. The stress test uses $95M of capex while the valuation uses $85M — and it is still pre-tax after pass 1 said so. Same memo, same page-and-a-half apart, two different capex numbers for the same business, immediately after a section establishing why the distinction matters. And pass 1's FINDING 7 noted the buffer is pre-tax while the valuation applies a 26% cash tax; the rewrite republished it pre-tax. Post-tax the buffer is ~$94M and the thresholds become ~21% (not 27%) and ~7.5% (not 14%) under the installment plan.

11. The "$0.41–$2.16" full-crystallization band implies a deduction that appears nowhere. It requires subtracting $2.34/share = $451M. The memo elsewhere names $598M of face, a ~$290M settlement PV, and a "~$273M shortfall." $451M is none of these and is never derived. It is in the reader-facing conclusion.

Bullish — the revision may still be too harsh here

12. $85M is not a maintenance capex figure, and the derivation does not support calling it one. The logic is: guided FY2026 capex of $95M, less a $10M Georgia/Texas growth increment, equals $85M of maintenance. That inference requires the pre-existing $85M budget to be 100% maintenance — an assumption, not a derivation. It is almost certainly false: Trulieve is building out Georgia following SB 220 and ramping production in emerging medical markets, and that spending sits inside the base, not only in the increment.

Two independent sanity checks say $85M is too high: - $85M is 9.6% of revenue. Retail maintenance capex runs 2–3%; cultivation adds several points. 9.6% is a growth budget. - $85M exceeds medical-only D&A of ~$77M for a business that just shed a third of its asset base and holds a flat store count. Maintenance capex above depreciation on a shrinking asset base is a contradiction in terms.

At $55M of maintenance capex — still well above H1'26's actual medical-only run rate — owner earnings are $127M and the band moves to roughly $3.4–$5.6. This is a $0.70–$1.50/share understatement, and it is the same kind of error pass 1 found five of.

13. The $10M growth split rests on a tweet, and the tweet contains a claim nothing else in the record supports. Source: Finsee on X, tagged [MEDIA] … social-media source, unverified against the filing. It states the increment is "primarily ramping up production and retail footprints in Georgia and Texas." Texas appears nowhere else in this record. The FY2025 10-K footprint lists nine states and Texas is not among them; the post-restructuring footprint is Florida, Georgia, Pennsylvania and West Virginia. A summary asserting a Texas buildout for a company with no disclosed Texas presence is not a source you hang $10M of a valuation input on. Combined with finding 12, the entire $95M → $85M derivation is unsupported at both ends.

14. Gross interest is charged while cash is netted — ~$11M/yr of interest income is dropped. The memo deducts "cash interest on real debt −$35M" (7.5% on $465M of gross debt) and nets the full $325.4M of cash against that debt. The 10-Q reports "Interest income for the six months ended June 30, 2026 was $5.7 million" — ~$11M/yr. You may net the cash and charge net interest, or hold the cash separately and charge gross. Doing both charges the cash twice. Worth ~$8M after tax, ~$0.30/share.

15. The cost of equity is itself an entity mismatch — the fourth instance, and the largest. The dossier sets the 13–18% hurdle with an explicit justification: "a sector premium for an industry that until April 2026 had no federal legality for any activity, no NYSE/NASDAQ access, restricted banking, no bankruptcy protection, and retains Schedule I status for all adult-use activity."

Check each against the entity the memo is valuing:

Justification Status for the retained medical entity
No federal legality Removed — Schedule III, 22-Apr-2026
No NYSE/NASDAQ access Removed — NYSE listed, 10-Jun-2026
Restricted banking Removed — follows Schedule III
No bankruptcy protection Removed — follows federal legality
Schedule I adult-use Deconsolidated into Harvest, 3-Jun-2026

Every single justification for the sector premium was removed from this entity within seven weeks. The dossier's cost-of-equity paragraph was written on the pre-restructuring premise, and its own STRUCTURAL DISCONTINUITY banner says trailing anchors must not be carried across that line. The memo carries the discount rate across it without comment — while prosecuting three other people for carrying numbers across the same line.

I am not saying 13–18% is wrong. Cannabis retains real risks the rate should price: a single-state concentration, a licence-board moat with a statutory dissolution formula, a $598M disputed government claim, and no adult-use optionality. But the memo owes an argument for why the rate survives when its stated basis did not, and it does not give one. A 10–12% rate is defensible for a federally-legal, NYSE-listed, 63%-gross-margin medical operator, and at 10% with g = 3% the memo's own $104M produces $7.64/share.

16. "Prove $280M+ sustained and the verdict flips; nothing else does" is contradicted by the memo's own table two pages earlier. Load-Bearing Factor #1 in the Executive Summary. But the memo's own sensitivity table moves fair value from $2.73 to $6.35 — a 2.3x swing — without touching earnings at all, purely by moving the hurdle. "Nothing else does" is false in the memo's own document.

Neutral

17. The IRC §1502 finding is legally correct in the abstract but rests on two unstated predicates. Treas. Reg. §1.1502-6(a) does impose several liability on each member of a consolidated group. But Trulieve Cannabis Corp. is a British Columbia corporation, and §1504(b)(3) excludes foreign corporations from an affiliated group — so the US consolidated group, if any, is headed by a US subsidiary. And Harvest Enterprises, LLC is an LLC: absent a check-the-box election it is a disregarded entity or a partnership, not a consolidated-return member at all. Either predicate failing collapses the point, and neither is in the record.

Note the trap the argument sets for itself: if Harvest was disregarded, its §280E exposure was always Trulieve's directly and §1502 adds nothing; if Harvest was separately taxed, §1502 does not apply. The memo's honest "Verify in the 10-Q contingencies note" is the right instinct — but it then promotes the unverified conjecture to Load-Bearing Factor #3 in the Executive Summary. A factor cannot be load-bearing and unverified at the same time.

18. The declined operating-lease correction: right conclusion, wrong reasoning, wrong number. The memo: > "Post-ASC 842 the rent is already expensed inside SG&A, so capitalizing the liability and charging the rent is the same species of double-count as the SBC error I just fixed."

The conclusion is defensible. The reasoning is a strawman. Nobody proposes capitalizing the liability while keeping the rent. The standard adjustment is a reclassification: add the lease liability to net debt, add the rent expense back to EBITDA, and replace it with imputed interest plus ROU amortization. That is not a double charge, and calling it one dispatches the correction without engaging it. The SBC analogy is wrong — SBC was genuinely the same line counted twice; this is one line moved between two places.

Done properly on ~$97M of liabilities at ~7.5%: rent addback ≈ +$20M, imputed interest ≈ −$7M, ROU amortization ≈ −$13M — roughly a wash on owner earnings, and net debt rises $97M. So the haircut survives the proper treatment; the memo's conclusion is only right by accident.

The memo is also internally consistent in a way worth crediting: it capitalizes finance leases and construction finance (which carry separately stated interest and are not in SG&A) and expenses operating leases (which are). That is a coherent rule. But the amount is wrong: operating lease liabilities are $85.8M long-term PLUS $11.3M current = $97.1M, so the alternative haircut is $0.50/share, not $0.45, and the alternative band should read $2.25–$4.00 on the printed base — or $2.09–$4.15 on the base the arithmetic actually produces.

WHAT I CHECKED AND FOUND CORRECT

A red team that only reports failures is not a red team. These held under attack:

  • The SBC correction is properly taken. The expense reconciliation ties across all four periods. No residual double-count.
  • The depreciation-in-COGS check is correct and correctly reasoned. Cash-flow D&A equals the income-statement line, so gross profit is genuinely pre-depreciation and $254M is a true EBITDA, not a mislabeled EBIT. The memo tested a hypothesis that would have hurt its own case and reported the negative result. That is the right instinct and it is rare.
  • Interest is not double-counted between the tax line and the owner-earnings line. 26% × (254 − D&amp;A − 35) then − $35M is the standard build: interest shields tax, then interest is paid. One deduction of principle, two appearances. Correct.
  • §163(j) does not bite. EBIT of ~$176M × 30% = $53M of headroom against $35M of interest. No limitation.
  • The stress-test drag arithmetic is right. 0.63 − (0.343 × 0.35) = $0.51/revenue dollar; $453M at risk; 124/453 = 27%; (124−60)/453 = 14%. All check.
  • The Buffett back-solve is exact. $2,180M × 13% / 18% = $283M / $392M. Pass 1 verified it; it survives.
  • "§280E is a tax provision, EBITDA is before tax" is correct, decisive against Lynch's bridge, and was the best single sentence in either draft.
  • The settlement-adjusted arithmetic is internally consistent ($290M ÷ 192.57M = $1.51; band − $1.51 = $1.24–$2.99) — it just inherits a base that does not follow from §2.2.
  • Publishing the peer table in both directions (6.8x and 8.7x) with the [MEDIA]/undated caveat is the correct response to pass 1 and is done properly.

POINTING THE GATE IN BOTH DIRECTIONS (parent question f) — MANDATORY

Has the revision STILL overcorrected in the bear direction? Yes.

Findings 12, 13, 14, 15 and 16 all run the same way pass 1's five did — the memo is harsher on this business than its own inputs justify. Stack only the defensible ones:

Adjustment Owner earnings Rationale
Memo's published build $104M —
Maintenance capex $55M not $85M $134M Capex > D&A on a shrinking asset base is not maintenance
Add back interest income $142M Cash netted twice
Correct the tax base (D&A $77M) $135M Bearish offset, taken

At $135M of owner earnings, a 10% hurdle (every stated basis for the sector premium removed from this entity) and 3% growth (registry growing, +4% sequential, Georgia expansion):

$135M ÷ (0.10 − 0.03) = $1,929M  + Harvest $150M − net debt $155M = $1,924M = $9.99/share

Push each input to the far edge of defensible — SG&A at $280M (31.5% of revenue), maintenance capex at 4% of revenue:

$146M ÷ 0.07 = $2,086M + $150M − $155M = $2,081M = $10.81/share

So: yes. $11.08 is defensible on a reasonable set of assumptions. Not on one heroic assumption — on three defensible ones that must all hold simultaneously. I say this plainly because the mandate requires it and because the memo's contrary claim — "$11.08 is unjustified at both ends" — is not supported by its own inputs once g is written down.

Was the 35% upward move itself too generous? Partly.

Findings 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10 and the FATAL-1 book-value correction all run the other way. Stack those:

Adjustment Effect
SG&A at the consolidated 37.6% ratio −$21M owner earnings, −15% band
Tax base corrected for medical-only D&A −$7M
Net debt incl. current portions + operating leases at $97.1M −$0.58/share
Harvest on owner-earnings discipline rather than a multiple-derived mark −$0.25 to −$0.50/share
P/B is 2.79x, not 1.83x Qualitative; the stock is far more expensive on assets than published

That build lands near $2.10–$3.40 — below the published band. So no, the corrections do not run uniformly one way. Pass 1's did. Pass 2's do not.

The honest conclusion is that the band is not two significant figures wide, it is one. The defensible range on the record as it stands runs roughly $2 to $10 depending on three inputs the record does not settle: medical-only SG&A (never disclosed), maintenance capex (never disclosed, and the only source for the split is a tweet), and the discount rate (whose stated basis has been repealed for this entity). The memo's own words — "a range of ignorance, not a range of estimate" — are exactly right and the memo does not live by them. A range of ignorance that spans $1.75 while the underlying uncertainty spans $8 is not a range of ignorance; it is a range of estimate with a disclaimer stapled to it.

THE VERDICT'S SENSITIVITY TO ITS OWN INPUTS (parent question g)

Is surviving a 35% arithmetic move robustness, or insensitivity?

Insensitivity, and the memo mistakes one for the other. A 35% move inside a claimed 250–400% gap cannot flip anything — the test had no power. The memo's formulation "the verdict is robust; the number is not" is backwards on the dimension that matters: the verdict is robust to owner earnings (which is what pass 1 moved) and not robust to the (r, g) pair (which nobody has moved, because g was never written down).

What input value flips the verdict to BUY, and is it in the plausible range?

The memo says: "Prove $280M+ sustained and the verdict flips; nothing else does." That is true only at r − g = 13%. Solve properly:

Value required to justify $11.08 = $11.08 × 192.57 + net debt $155M − Harvest $130M = $2,159M

  r − g = 13%   →  owner earnings needed = $279M    (2.7x the estimate — implausible)
  r − g = 10%   →  owner earnings needed = $215M    (2.1x — implausible)
  r − g =  7%   →  owner earnings needed = $150M    (1.4x — REACHABLE)

The flip point is not a single input. It is the triple (r, g, maintenance capex), and it is r − g ≈ 7% with owner earnings ≈ $150M.

Is that within the plausible range? Component by component:

Input Memo Flip value Plausible?
Discount rate r 13–18% 10% Yes — every stated basis for the sector premium was removed from this entity in seven weeks
Growth g 0% (unstated) 3% Yes — +4% sequential, registry at 939k and growing, Georgia SB 220
Maintenance capex $85M ~$50M Yes — below medical-only D&A of $77M, above H1 actual
Medical-only SG&A $305M ≤$290M Marginal — never disclosed; memo's own figure is already the favourable end
Owner earnings $104M ~$150M Only via the three above — not directly

Four of five flip values are individually plausible. Requiring all four simultaneously is what makes the refusal defensible. That is a materially weaker statement than the memo's, and it is the true one.

The correct formulation of the decision is not "the stock is 2.5–4x fair value." It is: the price requires four favourable readings of four undisclosed inputs to hold at once, and I will not underwrite that on one partial quarter of medical-only data with no SG&A line beneath the gross margin. That reaches WAIT/0% honestly. The memo reaches WAIT/0% by asserting a precision it does not have.

THE REAL CHARLIE MUNGER'S TAKE

Somebody handed this fellow a list of five mistakes, he fixed all five, and then he went and built a brand-new one in the space each of them vacated. That is not error correction. That is a man rearranging the furniture so the room looks the same after you move the sofa out.

Look at what he did with the tautology. We told him his "killer cross-check free of any tautology" was his own valuation wearing a percent sign. He took it out — good — and in the same paragraph installed a new one and called it a "genuinely independent witness." Genuinely. When a man reaches for an adverb to hold up a claim, check the claim. I checked it. It's stale by a quarter — he's quoting a book value from before the company wrote off four hundred million dollars — the multiple isn't 1.83 times, it's 2.79 times, and the return history he's convicting them with was earned under a tax code that no longer applies to the company he's valuing. Then he says it "touches neither my forward earnings estimate nor the discount rate" while the discount rate is sitting right there in the sentence, in the same clause, doing all the work. Divide his witness by his valuation and the discount rate cancels out. Two things that agree no matter what you assume are not two things.

And here's the part that ought to embarrass him: fix the book value and his own forward number earns 13.1% on it. He wrote "it has never once earned its cost of capital" about a business that, on his own arithmetic, clears the bottom of his own hurdle. He didn't catch it because he never updated the balance sheet.

Now the one that actually matters, and I want the council to sit with it. He published a sensitivity table — because we told him to — running the hurdle from 8% to 18% and announcing that the answer never changes. Every cell in that table assumes this business never grows again. Ever. Not one penny, forever, in nominal dollars. He never says so. It's hidden inside a division sign. Put three percent growth into his own 8% column — three percent, on a business whose patient registry is growing and whose own medical revenue was up four percent in a quarter — and his fair value is $10.33. At three-point-four percent it's $11.21 and the stock is cheap.

That is the exact trick this gate exists to catch, and he did it in the section he wrote to satisfy the gate. Varying one input while pinning the one that determines the answer isn't a sensitivity analysis. It's a magic trick with the card already up your sleeve.

He charges eighty-five million of capital expenditure against a business depreciating seventy-seven and then credits it zero growth. Pick one, sir. Either that money buys something or you shouldn't be spending it. Charging growth capital and crediting no growth is a way of losing a dollar a share by assumption and calling it conservatism. Conservatism is refusing to pay up. It is not doing the arithmetic wrong in a direction you find agreeable.

The opening argument — the one he replaced the twenty-six-million business with — is asserted and never computed. "H1 free cash flow is consolidated, full stop." Full stop. I did the stop-and-compute. Harvest left twenty-four and a half million of quarterly revenue and took two point nine million of operating cash flow with it. Twelve cents on the dollar, against a medical business running sixty-three. So the correction is worth about twenty percent, not "full stop," and the three experts he demolished with it land at $3.28 to $4.58 — which is inside his own band. He had corroboration and he threw it away because he wanted a demolition.

And then he prints a trigger price his own division doesn't produce. Four ninety-eight to eight ninety-five over a hundred ninety-two point five seven is $2.59 to $4.65. He prints $2.75 to $4.50 — wrong at both ends, in opposite directions, so it isn't even rounding — and a table on the next page says $2.73 to $4.05. Three answers, one page, and the one that reaches the summary is the one the red team suggested. He adopted our headline without re-deriving it. We told him last time he had three numbers for the same thing. He now has three different three numbers.

The standing tests, since they're mine:

  • Rat poison. Charged once, correctly, and the dilution kept separately at $34M. Fixed properly. No complaint.
  • Bullshit earnings. He refuses EBITDA with a checkable reason and confirmed the frame is clean by testing a hypothesis against his own interest and reporting the negative. That is the best work in either draft and I'll say so.
  • Pricing power. Still absent. Sixty, fifty and forty percent off your first three visits is not a franchise; it is a coupon with a licence attached. Three years of falling flower prices. Twenty-four percent of operators making money. A statute that mints four new competitors per hundred thousand patients, with twenty-two queued.
  • Too hard. Yes. And unlike him I'd say so without needing three decimal places to justify it.

The destination is right. He got there by a road with four new potholes in it, and he dug two of them himself while filling in the old ones.

THE REAL WARREN BUFFETT'S TAKE

Charlie's found the arithmetic. Let me ask the question nobody in this building has asked, which is what discount rate we're entitled to use.

The dossier tells us thirteen to eighteen percent, and it tells us why: an industry with no federal legality, no exchange listing, restricted banking, no bankruptcy protection, and Schedule I status. Five reasons. Now go through them one at a time for the company we're actually looking at. Medical cannabis became Schedule III on the twenty-second of April. The company listed on the New York Stock Exchange on the tenth of June. Banking access follows the schedule. So does bankruptcy protection. And the Schedule I business — the adult-use business — was moved into Harvest and deconsolidated on the third of June.

All five reasons were removed from this entity inside of seven weeks. The memo carries the discount rate across that line anyway, in a document that prosecutes three different people for carrying other numbers across the very same line. I don't say thirteen to eighteen is wrong. There's plenty left to worry about — one state, one licence board that can un-grant what it granted, and a six-hundred-million-dollar argument with the government. But you owe the reader a sentence explaining why the price of risk survived when every stated reason for it didn't, and there is no such sentence.

At ten percent — still a rich premium for a business earning sixty-three points of gross margin — and three percent growth, this thing is worth about seven and a half dollars on the memo's own earnings, and around ten on a capital expenditure figure I think is closer to the truth. So I have to tell you honestly: eleven dollars is defensible. Not obviously right. Defensible. And the memo says it is unjustified at both ends of a binary, which is a stronger claim than the memo's own inputs will carry.

Here's what I'd actually say to a partner, and it's a different sentence than the one in the memo.

The problem isn't that it's worth three dollars. The problem is that I can't tell you what it's worth to within a factor of four. Nobody has ever published a medical-only selling and administrative expense line for this company. Not once. The valuation pivots on that number, and the memo picks a value three points better than the consolidated ratio and calls it an allocation. The maintenance capital expenditure figure — the number that decides whether the owner earns a hundred million or a hundred and forty — traces to somebody's summary of an earnings call posted on a social network, and that summary names Texas, a state this company does not operate in on any page of this record. That is the evidentiary foundation of a load-bearing input. And the discount rate's justification was repealed by an act of the Justice Department in April.

Three inputs. None disclosed. Each worth two to three dollars a share. That's a range of two to ten, and I don't care that both ends are integers — the width is the finding.

On Rule Number One. You don't lose money by refusing something that turns out to be worth eleven dollars. You lose money by paying eleven dollars for something whose selling expenses have never been disclosed, whose largest liability is an argument with the Internal Revenue Service worth twenty-eight percent of the market value, whose cash cushion arrived by borrowing fifty-nine million dollars, and whose competitive protection is a count of licences the Florida legislature has published a formula for doubling. Twenty-eight operators becoming fifty. That formula is the whole moat, and it's written in the same statute that created it.

And the chief executive has bought zero shares. Seven sales, one point seven million shares, fourteen and a half million dollars, and not one purchase at any price. When you can't value a business, you look at what the people who can value it are doing with their own money. They're selling it.

Would I buy it? No — and now for a better reason than the memo gives. Not because it's worth three dollars. Because I cannot tell you whether it's worth three or ten, and a man who can't tell the difference has no business bidding on either.

Would I short it? No. Charlie's right that a live rescheduling hearing on somebody else's clock is not a position, it's a wager. And after this pass I'd add: you should never short something you've just proved you can value four different ways.

Too hard? Yes. But I want the file to say why it's too hard, because the memo has it wrong. It isn't too hard because of the Internal Revenue Service. The memo is right that the tax fight isn't the load-bearing variable, and that was its best insight in both drafts. It's too hard because the three numbers that decide the answer have never been printed by anybody, and one of them was sourced from a tweet.

THE "OLD SCHOOL" FINAL SCORECARD

  • Circle of Competence: OUT — and further out than pass 1 said. Not merely because the tax regime, corporate structure and listing venue were rewritten in seven weeks, but because the discount rate's own justification was rewritten in the same seven weeks and nobody adjusted it. When the price of risk is as unsettled as the cash flows, you are not in the circle; you are describing its circumference.

  • Moat Integrity: NARROW AND ON A LEGISLATED CLOCK. Unchanged, and unchallenged by anything in this pass. §381.986 Fla. Stat. mandates four new MMTCs per 100,000 new patients; 22 are queued; 28 operators become ~50. The scale evidence is genuine and it is a cost position, not pricing power — 60/50/40% off the first three visits settles that. Add the Ohio Attorney General's antitrust action naming Trulieve among nine MSOs, which attacks precisely the shelf access scale was supposed to buy.

  • Management Character: PROMOTERS. Unchanged. $177M of shareholder money into two failed ballot initiatives. "Adjusted EBITDA" running ~$97M/yr above GAAP on charges that recur annually. Reporting on a "medical-only" basis that has a revenue line and a gross margin and no cost line beneath it, ever. CEO: 0 purchases, 7 sales, 1,699,007 shares, ~$14.7M. Against that, the Harvest deconsolidation genuinely solved a listing problem no MSO had solved — clever is not aligned.

  • Berkshire Buy? NO — and now for a sharper reason. Not "it's worth $3 and costs $11." "I cannot tell you whether it is worth $3 or $10, and neither can the memo." That is the disqualifying fact, and it is a stronger refusal than the one the memo publishes because it does not depend on a band I have just shown does not follow from its own arithmetic.

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the SECURITY: F. Unchanged. No earnings, no dividend, no carry, 1.6%/yr dilution, 2.79x book (not 1.83x), largest liability disputed and >25% of market cap, three load-bearing inputs never disclosed. Margin of safety is not thin; it is unmeasurable.

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the MEMO'S WORK: C+. Down from pass 1's B, and the downgrade is deliberate. Revision 1 made honest mistakes in a defensible frame. Revision 2 made mistakes while being watched — it replaced a struck tautology with a new one, published a sensitivity table with the deciding variable pinned at an undisclosed value, adopted the red team's headline number without re-deriving it, dropped one pass-1 correction silently, republished another uncorrected, and quoted a stale balance sheet as its "genuinely independent" witness. The good work is still good: the SBC fix is clean, the depreciation-in-COGS test was run against its own interest and reported honestly, the §280E-cannot-lift-EBITDA point is decisive, and the insight that the IRS is not the load-bearing variable survives everything. Right destination. Second trip down the same road, and it acquired four new potholes while filling in five.
