ONE-LINE VERDICT ON THE VERDICT

Four FATAL findings, every one of them inside the rewrite: a "genuinely independent witness" that is a stale balance sheet, a repealed tax regime and the memo's own valuation in a hat; a sensitivity table with the deciding variable pinned at zero and never disclosed, which makes the headline robustness claim arithmetically false; a lead argument asserted "full stop" that is worth 20% and lands the experts it demolishes inside the memo's own band; and a printed Trigger Price that the memo's own division does not produce. The corrections run BOTH ways this time — the book value is far worse than published and the discount rate and capex are far harsher than justified — and the true range of ignorance is $2 to $10, not $2.75 to $4.50. WAIT / 0% / no long, no short stands, and I would defend it in front of anybody. But it must be defended on the width of the uncertainty, not on a false precision that has now failed two red teams in a row.

ARCHIVE — PASS 3 (full report)

REALITY CHECK — RED TEAM, PASS 3 · TRULIEVE CANNABIS CORP. (NYSE: TRLV)

Date: 2026-08-26 · Memo under review: Munger Synthesis Revision 3, WAIT, trigger $3.35–$5.17, position 0% Price: $11.08 (24-Aug) / $11.32 [CALC] · Reviewers: The Ghost of Charlie Munger · The Living Warren Buffett Scope: the REPLACEMENT reasoning in Revision 3 only. Nothing passes 1 or 2 corrected and the memo has now fixed is re-litigated.

HEADLINE FINDING

Five FATAL findings. And for the first time in three passes, they do not stack in one direction — two of them say the memo is still too harsh, two say it has now overshot into generosity, and one says the memo's central escape hatch is quantitatively false.

The single most important thing I found is this. The memo's entire published thesis is "those three unknowns span $3 to $10 a share; I will not underwrite a three-fold range." I decomposed that range input by input, walking the memo's own bear corner to its own bull corner one variable at a time:

Input moved (bear corner → bull corner) Value/share Δ Share of the total spread
Bear corner ($3.07) $3.07 — —
SG&A $334M → $305M $3.86 +$0.80 12%
Capex $75M → $55M $4.60 +$0.74 11%
Harvest $75M → $125M $4.86 +$0.26 4%
(r − g) 14% → 7% $9.86 +$5.01 74%

The (r − g) pair is 74% of the $3–$10 range. Medical-only SG&A — the number the memo says it is waiting for, the number in its thesis sentence, the number that makes this a WAIT rather than Too Hard — is 12% of it.

The memo's Executive Summary says the Q3 SG&A disclosure "alone collapses most of the $3–$10 range." It collapses eight-tenths of a dollar of a $6.79 range. The memo already has this fact — §8 says the ceiling moved 2.2x from the (r − g) pair "that nobody moved" — and then draws the opposite conclusion two sections later in order to escape the TOO UNCERTAIN tripwires. The escape hatch and the width claim are mutually exclusive: whatever makes the range wide is not what the disclosure fixes.

And then the second half of the finding, which is the one that surprised me. Fix the two method errors below and the range is not three-fold at all. One error inflates the ceiling (a bull corner built by suspending the memo's own capex/growth rule) and one deflates the floor (net debt charged twice). They run in opposite directions, so no previous pass caught either. Corrected:

Corrected honest range (memo&#x27;s own inputs, both errors fixed):   $3.87 – $6.83      (1.77x)
Memo&#x27;s published honest range:                                   $3.07 – $9.86      (3.21x)

At 1.77x, tripwire #4 does not fire and the TOO UNCERTAIN question never arises. The memo argued its way out of a gate it did not need to be standing in front of, using an argument that does not hold, because two arithmetic errors manufactured the width.

# Finding Grade Direction
1 (r−g) is 74% of the published range; the stated ground for overriding two TOO UNCERTAIN tripwires is false by the memo's own decomposition FATAL Neutral
2 Net debt charged twice — interest deducted inside owner earnings and net debt subtracted from a value capitalized at a cost of equity FATAL Bull (+18%)
3 Stress test still mismatched-basis at the third attempt: post-tax buffer ÷ pre-tax drag. ~34%/~16%, not ~25%/~12% FATAL Bull
4 The capex/growth rule §2 promulgates is never applied as a constraint — violated at the bull corner (free growth, +$3.00) and unstated in Build B (sub-hurdle reinvestment, −) FATAL Both
5 Harvest write-down rests on a half-quotation of the 10-Q; the multiple-rejection is applied selectively FATAL Bear
6–16 Eleven non-fatal findings, five bearish, four bullish, two neutral — Both

Verdict: WAIT / 0% stands, and it is now a firmer refusal than the memo's own. On corrected arithmetic the ceiling is $6.83, not $12.75, and $11.08 sits 62% above it — outside the range rather than inside it. The memo talked itself into a weaker position than its own facts support.

CHECK 0 — TAUTOLOGY GATE

The gate is clean this pass, and I want that on the record before the criticism starts.

  • The struck witness stayed struck and was not backfilled. Pass 2's FATAL 1 demanded it; Revision 3 removed it and installed nothing in the slot. That is the first time in three revisions a bear point has been withdrawn without a replacement of equal weight, and it is the single best thing about this draft.
  • P/B 2.79x is reported as a fact and explicitly not as a witness. Correct.
  • The memo pre-empts its own tautology: "A business earning exactly r is worth about book, which is $3.98 — a number that lands on top of my DCF central. That is not corroboration; it is the same computation (B × ROE/r ≡ OE/r), and I flag it as such rather than banking it twice." That is the gate being run by the author against himself, correctly, on a coincidence that would have flattered him. Credit where it is due — it is exactly what pass 1 and pass 2 asked for and neither got.
  • The §6 corroboration claim ($118M FCF-route vs $114M P&L-build) passes. I tested it symbolically. $118M derives from reported consolidated OCF less a Harvest marginal-margin adjustment; $114M derives from revenue × gross margin less SG&A, interest, tax and capex. They share no input but the company itself. Algebraically independent — this is a genuine second witness and the memo is entitled to it. (It is not on a matched basis, which is Non-Fatal 11, but it is not a tautology.)

Assumption smuggling — one instance survives, and it is FATAL 4. g is now stated in every cell, as pass 2 demanded. What is not stated is the implied return on the incremental capex that separates the two builds, and that unstated value determines both endpoints of the published Buy Zone. See below.

FATAL 1 — The range is 74% discount rate, and the escape from TOO UNCERTAIN is quantitatively false

Parent question (h), and it is the load-bearing question in the document.

The memo, §8:

> "Two now fire honestly: I am writing that I cannot value this within a factor of three (#4), and a $3–$10 range does imply I distrust my own estimate (#5)... I argue out on one ground, and it is narrow: this is non-disclosure with a known resolution date, not unknowability. Medical-only SG&A appears in the Q3 10-Q."

And the Executive Summary: "A first-ever medical-only SG&A and maintenance-capex disclosure in the Q3 10-Q — this alone collapses most of the $3–$10 range, and it is the reason this is WAIT rather than Too Hard."

Test it. Walk the memo's own bear corner (SG&A $334M, capex $75M, r 14%, g 0, Harvest $75M) to its own bull corner (SG&A $305M, capex $55M, r 10%, g 3%, Harvest $125M), one input at a time, using the memo's own build:

Bear corner:   EBITDA 559−334 = 225;  tax 26%×(225−77−24) = 32.24
               OE = 225 − 24 − 32.24 − 75 = 93.76
               93.76/0.14 = 669.7  + 75 − 154.7 = 590.0  ÷ 192.57 = $3.06   ✓ memo&#x27;s $3.07

+ SG&amp;A → $305M          OE 115.22   →  823.0 + 75 − 154.7 = 743.3  → $3.86    Δ +$0.80
+ capex → $55M          OE 135.22   →  965.9 + 75 − 154.7 = 886.2  → $4.60    Δ +$0.74
+ Harvest → $125M                                                   → $4.86    Δ +$0.26
+ (r−g) 14% → 7%                    → 1931.7 + 125 − 154.7 = 1902.0 → $9.88    Δ +$5.01   ✓ memo&#x27;s $9.86

Of the $6.79 spread: (r − g) is $5.01. SG&A is $0.80. Maintenance capex is $0.74.

The two items the memo says the Q3 10-Q resolves account for 23% of the range between them, and only one of them is actually a disclosure — the 10-Q reports total capex, not a maintenance/growth split, so the capex question is resolved by inference over "two quarters," not by filing. The disclosure the memo is waiting for collapses roughly one-eighth of its stated range. Three-quarters of the range is a judgment the memo has already made and could remake tomorrow without new information.

Why this is FATAL. It is the sole stated ground for overriding two tripwires, it appears in the Executive Summary as "Evidence That Would Resolve This," and it is contradicted by the memo's own §8, which says in terms that the ceiling moved 2.2x from (r − g) and that owner earnings — the thing SG&A and capex feed — "has no power to flip anything." The memo holds both propositions on facing pages.

One genuine credit inside this finding, which the memo undersells. Its claim that medical-only SG&A appears in the Q3 10-Q is stronger than it says. The deconsolidation closed 3-Jun-2026; Harvest is equity-method; therefore Q3'26 consolidated = medical-only by construction. The SG&A line will be disclosed mechanically, ~10 weeks out. That much is right, and the memo argues it too weakly. It just isn't worth what the memo claims.

Required fix: delete "this alone collapses most of the $3–$10 range." Replace with: "The Q3 disclosure resolves ~12% of my range. Seventy-four percent of it is my discount rate and growth rate, which no filing will settle. If that is unknowable, this is TOO UNCERTAIN; if it is a judgment I am entitled to make, I must publish the narrow band that judgment produces and stop invoking the width."

FATAL 2 — Net debt is charged twice, and r is a cost of equity

This is in the rebuilt §3 owner-earnings block and it is the direct descendant of pass 2's Finding 14, which the memo answered by fixing the smaller half of the problem and leaving the larger half untouched.

The memo's build:

Owner earnings (Build A) = EBITDA 239 − net cash interest 24 − cash tax 36 − maint. capex 65 = $114M
Equity value             = OE/r  +  Harvest 100  −  net debt 154.7
Discount rate            = &quot;Risk-free ~4% plus an equity risk premium ~5% ... plus a premium&quot;   ← a COST OF EQUITY

Owner earnings are already net of interest — they are a cash flow to equity. Capitalizing a levered cash flow at a cost of equity gives the equity value directly. Subtracting net debt again charges the debt a second time.

There are exactly two internally consistent conventions and the memo is using neither:

(A) EQUITY-SIDE:      FCFE ÷ cost of equity  =  equity value.   NO net-debt subtraction.
                      Build A, r = 12.5%:  114/0.125 = 912  + Harvest 100 = 1,012  ÷ 192.57 = $5.26

(B) ENTERPRISE-SIDE:  unlevered OE ÷ asset rate  −  net debt.   Add back after-tax net interest.
                      114 + (24 × 0.74) = 131.8;  131.8/0.125 = 1,054  + 100 − 154.7 = 999.7 = $5.19

    Memo&#x27;s hybrid:                          912  + 100 − 154.7 =   857.3            = $4.45

Either consistent convention gives ~$5.19–$5.26 at the central cell. The memo publishes $4.45. The double charge is worth $0.74–$0.81/share, ~17–18% of the band.

Run it across the whole grid on the equity-side convention:

r = 14% r = 12.5% r = 11%
Build A, g = 0 $4.75 $5.26 $5.90
Build B, g = 2% $4.15 $4.67 $5.37
Build B, g = 3% $4.48 $5.11 $5.97

Corrected Buy Zone: $4.15–$5.97, not $3.35–$5.17. (Enterprise-side gives $4.12–$6.32 — same conclusion, so the finding is not an artefact of my choice of convention.)

The mechanism, stated plainly so it can be checked: gross debt is ~$480.1M. Its after-tax interest cost of ~$26.6M/yr is deducted from owner earnings and then capitalized at 12.5%, which is a charge of $213M. The principal is then deducted a second time. Against that, the cash is credited twice in the memo's favour — $11M of interest income capitalized ($65M) plus $325.4M netted against debt — worth $0.34/share the other way. Net: the memo is $148M, or $0.77/share, too harsh.

Pass 2 told the memo it was "counting the cash twice." The memo credited interest income — which fixed the $0.34 — and left the $1.11 untouched, in the same paragraph. This is what it looks like when a red-team finding is implemented as a line-item edit rather than as a question about the frame.

FATAL 3 — The stress test is still mismatched-basis, at the third attempt

Revision 3, §7:

> "One capex figure this time — maintenance $65M — and post-tax, as pass 1 asked and Revision 2 ignored. Buffer: $559M − $320M − $65M − $24M = $150M pre-tax, $114M post-tax. Drag: 0.63 − (0.36 × 0.35) = $0.504 per revenue dollar, $448M at risk. FCF turns negative at a ~25% revenue decline; ~12% under a $60M/yr IRS installment plan."

The buffer is now post-tax. The drag is still pre-tax. Dividing one by the other is the same category error, corrected once and reintroduced in the correction.

Post-tax owner earnings  =  (0.63R − SG&amp;A(R) − 24 − 77) × 0.74  +  77  −  65
At R = 888, SG&amp;A = 320:     (559 − 320 − 24 − 77) × 0.74 + 12  =  102.1 + 12  =  $114.1M   ✓ ties

d(OE)/dR  =  (0.63 − 0.36×0.35) × 0.74  =  0.504 × 0.74  =  $0.373 per revenue dollar

Breakeven, no settlement:   114.1 / 0.373  =  $306M decline  =  34.4%   (memo: ~25%)

Cross-check for the tax shield running out: pre-tax income hits zero at R = $613M (a 31% decline), at which point OE is still +$12M because D&A of $77M exceeds capex of $65M. From there the drag reverts to the pre-tax $0.504, and $12M/0.504 = a further $24M. Total: FCF turns negative at a ~34% revenue decline.

With a $60M/yr IRS installment (a tax payment, not deductible — a straight post-tax charge):
   (114.1 − 60) / 0.373  =  $145M decline  =  16.3%    (memo: ~12%)

Correct: ~34% and ~16%. Published: ~25% and ~12%. The memo understates the buffer by nine points, a 36% error, on Load-Bearing Factor #3 of its own Executive Summary.

This is the third revision and the third wrong number for this one line: pass 1 corrected 22% → 27%; pass 2 corrected it again for the pre-tax basis; Revision 3 announced the pre-tax fix and then executed it on the numerator only. A memo whose claim to authority is arithmetic discipline cannot miss the same denominator three times.

FATAL 4 — The capex/growth rule is promulgated and then never used as a constraint

Parent questions (b) and (f), and this is the finding that makes the memo too generous rather than too harsh.

§2 states the rule: "you may charge maintenance capex and credit no growth, or charge full capex and credit the growth it buys — not charge growth capex and credit zero growth. So I run two coherent builds."

The rule is right. It is applied to construct the grid and abandoned everywhere else.

(i) The two builds are one build with a switch, and they do not converge

Build A and Build B share every input — revenue, gross margin, SG&A, D&A, interest, tax rate, Harvest, net debt, r — and differ in exactly one: capex, $65M vs $95M. That is precisely the $30M by which their owner earnings differ ($114M vs $84M). Two constructions that differ by one input are not two witnesses; they are a number and the same number minus thirty million.

They also do not converge. Solve for the g at which they agree:

114/r = 84/(r−g)   →   g = r(1 − 84/114) = 0.2632 r
   r = 11%    →  g* = 2.89%
   r = 12.5%  →  g* = 3.29%     ← above the memo&#x27;s own g ceiling of 3%
   r = 14%    →  g* = 3.68%     ← above it

Inside the published grid, Build A dominates Build B in five of six matched-r comparisons. And decisively: Build A's range ($3.94–$5.10) lies entirely inside Build B's ($3.35–$5.17). Neither published endpoint of the Buy Zone comes from Build A. Containment is not convergence, and a build that never touches either end of your band is not corroborating it.

(ii) Build B smuggles a sub-hurdle reinvestment return — and it sets both band endpoints

The rule says g must be "the growth the capex buys." The memo never checks what $30M buys. Check it:

Reinvestment rate b = 30/114 = 26.3%.    Identity: g = b × R.
   g = 2%   →  R =  7.6%
   g = 3%   →  R = 11.4%
   value-neutral (R = r = 12.5%)  →  g = 3.29%

Build B assumes management reinvests $30M/yr at 7.6–11.4% against a 12.5% hurdle — value destruction, never stated, and it is the reason Build B sits below Build A. That is a smuggled assumption of exactly the species Check 0 names, and it determines both endpoints of the published Buy Zone. Build B is not "the growth case." It is "the case where management spends $30M a year below the cost of capital," which is a legitimate scenario and must be labelled as one.

(iii) The bull corner breaks the rule in the opposite direction — and this is where the memo overshot

§5, the concession that produces the memo's most-quoted number:

> "SG&A at $305M, maintenance capex at $55M, r at 10% with g at 3% ... $9.86/share."

$55M of maintenance capex is below Build A's own $65M maintenance figure, paired with growth at the top of Build B's range. That is 3% perpetual growth for free — no capital funds it. It is the memo's own prohibition run backwards, and it is not one of the "four favourable readings" the memo says the price requires; it is a fifth, and it is not a reading of a disclosure, it is a suspension of a rule.

Correct it on the memo's own terms — if you credit g = 3%, you charge the full $95M:

EBITDA 254;  tax 26%×(254−77−24) = 39.78;  OE = 254 − 24 − 39.78 − 95 = $95.2M
   r = 10%, g = 3%:   95.2/0.07 = 1,360  + 125 = $1,485M  ÷ 192.57 = $7.71   (equity-side)
   r = 11%, g = 3%:   95.2/0.08 = 1,190  + 125 = $1,315M  ÷ 192.57 = $6.83   ← memo&#x27;s own r floor

The bull corner is $6.83–$7.71, not $9.86. And $12.75 — built on capex of 4% of revenue ($35.5M, half of maintenance) with r−g = 7% — is not a corner of anything; it is the rule suspended twice.

The bear corner, in fairness, survives the same test: $75M with g = 0 is the top of the memo's own $55–75M maintenance range, so it is the harshest coherent pairing rather than an incoherent one. I checked the bear side specifically because the memo's asymmetries have all run one way for three passes, and this one does not.

Why FATAL: the $9.86 ceiling is in the thesis sentence, the Executive Summary, and §8's claim that the ceiling "went from $4.50 to $9.86." That 2.2x move — the memo's proof that it has finally tested the input nobody tested — is one-third manufactured by suspending its own rule.

FATAL 5 — The Harvest write-down rests on half a sentence

Parent question (c). §3:

> "the 10-Q says the $188.5M mark was set using 'market multiples of comparable publicly traded companies' — the very frame I spend a page rejecting."

Here is the sentence, from raw_verify.txt:

> "The fair value of the retained investment was determined using a combination of the income approach and market approach, including consideration of discounted cash flow analyses, market multiples of comparable publicly traded companies, and other relevant..."

The income approach is named first. Discounted cash flow is named first among the methods. The memo quotes the fourth clause and characterizes the mark as though the first two do not exist — and the first two are precisely the frame the memo endorses and builds its own valuation on.

Is the $100M derived or asserted? Asserted. There is no arithmetic anywhere in the memo for it — no Harvest revenue, no Harvest margin, no Harvest capex, no discount rate, no control or marketability discount computed. A 47% haircut off the only audited number in the record, worth $88.5M = $0.46/share, arrives as a judgment sentence.

Is the rejection applied consistently? No. §7 runs a private-buyer test at 5–7x EBITDA on $239M and reports the $5.97–$8.42 result approvingly as evidence that "the corrected discount rate closed most of the gap." Multiples are inadmissible when they would add $88.5M to Harvest and admissible when they support the narrative about the marginal buyer. That is the double standard the question asks about, and it is there.

The haircut may well survive on other grounds — non-control, non-marketable, no ability to force a distribution, still Schedule I, still §280E, already loss-making at −$3.3M. Those grounds are real and the memo lists them. But they are not the ground it states, and a memo whose thesis sentence convicts itself for sourcing an input from a tweet cannot half-quote a 10-Q to move $0.46/share.

Required fix: withdraw the "market multiples" characterization, quote the full sentence, and either derive the $100M from Harvest's own economics or publish it as an explicit judgment with the range $0–$188.5M.

NON-FATAL FINDINGS

Five bearish, four bullish, two neutral.

The discount rate (parent question a)

6. It is asserted, not derived — and at least four of its seven stated bases are charged again elsewhere in the same model. BULLISH.

The derivation is: rf ~4% + ERP ~5% = 9%, then "the premium above that must now be priced on risks that actually remain," then "I adopt 11–14%, central 12.5%." Nothing connects any listed risk to any basis point. The 2–5 point premium is a judgment, and the memo says so honestly ("This is my number"). But look at what is inside it:

Stated basis for the premium above 9% Where else the memo charges it
$598M disputed claim, 28% of cap, no timetable Deducted separately in §4 as a PV of $290M / $450M
"One partial quarter... no cost line beneath the gross margin" Charged as the $305–334M SG&A range and as the published width
"90% of Harvest's economics with zero control" Charged as a 47% haircut — and Harvest's cash flows are not in the operating build at all
Licence dissolution formula, 28 → ~50 MMTCs Charged as g = 0 in Build A
Single-state concentration Legitimate
Schedule III is a DOJ order, not a statute; DEA proceeding live Legitimate
Commodity, three years of falling prices, 24.4% profitable Partly in the 63% margin and g

To the parent's specific question: "a single-state licence-lottery retailer with one disclosed quarter" is an earnings-uncertainty argument, not a discount-rate argument, and the memo charges it twice. Non-disclosure of a cost line is estimation uncertainty about the expected cash flow. It belongs in the cash flow, or in the margin of safety, or in the width — and the memo puts it in all three and in r. Buffett's own practice is the opposite: one rate, and uncertainty handled by refusing to buy. A memo written in his voice should not be bumping a discount rate for things it has already priced.

Strip the four double-charges and the defensible premium above 9% is roughly 1–3 points — r ≈ 10–12%, which is exactly what pass 2 said. Two points of r is worth about $1.00/share at the central cell.

7. The $9.86 ceiling is built at a rate §1 explicitly rejects. NEUTRAL, but it is an internal contradiction in the thesis sentence.

§1: "Not 10%, which would treat a single-state licence-lottery retailer with one disclosed quarter and a 28%-of-cap government claim as a normal listed operator." §5 then builds the honest range's ceiling at r = 10%. The memo cannot rule a rate out of its grid and back into its headline. At the memo's own 11% floor the ceiling is $8.61 on the memo's arithmetic (and $6.83 once FATAL 4 is fixed). The "$3 to $10" width — the entire published thesis — depends on a rate the memo says is wrong.

8. The memo never looks at what this company actually pays for capital. BEARISH, and it is the only market-observed cost of capital in the record.

The 10-Q shows mortgage notes at 8.14%–8.29% and a promissory note at 7.50%. That is filed, entity-specific, secured borrowing. A 12.5% cost of equity against 8.2% secured debt is a 4.3-point equity premium for a business with ~35% leverage, negative GAAP earnings, a $598M disputed claim and a licence with a statutory dissolution formula. That argues 12.5% is too low, not too high — and it is a far better anchor than rf + ERP + judgment. The memo derives its most load-bearing input from a textbook and never opens the debt footnote. Worth naming because it is the one place a genuinely independent witness on r was available and was not used.

Did the memo just move the number to satisfy the gate? Partly exonerated. Pass 2 said 10–12% was defensible; the memo adopted 11–14% with a central of 12.5%, i.e. above the top of what the red team offered. It did not take the handed number. But note that no cell in the published 11–14% range produces a value near $11.08, while the conceded bull case requires 10% — so the adopted range is, whether by design or not, the widest range that cannot flip the verdict.

SG&A and D&A (parent question d)

9. $320M has NOT overshot. It is still on the favourable side of every observed datapoint. BEARISH.

$320M / $888M = 36.04%
Observed consolidated:  Q1&#x27;26  104.9/287.0 = 36.55%      Q2&#x27;26  101.8/271.0 = 37.56%

36.0% is below both observed quarters. And the structural argument runs the same way: Harvest was a carve-out; corporate overhead stayed with the parent; post-deconsolidation that same overhead spreads across 82% of the former revenue base. The medical-only ratio should be above the consolidated ratio, not below it. The confirming datapoint is in the memo's own dossier — Q2'26 SG&A was $101.8M against Q2'25's $101.1M, essentially flat, in a quarter that lost two-thirds of a division. That is a fixed-cost signature.

So the answer to (d) is: the correction did not overshoot in the bear direction; it landed at the midpoint of a range ($305–334M) whose low end has no evidentiary support at all. A defensible central is $330–335M, worth −$0.35 to −$0.45/share. The memo describes $320M as taking the bearish finding; arithmetically it took half of it.

10. D&A $77M is correctly adopted but is a derived allocation, not a disclosure. NEUTRAL. It is pass 2's arithmetic, it enters only the tax line, and it moves owner earnings by ~$7M. Adopting a bearish red-team finding without argument is the right behaviour; it should just be labelled [CALC], not stated flat.

The rest

11. The §6 corroboration is independent but not on a matched basis. BEARISH on the corroboration, not on the verdict.

$118M is derived from H1'26 OCF of $108.8M, which includes ~$61.0M/yr of non-cash uncertain-tax-position accrual — tax expensed and not paid. $114M charges a 26% cash tax. Strip the accrual: H1 OCF $47.8M − capex $35M = $12.8M, annualized ~$26M consolidated. The FCF route gives $118M if the disputed tax is never paid and ~$21–26M if it is; $114M sits between. The memo notes the add-back in a parenthetical and then banks the corroboration anyway. The agreement is a coincidence of offsetting differences — no tax charge but full actual capex on one side, a 26% tax charge but lower maintenance capex on the other. Say so, or match the bases.

12. The headline contradicts the body — third consecutive revision. Parent question (e). NEUTRAL, and it is the reader-facing sentence.

Executive Summary: "Trigger: Buy anywhere ≤ $5.17 (absurdly cheap floor $3.35 | fair value limit $5.17)."

Body: "$11.08 is defensible" · "the honest range on this record is $3.07 to $9.86" · "at the far corner it reaches $12.75."

"Fair value limit $5.17" and "the honest range tops at $9.86" cannot both be true. A $5.17 trigger against a $9.86 ceiling is a 47% margin-of-safety rule and would be defensible said that way — but the memo calls it a fair value limit, which is an estimate claim, and the estimate claim is contradicted twice in its own §5. (I note for the record that the exact phrase "no margin of safety at $11.08 even at the bull corner" does not appear in the memo; the contradiction I can document is this one, and it is the same species.) Pass 1's Finding 6 and pass 2's FATAL 4 were both "the headline is not what the body produces." Third revision, third occurrence, new numbers.

13. The range of ignorance was still not published. Parent question (g). NEUTRAL.

Revision 2 Buy Zone:  $2.75 – $4.50   width $1.75   ratio 1.64x
Revision 3 Buy Zone:  $3.35 – $5.17   width $1.82   ratio 1.54x

Same width. Narrower ratio. Pass 2 recommended publishing the range of ignorance in the trigger. The memo published the range of ignorance in §5 and §8 prose and then printed a $1.82-wide band as the Trigger Price, the Buy Zone, the Executive Summary trigger, and the Position Size condition. §8 diagnoses the disease — "insensitivity mistaken for robustness" — and §9 reprints the symptom. The verdict still survives because the output format cannot express the uncertainty; the memo has simply moved the honest statement into a paragraph nobody quotes.

To be fair to the memo, my own corrected range is 1.77x wide, so a narrow band may be the right answer — but then §5, §8 and the thesis sentence have to go, and the memo has to defend "worth ~$5 against $11," which is the position it spent this revision abandoning.

14. The $450M full-crystallization PV is still not reproducible from the stated method. BEARISH-neutral, $0.13/share.

Pass 2 flagged the $2.34/share deduction as underived. Revision 3 says "that derivation is above." Run it: $598M over five years at 12.5%, ordinary annuity = $119.6M × 3.5606 = $426M ($2.21/share); annuity-due = $479M ($2.49). The printed $450M is neither. Small, but it is the third revision in which a printed figure does not follow from its own stated derivation.

15. Book value $3.98 is reported as a fact and is a derived-by-subtraction figure carrying an NCI. NEUTRAL.

Pass 2 attached an explicit caveat — "my equity figure is derived by subtraction and is total equity including any non-controlling interest; confirm against the 10-Q equity statement." Revision 3 dropped the caveat and printed "Book equity is $765.9M... so P/B is 2.79x" as fact. The 10-Q shows non-controlling interests exist (H1'26 net loss attributable to NCI −$5.9M), so equity attributable to common is below $765.9M and P/B is ≥2.79x. The direction runs against the memo, so nothing turns on it — but §3's stated purpose is to report facts rather than witnesses, and this one is an inference. (I checked the $2,077.4M asset figure against the alternative $2,095.5M in the dossier: the latter is the 8-K pro forma, the former is the actual Q2 balance sheet. The memo used the right one.)

16. Interest income is falling, not stable. BEARISH, small — offsets FATAL 2 slightly.

The 10-Q: "Interest income for the six months ended June 30, 2026 was $5.7 million, a decrease of $1.0 million from $6.7 million." Meanwhile private placement notes rose $136.7M → $195.8M. Forward net interest is worse than $24M, not better. ~$0.10/share against my $0.77.

POINTING THE GATE IN BOTH DIRECTIONS (parent question f) — THIRD TIME, AND THE ANSWER HAS CHANGED

The parent is right to press this. Pass 1 moved the band up. Pass 2 moved it up. Three passes ratcheting one way is a process telling on itself. So I will give the answer in two parts, because this time the corrections split.

Is the correct answer BUY? No — and the corrected arithmetic makes the refusal stronger, not weaker.

Take every bullish correction in this pass at once: fix the net-debt double charge (FATAL 2), fix the stress test (FATAL 3), and give the business the benefit of the memo's own EBITDA cross-check.

Corrected central (Build A, r = 12.5%, equity-side):                      $5.26
Corrected Buy Zone (both builds, equity-side):                     $4.15 – $5.97
Corrected honest range (bear corner fixed, bull corner rule-compliant):
        bear:  93.76/0.14 + 75          = $744.7M  ÷ 192.57  =  $3.87
        bull:  95.2/0.08  + 125         = $1,315M  ÷ 192.57  =  $6.83   (r = 11%, g = 3%, full capex)
                                                                  $3.87 – $6.83   =  1.77x

Against $11.08, the price is 62% above the top of the corrected range. The memo's published range has $11.08 inside it ($3.07–$12.75). Mine does not. I reached a firmer refusal by correcting the memo in the bull's favour, because the two errors I found ran in opposite directions and the ceiling error was the larger one.

Test it the other way, from the price down. To justify $11.08 you need equity of $2,133.7M; less Harvest at $100M, that is $2,034M of operating value, which is $254M of owner earnings at r − g = 12.5% or $142M at r − g = 7%. Owner earnings of $142M requires SG&A at $305M (below every observed quarter), maintenance capex at $55M (below the memo's own maintenance floor), and g = 3% funded by that same $55M. The price does not require four favourable readings. It requires three favourable readings plus one suspension of an accounting identity. That is not a defensible conjunction; it is a defensible conjunction plus a free lunch.

I will also say what the bulls have, honestly. The build ties to history better than the memo admits: FY2025 GAAP EBITDA margin was 28%, and 28% × $888M = $249M against the memo's $239M; the medical entity carries three more points of gross margin, which argues $260–275M. At $275M of EBITDA the central cell is $5.56 equity-side. That is a real cross-check the memo could have run and did not, and it runs in the bull's favour. It still does not get near $11.08.

Answer: no. A sequence of individually-reasonable bear choices has not accumulated into a wrong conclusion. It has accumulated into a right conclusion with a wrong number attached — three times.

Has Revision 3 overshot into generosity? Yes, at the ceiling, and I can name the exact mechanism.

This is the second half of the answer and it matters more than the first, because it identifies what changed about the ratchet.

Passes 1 and 2 raised the band by finding arithmetic errors — an SBC double-count, a smuggled g. Error-correction converges; each pass has less left to find. Revision 3 raised the ceiling by relaxing a rule — it suspended its own capex/growth constraint at the bull corner to reach $9.86 and suspended it twice to reach $12.75. Concession does not converge. A memo that concedes on every point is not more accurate than one that concedes on none, and §0's stated test for this revision — "whether the bear case comes down and stays down" — is the wrong test, because it makes the direction of movement the criterion instead of the correctness of it.

Name it plainly: Revision 3 is too generous at the top and still too harsh at the bottom. The $9.86 is overstated by ~$3.00 (free growth). The $3.35 is understated by ~$0.80 (double-charged debt). Those two errors are what create the three-fold width the memo has built its entire thesis on. The width is not the finding. The width is the residue of two mistakes pointing away from each other.

And that is the answer to the process question underneath all of this. The third pass did not ratchet the same way. It ratcheted the floor up and the ceiling down — which is what you should expect from a process that has actually started to converge, and it is the first evidence in three passes that the bottom has been found.

THE REAL CHARLIE MUNGER'S TAKE

He's finally stopped defending and started thinking. I'll say that first, because it's true and because I've spent two passes telling him the opposite.

He struck the witness and put nothing in the hole. Three revisions running, every time we knocked out a bear point he built a new one in the same spot, and this time he just left it empty. He wrote down the growth rate. He caught himself sitting on top of a book-value identity that would have flattered him and said out loud, before anybody asked, that it was the same computation in a different hat. That is a man running the gate against himself. It's rare and I'll not pretend otherwise.

Now. He has built his entire thesis on a sentence — those three unknowns span three dollars to ten dollars a share — and I took that sentence apart one variable at a time, using his own numbers, and seventy-four percent of the width is his discount rate and his growth rate. The selling expense he can't stop talking about, the one in his headline, the one that makes this a WAIT instead of the Too Hard pile — eighty cents of six dollars and seventy-nine. Twelve percent. And then he tells the reader that the Q3 filing "alone collapses most" of the range. It collapses an eighth of it. He knows this; he wrote it himself two pages earlier, that the ceiling moved two-point-two times from the pair "nobody moved." He has the fact on page eight and the opposite conclusion on page nine, and the conclusion is what let him out of a gate he'd just admitted he was standing in front of.

Then the arithmetic, and this one is a beauty because it runs his way. He deducts the interest inside owner earnings — that's a cash flow to the shareholder, after the bank is paid — capitalizes it at a cost of equity, and then subtracts the debt all over again. He's charging the mortgage twice: once as the monthly payment, once as the balance. Do it either honest way and his four forty-five is five nineteen or five twenty-six. Eighteen percent, in the one build the whole memo rests on, and he introduced it in the paragraph where he was fixing the last thing we told him about the same line. We said you're counting the cash twice, so he credited the interest income and never asked whether the frame was wrong. That is what it looks like when a man implements a correction instead of understanding it.

The stress test. Third time. Pass one told him twenty-two was twenty-seven. Pass two told him it was pre-tax. He writes "post-tax, as pass one asked" — and then divides a post-tax buffer by a pre-tax drag. He fixed the numerator and shipped the denominator. It's thirty-four percent, not twenty-five, and sixteen, not twelve, and it's Load-Bearing Factor number three in his own summary. Three revisions, three wrong numbers, one line.

And here is where I part company with the man on the other side of the room, because for once the memo is too soft. He wrote the rule himself, in italics: charge maintenance capex and credit no growth, or charge full capex and credit the growth it buys. Good rule. Then he goes to build his bull corner and charges fifty-five million of maintenance capex — less than the sixty-five he calls maintenance — while crediting three percent growth forever. Growth for nothing. He forbade it on page two and did it on page five, and page five is where the nine eighty-six comes from, and nine eighty-six is in his thesis sentence. Fix it on his own rule and the corner is six eighty-three. The twelve seventy-five isn't a corner at all — that's the rule suspended twice and a capex number half of maintenance.

Two builds, he says. They share every single input but one. That's a number and the same number minus thirty million wearing a second hat, and he never once checked what the thirty million buys — which is seven and a half to eleven percent against a twelve and a half percent hurdle. His "growth case" is the case where management lights money on fire, and both ends of his published band come out of it while the other build sits quietly in the middle touching neither.

And the Harvest write-off. He quotes the filing saying "market multiples of comparable publicly traded companies" and says that's the frame he rejects. Go read the sentence. "A combination of the income approach and market approach, including consideration of discounted cash flow analyses, market multiples..." The discounted cash flow is named first. That's his frame. He quoted the fourth clause and moved eighty-eight million dollars. Then two pages later he runs a private-buyer test at five to seven times EBITDA and likes the answer. Multiples are poison when they'd raise Harvest and medicine when they'd flatter his narrative about the marginal buyer. A man who puts "the maintenance-capex split traces to a tweet" in his own thesis sentence does not get to half-quote a 10-Q.

The standing tests: rat poison charged once and the dilution kept separate at thirty-four million — fixed, no complaint. Bullshit earnings — refused, with a checkable reason, three revisions running, and he's the only one on this council who understood that a tax provision cannot lift a pre-tax number. Pricing power — still absent, and sixty-fifty-forty off your first three visits will still be a coupon with a licence attached in the next revision too.

The destination is right and it is more right than he says. Fix his two mistakes — one at each end — and the range isn't three-fold, it's one-point-eight-fold, four dollars to seven, and the stock is eleven. He didn't need the width. He built it out of two errors pointing in opposite directions and then made it the whole argument.

THE REAL WARREN BUFFETT'S TAKE

Charlie's got the arithmetic. Let me take the part that's about judgment, because that's what's left, and it's where the memo's honest and where it isn't.

On the discount rate. He was told the old one was repealed and he re-derived it, and I want to give him proper credit for something: we suggested ten to twelve, and he came back with eleven to fourteen. He didn't take our number. That's the opposite of what a man does who's just satisfying a gate, and after two passes of watching him backfill, it deserves saying.

Now the trouble. Look at what he's put inside that premium. The six-hundred-million-dollar argument with the government — he charges it in the rate and then subtracts its present value separately, two pages later. The Harvest stake he can't control — he charges it in the rate, and Harvest's cash flows aren't in the build at all, and he's already taken forty-seven cents on the dollar off the carrying value. The licence board that can un-grant what it granted — he charges it in the rate and again as zero growth. And the one that matters most: "one disclosed quarter with no cost line beneath the gross margin." That's not a discount rate. That's not knowing what the earnings are. You don't handle not knowing the earnings by raising the price of risk. You handle it in the earnings, or you handle it by not buying — and he does both of those too. He's charged the same worry in four places and called the sum a derivation.

And there's a number sitting in the filing he never opened. This company borrows on mortgage notes at eight point one and eight point three percent. That's real money, priced by real lenders, on this entity, this year. He built his cost of equity out of a four-percent Treasury and a five-percent textbook premium and a judgment, and he never once looked at what the company actually pays. The only market-observed cost of capital in the record, and it's in a footnote he didn't read. Four and a half points of equity premium over the company's own secured debt, with a third of the balance sheet in borrowings and the largest liability in dispute — that argues his twelve and a half is generous, not stingy.

Then he does the thing I can't let pass. He writes, in the section where he sets the rate: "Not ten percent." Rules it out. Explains why. And then he builds the top of his honest range — the nine dollars and eighty-six cents that's in his thesis sentence, in his summary, in the line about how the ceiling moved two-point-two times — at ten percent. You cannot rule a number out of your grid and back into your headline. On his own eleven percent floor, and with his own capex rule enforced, the top is six dollars and eighty-three cents.

Which brings me to the thing I did not expect to be writing. I went looking for the place this memo was still too hard on the business, because it's been too hard twice and I told it so twice. I found it — he's charging the debt twice and it's worth eighty cents. And then I found the other one, and it goes the other way and it's worth three dollars. His range is wide because he made two mistakes that point away from each other. Fix both and it's four dollars to seven, and the stock is eleven, and the refusal is easier than the one he published, not harder. He talked himself into a weaker position than his own facts support, and then built the whole memo on the weakness.

On Rule Number One, and this is the part that doesn't move. I don't need the third decimal. Two billion one hundred thirty million dollars for a business in one state, whose competitive protection is a count of licences the legislature has published a formula for doubling — twenty-eight becoming fifty — whose largest liability is a six-hundred-million-dollar argument with the Internal Revenue Service that the IRS has said in public is not valid, whose cash went up seventy million while it borrowed fifty-nine, and whose chief executive has bought zero shares and sold one point seven million. On the most generous arithmetic anyone in this building has produced across three passes, it earns about a hundred and fourteen million dollars. That's a five-point-three percent owner yield with no dividend, against a four percent Treasury, on a licence.

Would I buy it? No. Not because I can't tell you whether it's worth three or ten — I can tell you it's worth about four to seven, and that's the correction to what the memo says about itself. Because seven is not eleven, and the four-to-seven still rests on a selling expense line no human being has ever printed.

Would I short it? No. No borrow cost in the record, no short interest, no days-to-cover, a live DEA proceeding on somebody else's calendar. He says that plainly now, and says it's a judgment rather than a calculation. That's right.

Too hard? Here is where I'll give him the point he argued for and undersold. The deconsolidation closed the third of June. Harvest is equity-method. So the third-quarter income statement is the medical-only income statement, mechanically, and it's about ten weeks out. That's a genuine known resolution date and it makes WAIT the honest posture rather than Too Hard. He's right about the conclusion. He's wrong about why: that filing settles an eighth of his range, not most of it, and the other three-quarters is his own judgment about the price of risk — which no filing has ever settled for anybody.

THE "OLD SCHOOL" FINAL SCORECARD

  • Circle of Competence: OUT. Unchanged and unchallenged. One partial quarter of medical-only revenue and gross margin and no cost line beneath it, ever. A tax regime, corporate structure and listing venue rewritten in seven weeks. A moat with a statutory dissolution formula. Item 10 absent, Section M empty, Section H contaminated, no verbatim management Q&A. I add one thing pass 2 didn't say: the memo's own discount rate has now been rewritten twice in three drafts, from 13–18% to 11–14%, and 74% of its published range is that number. When the price of risk is the answer, you are not analysing a business — you are quoting your own mood.

  • Moat Integrity: NARROW AND ON A LEGISLATED CLOCK. Unchanged. §381.986 Fla. Stat. mints four MMTCs per 100,000 new patients; 22 queued; 28 becoming ~50. Genuine scale evidence — 35% flower share on 22% of doors, 78% medical retention — and it is a cost position, not pricing power, settled by 60/50/40% off the first three visits and three straight years of falling flower prices. The Ohio AG's antitrust action names Trulieve among nine MSOs and attacks precisely the shelf access scale was supposed to buy.

  • Management Character: PROMOTERS. Unchanged. $177M of shareholder money into two failed ballot initiatives, one beaten 56% against a 60% bar. "Adjusted EBITDA" ~$97M/yr above GAAP on charges that recur annually. A "medical-only" reporting basis with no cost line under it. CEO: 0 purchases, 7 sales, 1,699,007 shares, ~$14.7M. Cash up $69.9M while private placement notes rose $59.1M.

  • Berkshire Buy? NO — and now for a narrower and better reason than either the memo's or pass 2's. Not "it's worth $3 and costs $11," which was wrong. Not "I cannot tell you whether it's worth $3 or $10," which is the memo's line and is an artefact of two arithmetic errors. "On corrected arithmetic it is worth $3.87 to $6.83, the price is $11.08, and the range still rests on a selling-expense line nobody has ever printed." That refusal does not depend on a width I have just shown is manufactured.

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the SECURITY: F. Unchanged. No earnings, no dividend, no carry, 1.6%/yr dilution, ≥2.79x book, largest liability disputed and >25% of market cap, three load-bearing inputs never disclosed, price 1.6–2.9x every corrected intrinsic estimate.

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the MEMO'S WORK: B−. Up from pass 2's C+, and the upgrade is earned. The tautology gate was run by the author against himself and passed. The struck witness stayed struck with nothing backfilled — the first net reduction in the bear case in three revisions. Growth is stated in every cell. §1502 is properly demoted. The §0 confession about motivated reasoning is the most honest paragraph in any of the three drafts, and the concession that its prior robustness was insensitivity is correct. It loses the A for a net-debt double charge worth 18% of its own band, in the exact line pass 2 asked it to fix; a stress test whose denominator has now been wrong three revisions running on an Executive Summary headline; a bull corner built by suspending the coherence rule it wrote two pages earlier; an $88.5M write-down resting on the fourth clause of a sentence whose first clause says the opposite; and an escape from TOO UNCERTAIN that its own §8 refutes. Right destination, third trip, and this time it walked past the shorter road.

What keeps it a WAIT rather than a conviction is that the whole range still sits on a medical-only selling-expense line no human being has ever printed — and the Q3 10-Q, ten weeks out, is mechanically the first one that will."

ONE-LINE VERDICT ON THE VERDICT

Five FATAL findings, and for the first time they do not all point the same way: the memo's published $3–$10 width is 74% discount rate and 12% the disclosure it says will collapse it, so its escape from TOO UNCERTAIN is refuted by its own §8; it charges net debt twice against a cost of equity and is $0.80/share too harsh at the floor; its stress test divides a post-tax buffer by a pre-tax drag for the third revision running, making a Load-Bearing Factor headline wrong by nine points; its $9.86 ceiling is manufactured by suspending the capex/growth rule it promulgated two pages earlier and is really $6.83; and its $88.5M Harvest write-down rests on the fourth clause of a 10-Q sentence whose first clause names the discounted-cash-flow frame the memo endorses. Correct the ceiling error and the floor error — they run in opposite directions, which is why no previous pass caught either — and the honest range is $3.87 to $6.83, a factor of 1.8, the tripwires do not fire, and the price is 62% above the top rather than inside the band. WAIT / 0% / no long, no short stands, and it stands on firmer ground than the memo claims for it. The memo's real error in Revision 3 is not that it conceded too little. It is that it conceded to a width it had accidentally built, and then made the width the argument.

ARCHIVE — PASS 4 (full report)

REALITY CHECK — RED TEAM, PASS 4 · TRULIEVE CANNABIS CORP. (NYSE: TRLV)

Date: 2026-08-26 · Memo under review: Munger Synthesis Revision 4, WAIT, trigger $3.45–$5.25, position 0% Price: $11.08 (24-Aug) / $11.32 [CALC] · Reviewers: The Ghost of Charlie Munger · The Living Warren Buffett Scope: the REPLACEMENT reasoning in Revision 4 only. The SBC double-count, stale book value, g-smuggling, struck witnesses, UTP flag, Apple comparison, old trigger arithmetic, net-debt double-charge, half-quoted Harvest sentence and old stress-test bases are settled and are not re-litigated.

HEADLINE FINDING

Five FATAL findings. Two of them are large, they run in opposite directions, and they cancel almost exactly at the centre — which is why Revision 4 arrives at approximately the right central estimate for entirely wrong reasons, and then cites the near-agreement of its own corrections as proof that the process has converged.

The two big ones:

  1. The memo's "EBITDA" is not EBITDA. It computes EBITDA = gross profit − SG&amp;A. Gross profit is struck after the depreciation of cultivation and production assets, which under ASC 330 is capitalised into inventory and released through cost of goods sold. EBITDA is before all D&A. The missing add-back is worth ~$50M on an $888M revenue base — and it lands the memo's $227M at $277M, inside its own "$260–275M" GAAP-margin figure. The cross-check the memo published as unresolvable is resolved by a definition, not by evidence. Owner earnings go $105M → ~$142M. BULLISH, ~+$1.60/share at the memo's own rate.

  2. The discount-rate anchor is the wrong instrument, and the right one is in the record. The memo anchors an 11–13% cost of equity to "the company's own secured borrowing cost of 7.50–8.29%." Those two instruments are a $970 thousand promissory note acquired in the Harvest acquisition in October 2021 and maturing in eight weeks, and $93.6M of legacy mortgage notes. The memo calls them "market-priced, entity-specific, this-year debt." Sitting in the same record, unmentioned: $200M of 10.5% Senior Secured Notes due 2030, issued at par in December 2025 and January 2026 — the largest, newest and only genuinely market-priced instrument in the stack. An 11% cost of equity is fifty basis points above the company's own senior secured coupon. BEARISH, r → 14–17%.

Net them and you get this:

Memo, Revision 4:        band $3.40 – $7.92    central $5.26    price 40% above the bull corner
Corrected (both fixed):  band $3.28 – $7.73    central $5.62    price 43% above the bull corner

The published answer is nearly right. The published reasoning is not, and the offsetting is the finding. Revision 4's §8 declares: "The corrections split, the band narrowed from 3.2x to 2.3x... That is what convergence looks like, and it is the first evidence in four passes that the bottom has been found." It is not. A pass that finds two errors worth $1.60 and $1.50 a share which happen to cancel has not found the bottom; it has found a coincidence. Convergence would be a pass in which the largest surviving error is smaller than the last one. This pass's largest errors are the same size as pass 3's.

# Finding Grade Direction Worth
1 EBITDA = GP − SG&amp;A omits D&A inside COGS. Definitional, not judgmental. Resolves the memo's own "unresolved" cross-check FATAL Bull +$1.60
2 Cost of equity anchored to a $970K note assumed in 2021 and legacy mortgages, while $200M of 10.5% senior secured notes issued at par eight months ago sit unread in the record FATAL Bear −$1.24

Verdict: WAIT / 0% stands, and I could not break it in either direction. On the corrected record the price is 43% above the bull corner. Even granting the memo's own 11% floor together with the EBITDA correction — the most bullish coherent construction I can build without suspending a rule — the ceiling is $9.80 and the price is still above it. The verdict is genuinely robust. The memo's claimed reason for its robustness is false for the fourth consecutive revision.

CHECK 0 — TAUTOLOGY GATE

Two clean passes and one violation.

Clean — and Revision 4 deserves the credit. The memo now correctly declares the "two builds" to be one build with a switch and refuses to bank them as two witnesses. I verified the algebra it claims:

E = 105.24 (maintenance basis)   I = 30   b = I/E = 28.51%   r = 12%   R = r
g = b × R = 3.421%
(E − I)/(r − g) = 75.24/0.085793 = $877.0M   ≡   E/r = 105.24/0.12 = $877.0M   ✓ identical
R = 16%: 75.24/0.074288 = $1,012.8M  = +15.5%   ✓ memo&#x27;s &quot;+15%&quot;
R =  8%: 75.24/0.097158 = $774.4M    = −11.7%   ✓ memo&#x27;s &quot;−12%&quot;

That table is arithmetically sound and the "algebraically identical" claim in §3 is correct. It is the first time in four revisions that the memo has struck one of its own witnesses on Check-0 grounds before I got to it.

Clean — §6. "$118M against my $105M is algebraically independent... but it is not on a matched basis, and I will not bank it as I did last time." Correct, and correctly self-denied.
