FATAL FINDINGS

FATAL 1 — The memo's "EBITDA" is not EBITDA. It omits the depreciation inside cost of goods sold, and that single omission resolves the cross-check the memo published as unresolvable.

Parent question (b), and the answer is not the one the question anticipates: the record does settle it, definitionally, in the bull's favour.

The error

§4's build:

Central
Revenue $888M
Gross profit @ 63% $559M
less SG&A (incl. SBC) −$332M
EBITDA $227M

Here is the income statement, from raw_forensic.txt line 2–8 and initial_dossier.txt 476–510:

Revenue                                   271.0
Cost of goods sold                        108.7
Gross profit                              162.3
Expenses:
  Selling, general, and administrative    101.8
  Depreciation and amortization            25.7      ← a SEPARATE OPERATING line
  Loss (gain) on disposal/impairment        2.0

D&A is presented below gross profit as its own expense line. That line is the operating D&A — retail, corporate, intangibles. Trulieve is a vertically integrated cultivator with a 750,000 sq ft automated indoor facility and 3.5M sq ft of production. Under ASC 330 the depreciation of cultivation and manufacturing assets is capitalised into inventory and released to cost of goods sold on sale. It is inside the $108.7M, above the gross profit line, and it never appears anywhere the memo can see it.

EBITDA is earnings before all interest, taxes, depreciation and amortisation. Gross profit − SG&amp;A is EBITDA minus the D&A buried in COGS. It is not a conservative estimate of EBITDA; it is a different quantity.

The proof, on the company's own filed FY2025 numbers

The memo's own cross-check is the reconciliation, and it reconciles to the penny once you supply the missing term:

FY2025 revenue                    $1,181.2M
FY2025 GAAP gross profit            $711M   (60.2%)   [8-K, verbatim]
FY2025 SG&amp;A (H1&#x27;25 219.9 ×2 + H2)   ~$440–450M
FY2025 GAAP EBITDA                  $330M   (28%)     [dossier, XBRL CALC]

  Memo&#x27;s method:   711 − 445           =  $266M      ← does NOT tie to $330M
  Correct method:  711 + D_cogs − 445  =  $330M      → D_cogs = $64M = 5.4% of revenue

Cross-checked a second, independent way against Q1'26, the only quarter with a disclosed EBITDA:

Q1&#x27;26 revenue $287M · GAAP gross profit $170M · SG&amp;A $104.9M (= H1 206.7 − Q2 101.8)
  GP − SG&amp;A                    = $65.1M
  Reported ADJUSTED EBITDA     = $100M
  less SBC (~6.9) and one-offs → GAAP EBITDA ≈ $83–88M
  ⇒ D_cogs ≈ $18–23M/qtr  =  6.3–8.0% of revenue

Two routes, two periods, two data sources: D&A inside COGS is 5–7% of revenue. On $888M that is $44M–$62M; call it $50M.

What it does

                              memo        corrected
EBITDA                        $227M       $277M          ← inside the memo&#x27;s own $260–275M cross-check
less net cash interest        −$24M       −$24M
less cash tax @26%            −$33M       −$46M          (26% × [277 − 77 − 24])
less maintenance capex        −$65M       −$65M
OWNER EARNINGS TO EQUITY      $105M       $142M          ← +36%

(This is the conservative reading, treating the memo's $77M as total medical D&A. If $77M is the operating-expense line only — which the income-statement structure suggests — total D&A is ~$127M, the tax shield is larger, and owner earnings are $155M. I use $142M throughout.)

At the memo's own r = 12%: 142/0.12 + 135 = $1,318M ÷ 192.57 = **$6.85**, not $5.26.

Why this is FATAL

Because §4 says: "The SG&A-ratio evidence and the EBITDA-margin evidence genuinely disagree, and the record does not settle it."

They do not disagree. They differ by exactly one omitted add-back, and the record settles it in one line. The memo built a "genuinely unresolvable" hole out of its own definitional error, published the low side of that hole as its central case, and offered the hole to the reader as a disclosure item for the Q3 10-Q. There is nothing for the Q3 10-Q to settle. The Q3 10-Q will report EBITDA the way GAAP reports it — before all D&A — and it will print a number near $277M, not near $227M.

Required fix: restate EBITDA as gross profit + D&amp;A-in-COGS − SG&amp;A, disclose D_cogs as a [CALC] at 5–7% of revenue, delete "the record does not settle it," and delete the $227M-vs-$250M "conflict" from the Executive Summary's Evidence That Would Resolve This.

FATAL 2 — The cost of equity is anchored to a $970 thousand note assumed in 2021, while $200M of 10.5% senior secured paper issued at par eight months ago sits unread in the same file.

Parent question (a). The memo asked the right question, opened the right footnote, and read the wrong two lines of it.

What the memo says

> "Pulling the other way is the number I should have looked at first and did not: the company's own filed secured borrowing costs 7.50%–8.29%. That is market-priced, entity-specific, this-year debt. A 12% cost of equity is a ~4-point premium over the company's own secured paper... thin, if anything."

And: "I adopt 11–13%, central 12%... Nothing below 11% appears anywhere in this memo."

What the record says

From raw_verify.txt line 215 — the maturities note the memo cites:

> "...4,700 8.14% 8.29% 11/1/2027 Total mortgage notes payable 93,581 · Promissory Notes Payable Note acquired in Harvest Acquisition in October 2021 970 988 7.50% 7.50% 10/24/2026"

The 7.50% instrument is $970 THOUSAND, was acquired in an acquisition in October 2021, and matures on 24 October 2026 — eight weeks from now. It is 0.33% of the notes stack. It is not this-year debt, it was not priced by the market for this entity, and it is not a cost of capital. The 8.14–8.29% mortgages are also legacy secured real-estate paper.

From initial_dossier.txt line 1015 — never cited anywhere in four revisions:

> "In December 2025, the Company closed a private placement of 10.5% Senior Secured Notes due 2030, issuing the notes at par for aggregate gross proceeds of approximately $140.0 million. In January 2026, the Company closed an additional private placement of 10.5% Senior Secured Notes due 2030... a second tranche... approximately $60.0 million. The notes are senior secured obligations of the Company, bear interest at a rate of 10.5% per annum..."

That ties to the maturities table (2030: $200,000) and to the balance sheet (Private placement notes, net $195.8M vs $136.7M). It is the largest single instrument in the capital structure, it was issued at par by arm's-length buyers eight and twelve months ago, and it is SENIOR SECURED.

The memo's own §7 bear fact — "cash rose $69.9M in H1 while private placement notes rose $59.1M — the cushion is financed, not earned" — is the January 2026 tranche. The memo cites the transaction and never asks its price.

The corrected anchor

Private placement notes   $200,000K  @ 10.50%   (Dec-25 / Jan-26, at par, senior secured)
Mortgage notes payable     $93,581K  @  8.22%   (legacy real estate)
Promissory note (2021)        $970K  @  7.50%   (assumed, matures 24-Oct-2026)
────────────────────────────────────────────────
Weighted average cost of NOTES               =  9.77%
Marginal / most recent SENIOR SECURED rate   = 10.50%
(construction finance $120.2M and finance leases $59.8M carry higher implicit rates — the blend is a floor)

The memo's 11% floor is 50bp above the company's own senior secured coupon. Its 12% central is 150bp above it. Its 13% ceiling is 250bp above it. Equity in this business is junior to $200M of 10.5% senior secured notes, $93.6M of mortgages, $120.2M of construction finance and $59.8M of finance leases; it has one disclosed quarter of medical-only operating history; it carries a $598.2M disputed liability the IRS has publicly called invalid; its moat is a licence count with a statutory doubling formula; and it earns nothing on a GAAP basis. A 50-basis-point equity risk premium over the company's own senior secured paper is not a discount rate. It is a rounding error.

The minimum defensible spread of common equity over an entity's own senior secured coupon, for a business with ~35% leverage and negative GAAP earnings, is 3 points; 5–7 is conventional. That gives 13.5% – 17.5%.

And there is an independent witness the memo silently overrode. The refined dossier's mandated input:

> LOCAL COST OF EQUITY: 13–18% — with the standing instruction "⚠️ THE PIPELINE'S VALUATION ANCHORS BELOW ASSUME A LOWER, GENERIC DISCOUNT RATE."

The memo's entire adopted range (11–13%) sits at or below the dossier's floor, and the memo nowhere states that it is overriding it. Two independent constructions — the company's own marginal secured coupon plus a minimum equity spread, and the dossier's rf + ERP + sector premium — converge on 14–17%, central 15%. I adopt that.

Direction and honesty

I record two things against myself. First, this red team's pass 2 handed down 10–12% and pass 3 endorsed stripping the double-charges down to 10–12%. That recommendation was made without the 10.5% notes, and it was wrong. The memo did not invent the low anchor; it inherited a low anchor from us and then found the two smallest instruments in the footnote to confirm it. I am correcting the red team as well as the memo. Second, pass 3's non-fatal #8 pointed at the debt footnote and named 8.14–8.29% and 7.50% — pass 3 read the Q2 maturities note and never opened the December 2025 issuance disclosure. The memo elevated our incomplete read into the anchor for its single most load-bearing input, and that is how a red-team finding implemented as a line-item edit propagates an error instead of fixing one.

Corrected grid, both FATAL 1 and FATAL 2 applied (OE $142M, Harvest $135M):

r = 17% r = 16% r = 15% r = 14%
$5.05 $5.32 $5.63 $5.98

FATAL 3 — Check 0 violation: the "twelve-cent difference" is tuned by an underived 65%, and the two conventions agree exactly at 60%.

Parent question (f). This is the Check-0 worked example, reproduced: a sensitivity that varies one input while holding another at the precise value that determines the answer.

§1, presented as the memo's proof that its frame choice is immaterial:

> "Run it explicitly instead — add cash at face (+$1.34) and subtract a probability-weighted tax settlement (65% of face over five years at 12% = $280M = −$1.46) — and the central case moves from $5.26 to $5.14. A twelve-cent difference. The convention does not drive the answer, and I show both so nobody has to take that on faith."

The arithmetic ties: 0.65 × 598.2 = $388.8M; ÷5 = $77.76M/yr × a(5,12%) 3.6048 = $280.3M ÷ 192.57 = $1.455. And 325.4/192.57 = $1.690 less the already-capitalised interest income 8/0.12/192.57 = $0.346 → +$1.344. 5.26 + 1.344 − 1.455 = $5.149. ✓

Now vary the one input that was held fixed. Let p be the settlement fraction:

Alternative-convention value  =  5.26 + 1.344 − 2.2388p

  p = 0%   →  $6.60      p = 60.0% →  $5.26   ← the two conventions agree EXACTLY here
  p = 40%  →  $5.71      p = 65%   →  $5.15   ← the memo&#x27;s chosen point
  p = 90%  →  $4.59      p = 100%  →  $4.37

The conventions agree at p = 60%. The memo chose 65%. The "twelve-cent difference" is the distance from 60 to 65. Across the full range of p the alternative convention spans $4.37 to $6.60 — a $2.24 spread, 43% of the central estimate. The convention does not drive the answer; the settlement probability does, and it is asserted nowhere and derived nowhere in the memo.

And 65% is a bull assumption against the company's own books. Trulieve carries the full $598.2M as an uncertain tax position liability. Under ASC 740-10 that is management's own accounting judgment that the position is not more-likely-than-not sustainable — the company has reserved essentially 100%. The IRS has said in public that the refund claims "are not valid." Interest is compounding ($15.2M in Q1'26 against $9.0M in Q1'25). At the company's own balance-sheet position the two conventions differ by 89 cents, or 17% of the central case — not twelve.

This is FATAL under Check 0's explicit instruction ("Report any tautology or smuggled assumption as FATAL"), and it is FATAL under the skill's headline test, because the sentence "The convention does not drive the answer" is the memo's stated warrant for the frame the entire §4 grid rests on.

Required fix: state p as a named judgment, publish the $4.37–$6.60 sensitivity, and delete "the convention does not drive the answer" — or adopt the company's own 100% reserve and take the 89 cents.

FATAL 4 — "The price sits 40% above even the bull corner," graded High conviction, is true only because FATAL 1 and FATAL 2 cancel. Fix FATAL 1 alone and the gap is 13%.

Parent questions (c), (g) and (h) converge here, and this is the finding that matters most for the process.

Executive Summary: "Conviction: High on the refusal — the price sits 40% above even the bull corner." §8: "The corrections split, the band narrowed from 3.2x to 2.3x, and the price moved from inside the range to 40% outside it. That is what convergence looks like, and it is the first evidence in four passes that the bottom has been found."

Test the claim's stability. Take the memo's own five-corner construction and correct only the EBITDA definition (FATAL 1), leaving the discount rate exactly where the memo put it at 11–13%:

BULL  SG&amp;A 315 · capex 55 · r 11% · R 16% · Harvest 188.5 · D_cogs 55
      EBITDA 299 · tax 26%×(299−77−24) = 51.48 · E = 168.5 · b = 17.80% · g = 2.849%
      (168.5 − 30)/(0.11 − 0.02849) = $1,699.2M + 188.5 = $1,887.7M ÷ 192.57  =  $9.80

BEAR  SG&amp;A 345 · capex 75 · r 13% · R 8% · Harvest 75 · D_cogs 45
      EBITDA 259 · tax 41.08 · E = 118.9 · b = 25.23% · g = 2.019%
      (118.9 − 30)/(0.13 − 0.02019) = $809.6M + 75 = $884.6M ÷ 192.57       =  $4.59

$4.59 – $9.80. The price is 13% above the bull corner, not 40%. A 13% gap on a business whose load-bearing cost line has never been printed is not a High-conviction refusal; it is inside the noise of the corner. The memo's stated ground for its conviction grade evaporates under a single correction it did not find.

What restores the 40% is FATAL 2 — the discount rate:

CORRECTED, BOTH FIXED
BULL  SG&amp;A 315 · capex 55 · r 13.5% · R 16% · Harvest 188.5 · D_cogs 55   →  $7.73
CENTRAL                    r 15%    · R = r · Harvest 135   · D_cogs 50   →  $5.63
BEAR  SG&amp;A 345 · capex 75 · r 18%   · R 8%  · Harvest 75    · D_cogs 45   →  $3.28

Band $3.28 – $7.73 (2.36x).  Price $11.08 = 43% above the bull corner.

So the memo's headline number is right and its reason is wrong. It reports 40% because two errors of $1.60 and $1.24 a share point away from each other, exactly as pass 3's two errors did, one revision after the memo declared that phenomenon cured.

This answers (c) directly: the retraction of "$11.08 is defensible" does NOT survive on the memo's own numbers, and survives only via a correction the memo got backwards.

§5: "To justify $11.08 you need $1,945M of operating value net of Harvest, which is $186M of owner earnings even at r = 11% with 16% reinvestment returns — 1.8x my central estimate."

Solve it properly. Required operating value = 11.08 × 192.57 − 188.5 = $1,945.2M, with I = $30M and g = 0.16 × 30/E:

r = 11.0%  (the memo&#x27;s own floor)   →  E required = $196.4M
r = 13.5%  (lowest defensible)      →  E required = $256.1M
r = 15.0%  (corrected central)      →  E required = $289.5M

Against the corrected central of $142M:

Discount rate used Required OE Multiple of central Is the retraction safe?
Memo's own 11% floor $196M 1.38x No — this is a reachable number
Corrected 13.5% floor $256M 1.80x Yes
Corrected 15% central $290M 2.04x Comfortably

A retraction that holds at 1.8x only because the discount rate was set 250bp too low is not a retraction — it is the same selection problem the memo confessed to in §0, running in the opposite direction. The memo took the bearish end of the EBITDA cross-check and the bullish end of the discount rate, and the two produced a comfortable-looking 1.8x. Either one alone destabilises it.

(For the record, the memo's printed $186M does not reproduce from its own stated inputs on either natural reading — fixed-$30M reinvestment gives $196.4M, fixed-28.6%-b gives $175.0M. Fourth revision, and a printed figure still does not follow from its stated derivation. Logged as non-fatal #12.)

FATAL 5 — The stress-test parenthetical is inverted in sign. Fourth revision, fourth wrong number, on Load-Bearing Factor #3.

§7 and the Executive Summary: "FCF turns negative at a ~32% revenue decline (~34% once the tax shield exhausts and the drag reverts pre-tax); ~14% under a $60M/yr IRS installment plan."

The memo's own inputs, verified:

Buffer  =  (559 − 332 − 24 − 77) × 0.74 + 77 − 65  =  93.24 + 12  =  $105.24M   ✓ ties to OE
Drag, post-tax  =  (0.63 − 0.374 × 0.35) × 0.74  =  0.4991 × 0.74  =  $0.3693/revenue $   ✓ memo&#x27;s 0.369
Naive breakeven =  105.24 / 0.3693  =  $285.0M  =  32.1% decline   ✓ memo&#x27;s ~32%

Now do the piecewise version the parenthetical claims to be doing:

Phase 1 — while taxable.  Pre-tax income = 559 − 332 − 24 − 77 = $126M, falling at $0.4991/$.
          Hits zero at $252.5M of revenue decline = 28.4%.  OE there = 0 + 77 − 65 = +$12M.
Phase 2 — shield exhausted, drag reverts to the PRE-TAX $0.4991.
          $12M / 0.4991 = a further $24.0M = 2.7%.

TOTAL:  $276.5M  =  31.1% revenue decline.

When the tax shield exhausts, the drag gets BIGGER (0.4991 vs 0.3693), so free cash flow falls FASTER and breakeven arrives SOONER. Correct is ~31%, below the naive 32%. The memo prints ~34%, above it. The parenthetical moves the number in the wrong direction and presents the result as additional cushion.

The IRS-installment figure is right: (105.24 − 60)/0.3693 = $122.5M = 13.8% ✓ ~14%.

This line has now been wrong in all four revisions: pass 1 corrected 22%→27%; pass 2 corrected the pre-tax basis; pass 3 caught a post-tax buffer divided by a pre-tax drag; Revision 4 fixed the denominator and then bolted on a two-point adjustment with the sign reversed. It is small in dollars — it does not move the band by a cent — and I grade it FATAL only because the clause is affirmatively false in sign and sits in the Executive Summary as Load-Bearing Factor #3. (With FATAL 1's EBITDA correction the buffer is materially larger again; the memo should recompute from the restated base rather than patch this number a fifth time.)

NON-FATAL FINDINGS

Four bullish, three bearish, two neutral.

On Harvest (parent question d)

6. The 25–35% control-and-marketability discount is applied to the right attribute but to a mark that already contains it. BULLISH, ~+$0.28.

The parent's framing — that a control discount belongs on a minority economic stake and Trulieve's economics are 90% — is not quite right, and I will say so plainly: a discount for lack of control (DLOC) attaches to the absence of control rights, not to the economic percentage, and Trulieve has zero control. The memo is discounting the right attribute.

The problem is different and it is real. Under ASC 810-10-40-5, on deconsolidation the parent measures the retained noncontrolling investment at fair value at the deconsolidation date — and ASC 820 fair value is the exit price for the instrument actually held, which here is a 90%-economic, zero-control, non-marketable LLC interest. The 10-Q says the mark was set "using a combination of the income approach and market approach, including consideration of discounted cash flow analyses, market multiples of comparable publicly traded companies." Whatever DLOC and DLOM are appropriate to that instrument are already inside the $188.463M. Taking a further 25–35% off is discounting the same two attributes twice. Worth $53.5M = $0.28/share.

There is a genuine counter, which is why this is not FATAL: the mark is a management-determined Level 3 estimate in an unaudited interim filing, it sized a loss the record reports at both $403.3M and $688M, and Harvest is already generating equity-method losses. Those are grounds for a reliability haircut. They are not grounds for a control-and-marketability haircut, and the memo names control and marketability specifically.

7. "Derived, not asserted" is a mischaracterisation. NEUTRAL.

§4: "A combined control-and-marketability discount of 25–35% off an audited mark gives $135M central, range $75M–$188.5M — derived, not asserted." Selecting a percentage band from a textbook and multiplying is not a derivation. There is still no Harvest revenue, no Harvest margin, no Harvest capex and no Harvest discount rate anywhere in four revisions. Pass 3's required fix was "either derive the $100M from Harvest's own economics or publish it as an explicit judgment." Revision 4 did neither — it dressed the judgment as a derivation and moved the number $35M. From a memo whose entire rhetorical posture is derived-versus-asserted, that matters.

8. The consistency check the parent asked for: $135M against $188.463M and the $3.3M equity-method loss. NEUTRAL, trivial. The $188.463M is the deconsolidation-date mark; the 30-Jun carrying value is $188.463 − $3.3 = $185.2M. The memo's bull corner should use $185.2M, not $188.5M — worth $0.017/share. The −$3.3M covers roughly 27 days (3–30 June); annualising it to ~$45M of loss, as the memo's "already loss-making" language invites, is not supported by a stub period contaminated by deconsolidation accounting.

On the growth rule (parent question e)

9. R ≈ r is defensible as a central, but its stated justification is a basis violation the dossier explicitly forbids. BULLISH.

§3: "I take R ≈ r as the central case... a business whose best ROIC in five years was 8.3%, facing 28 operators becoming ~50 and three straight years of falling flower prices, is unlikely to reinvest above its cost of capital."

The 8.3% is FY2025 ROIC on the consolidated, pre-restructuring, pre-Schedule-III entity that paid an effective tax rate of 228% under §280E. The dossier's standing warning: "⚠️ The entity trading today is not the entity that produced the FY2022–FY2025 financials. Any expert citing a trailing figure must state whether it is consolidated or medical-only." Removing §280E does not change project pre-tax economics but transforms after-tax returns on the same capital. Using a §280E-era consolidated ROIC to set the reinvestment return on post-§280E medical-only capex is precisely the basis mismatch the memo has spent four revisions convicting others of.

And the record contains the strongest available argument for R > r, which the memo does not engage: Georgia SB 220, the "Putting Georgia's Patients First Act," signed by Gov. Kemp on 12 May 2026 — ENACTED, authorising medical flower and vaporisation for patients 21+, adding lupus, HIV and IBD as qualifying conditions, and removing the end-stage requirement. That is a legislated demand expansion in a limited-licence state where Trulieve "cultivates, processes and manufactures all cannabis products sold in its dispensaries." Incremental capex into a newly enlarged licensed market with in-place vertical infrastructure is the textbook case for R > r, at least for a period.

The memo's other reasons for R ≈ r — 28 operators becoming ~50, three years of falling flower prices, 24.4% of operators profitable, volume up and price down — are sound, and it runs R at 8% and 16% at the corners. So R ≈ r survives as a central. The stated justification does not, and the corners should probably be 10%/18% rather than 8%/16%.

10. The $30M of incremental capex — the engine of the entire g = b × R apparatus — traces to a social-media summary, and Revision 4 dropped the provenance flag Revision 3 carried. BEARISH on hygiene, neutral on value.

The dossier: "FY2026 capex guidance: up to $95M, raised from a prior $85M expectation; the incremental $10M earmarked for emerging medical markets, primarily Georgia and Texas [MEDIA per Finsee/X summary of the Q2 call — social-media source, unverified against the filing]."

$95M − $65M maintenance = $30M is the entire basis of b = 28.5%, and the R row is 31% of the memo's published range — the single largest contributor. Three defects: (i) "up to $95M" is a ceiling, used as a point estimate; (ii) the source is an unverified X post, flagged as such in the memo's own dossier; (iii) Revision 3 disclosed this — pass 3 quotes the memo's own thesis sentence convicting itself that "the maintenance-capex split traces to a tweet." Revision 4 promoted the number to load-bearing and deleted the disclosure. Observed capex runs $14M (Q1) and $21M (Q2) — $35M in H1 including five months of Harvest — so $95M implies $60M in H2, a step-up nothing in the record supports.

(Note this input is now stale in a second way: with FATAL 1 corrected, b = 30/142 = 21.1%, not 28.5%, so every g in the §3 table is overstated by about a quarter.)

On presentation and arithmetic

11. The Executive Summary asserts something its own numbers contradict — third consecutive revision. BEARISH, factual.

Load-Bearing Factor #2: "My $332M sits above both observed quarters (36.6%, 37.6%)." $332M ÷ $888M = **37.39%**. That is above 36.55% and below 37.56%. It is above one and below the other. The memo's badge of conservatism on its stated Load-Bearing Factor #2 is a factual error in the direction that flatters it. §4 prints the 37.4% correctly on the facing page.

12. The memo's own conceded fallback does not reproduce from its own build. NEUTRAL, $0.29. "At $250M of EBITDA the central case is $6.28, not $5.26." Run it on the memo's stated tax formula: tax = 26% × (250 − 77 − 24) = $38.74M; OE = 250 − 24 − 38.74 − 65 = $122.26M; ÷0.12 + 135 = $1,153.8M ÷ 192.57 = **$5.99**. The memo appears to have raised EBITDA by $23M and left the tax line at $33M. It is the same defect as the $186M and the $450M before it: a printed figure that does not follow from its stated derivation, fourth revision running.

13. The ≤12% Harvest marginal cash margin is derived from a single quarter-over-quarter OCF delta contaminated by a $55M inventory release. BEARISH on the §6 correction's precision. "Harvest gave up $24.5M of quarterly revenue while consolidated OCF fell only $56.0M → $53.1M, i.e. $2.9M — a ≤12% marginal cash margin." Inventories fell $242.3M → $186.9M over H1 — a ~$55M working-capital release running through OCF, plus the deconsolidation removed Harvest's receivables and payables outright. Attributing the entire $2.9M QoQ OCF delta to Harvest's marginal economics is not supportable at one decimal place. The direction is right; the "≤12%" is a point estimate on a contaminated base.

14. The memo repeatedly calls a Q2 10-Q figure "audited." NEUTRAL. "a 25–35% discount off the audited $188.463M mark" / "the only audited number in the record." Interim Form 10-Q financial statements are unaudited, subject only to a review. Small, and it runs against the memo's position on Harvest, but a memo that struck a witness for half-quoting a filing should not upgrade a review to an audit.

On the standing tests, for the record: SBC is charged once inside SG&A with the $34M dilution figure kept separate as a fact rather than a second charge — clean, no complaint. EBITDA is used as an intermediate and never as an earnings claim — the memo's insistence that §280E is a tax and EBITDA is pre-tax, so relief cannot lift it by a dollar, remains the single sharpest observation any participant has made across four revisions and it survives this pass intact. Pricing power remains absent: 60/50/40% off the first three visits into three straight years of falling flower prices is a coupon with a licence attached.

POINTING THE GATE IN BOTH DIRECTIONS (parent questions f, g, h) — FOURTH TIME

(f) Is the equity-side convention applied consistently? Yes, everywhere I could test it — and that is a genuine first.

I checked every site the memo touches:

Site Convention Verdict
§4 grid ($4.90 / $5.26 / $5.67) OE/r + Harvest, no net debt ✓ reproduces to the cent
§5 back-solve ($2,133.7M − $188.5M = $1,945.2M) equity-side, no net-debt add-back ✓
§7 stress test (buffer $105.24M ≡ OE) post-tax equity flow throughout ✓ ties exactly
§6 echo test ($118M vs $105M) both equity-side flows ✓
§7 P/B ≥2.79x on book equity equity-side measure ✓
§4 five-corner walk (17/15/13/31/24%) equity-side at every step ✓

The recurring defect of the first three revisions — a convention adopted in one section and violated in another — is genuinely fixed. I looked for it hard and it is not there. That is the best thing about Revision 4 and it should be said before the criticism.

The one thing left inside the frame is FATAL 3: the $598.2M claim is charged at zero in the headline while cash is valued at ~20% of face, and the memo's demonstration that these offset holds only at p ≈ 60%.

(g) Has Revision 4 over-narrowed? No. 2.3x is about right, TOO UNCERTAIN does not fire, and the memo is correct to have withdrawn the escape argument — but not for the reasons it gives.

My independently corrected range is $3.28 – $7.73, a factor of 2.36 — essentially the memo's 2.3x. So the parent's worry about false precision in the other direction is not borne out: the honest output is not a wider range and it is not TOO UNCERTAIN.

Decomposing my corrected range one input at a time, bear corner to bull corner:

Input moved Value Δ Share
Bear corner $3.28 — —
SG&A $345M → $315M $3.91 +$0.63 14%
Maintenance capex $75M → $55M $4.49 +$0.58 13%
Harvest $75M → $188.5M $5.08 +$0.59 13%
D&A-in-COGS 5% → 6% of revenue $5.29 +$0.21 5%
Reinvestment return R 8% → 16% $5.72 +$0.43 10%
Discount rate 18% → 13.5% $7.73 +$2.01 45%

Judgment inputs (r, R) = 55%. Disclosable inputs = 45%. The memo's split is right. I want that on the record, because pass 3 told it the split was 74/26 and it did the work.

But the memo then draws a conclusion from the split that does not follow: "a far more balanced picture than Revision 3's, precisely because narrowing r to 11–13% and binding the capex rule removed the manufactured width." Narrowing an input's assumed range mechanically reduces its share of the total range. That is arithmetic, not a discovery about the business — and here the narrowing was produced by the invalid anchor of FATAL 2. Restore r to a defensible 13.5–18% and r alone is back to 45% of the width. The 55/45 split is right; the memo's explanation for why is circular.

Tripwires — I agree with the memo's conclusion on all four, and I checked each independently. #4 does not fire (2.36x, well inside a factor of three). #5 does not fire (the band is derived, however imperfectly, not padded). #1 does not fire (one SEVERE flag). #2 does not fire — medical-only SG&A is undisclosed with a mechanical resolution date, because the deconsolidation closed 3 June and Harvest is equity-method, so the Q3 income statement is the medical-only income statement by construction.

One caveat that the memo owes the reader. The 2.36x width is stable only because both FATALs are fixed together. Fix FATAL 1 alone and the range is $4.59–$9.80 with the price 13% above the ceiling — a range that still does not trip #4, but a refusal that could no longer be graded High. The memo's tripwire conclusion is right; its stability is contingent on an error it has not found.

(h) Is WAIT / 0% right, or is the verdict the fixed point? The verdict is right, and I could not break it. The claim of convergence is not right, and this is the second consecutive revision to make it prematurely.

I tried to break it in the bull's direction and could not. The most bullish construction I can assemble without suspending a rule — EBITDA correctly defined, D&A-in-COGS at the top of its range, SG&A at $315M, maintenance capex at $55M, Harvest at the full mark, R at 16%, and the memo's own indefensibly low 11% floor — produces $9.80. The price is $11.08. There is no coherent construction in this record that reaches the price. At a properly anchored 13.5% floor the ceiling is $7.73 and the price is 43% above it. To justify $11.08 you need $256M of owner earnings against a corrected central of $142M.

I also checked the bear direction and could not push it below $3.28 without violating a rule: the bear corner already pairs the top of the maintenance-capex range with the top of the SG&A range, the bottom of Harvest, and an 18% discount rate at the ceiling of the dossier's own band. That is the harshest coherent pairing, not an incoherent one.

So: WAIT / 0% / no long, no short is the right answer on the corrected record, and it is the right answer for the reason the memo gives — the price is outside the range, not inside it.

But the convergence claim must go. §8: "That is what convergence looks like, and it is the first evidence in four passes that the bottom has been found." Look at what four passes have actually produced:

Central Band Bull corner Stress threshold
Revision 1–2 — $2.75–$4.50 — ~22% / ~25%
Revision 3 $4.45 $3.07–$9.86 $9.86 ($12.75 "extreme") ~25%
Revision 4 $5.26 $3.40–$7.92 $7.92 ~32% (~34%)
Pass 4 corrected $5.62 $3.28–$7.73 $7.73 ~31%

Pass 3 found two errors pointing away from each other and called the split evidence of convergence. Pass 4 has found two more errors pointing away from each other, of the same magnitude, in the replacement argument. A process is converging when the largest surviving error shrinks. Here it has not: pass 3's were worth ~$0.80 and ~$3.00; pass 4's are worth ~$1.60 and ~$1.24. The offsetting is not convergence. It is the same disease presenting for the fourth time — and the memo has now twice mistaken the cancellation of large errors for the absence of them.

The honest process statement is the one the memo cannot write about itself: the verdict has been stable across four revisions not because the arithmetic converged on it, but because the gap between $11.08 and any defensible number is so large that four revisions' worth of $1–$3 errors could not close it. That is a real and important fact — it is why the refusal is safe — but it is the opposite of the claim that the arithmetic has settled.

THE REAL CHARLIE MUNGER'S TAKE

He fixed the frame. I'll open with that, because I've spent three passes telling him he was charging the mortgage twice and this time he went and looked at the whole thing instead of patching the line I pointed at. I checked six places where he could have slipped back into the old convention and he didn't slip in any of them. He killed his own second witness on the two builds — worked the algebra himself, found they were one number with a switch, and said so out loud before I could. That's the gate being run by the author against the author. Twice now. Credit given.

Now.

He wrote down a line that says EBITDA and it isn't EBITDA. He takes his gross profit and subtracts his selling expense and calls the answer earnings before depreciation. Well — where do you suppose the depreciation on a seven-hundred-and-fifty-thousand-square-foot indoor grow house goes? It goes into the plants. The plants go into inventory. The inventory goes into cost of goods sold. It's sitting above the line he started from, and he never went looking for it, and I can prove it in one line of his own filings: take last year's gross profit of seven hundred eleven million, take his selling expense of four hundred forty-odd, and you get two sixty-six — and the company reported three hundred thirty. Sixty-four million dollars of depreciation he cannot see because it's hiding in a bag of flower. Do it on the first quarter of this year and you get the same answer a different way. It's five to seven cents on every revenue dollar, and on his eight hundred eighty-eight million that's about fifty million dollars, and it takes his two twenty-seven to two seventy-seven.

Which is the punchline, and it's a beauty. He printed a whole paragraph confessing that his two EBITDA methods disagree and "the record does not settle it." The record settles it in one line. There was never a disagreement. There was one method missing a term. He built himself an honest-looking hole, published the low side of it as his answer, and then handed the hole to the reader as something the Q3 filing might fix. The Q3 filing will report EBITDA the way everyone reports EBITDA — before all the depreciation — and it will print something near two seventy-seven and he'll wonder where it came from.

And then the other one, and this is the one that gets him for reading half a footnote again — the same sin he confessed to on Harvest, one revision later, in the input that matters most. He says the company's own secured borrowing costs seven and a half to eight and a quarter, market-priced, entity-specific, this-year debt. Go and read it. The seven and a half percent note is nine hundred and seventy thousand dollars, it was acquired in an acquisition in October of 2021, and it matures in eight weeks. That's not this year's debt, that's not market-priced, and it's a third of one percent of his borrowings. And sitting in the same file, which nobody in four passes has opened — two hundred million dollars of ten-and-a-half-percent Senior Secured Notes, sold at par, in December and January. Senior. Secured. At par. Eight months ago.

So his eleven percent cost of equity — the floor he says nothing in the memo goes below — is fifty basis points over what this company pays its most protected lender. Fifty. For the common stock of a one-state licence-holder with one quarter of history and a six-hundred-million-dollar argument with the Internal Revenue Service. That isn't an equity risk premium; that's a typo with a paragraph of prose wrapped round it. Fourteen to seventeen is the number, and his own dossier told him thirteen to eighteen on page one and he sailed straight past it without saying he was overruling it.

And here's the thing that ought to keep him up. Those two mistakes are worth a dollar sixty and a dollar twenty-four, and they point in opposite directions, so they cancel, so his five twenty-six is about right and his forty-percent-above-the-bull-corner is about right. He gets to the right kerb by walking off both sides of the road. And then he writes in section eight that his corrections split and the band narrowed and "that is what convergence looks like." Charlie — that's what cancellation looks like. Last pass found two errors that cancelled. This pass found two more, the same size, in the paragraph you wrote to replace them. If you've converged, show me the pass where the biggest thing I find is worth thirty cents.

The little one, for the fourth time running, because it's the same line every time: his stress test. He got the denominator right at last — I checked it, thirty-six-point-nine cents on the revenue dollar, correct. Then he adds a parenthesis saying that when the tax shield runs out the cushion gets bigger, thirty-two goes to thirty-four. When the shield runs out the drag goes up, so the cash goes negative sooner. It's thirty-one, not thirty-four. Four revisions, four wrong numbers, one line, and it's Load-Bearing Factor number three in his own summary.

And the smuggle. He tells us the frame doesn't matter — cash at face, less a settlement, twelve cents. Twelve cents at sixty-five percent. The two frames agree exactly at sixty. Take the company's own balance sheet, which reserves the whole five hundred and ninety-eight million, and the difference is eighty-nine cents. He held the one number that decides the answer and waved the other one around. That's the KSPI trick in a new hat and he ought to know it by now.

The destination is right and I could not move it. I built him the most generous machine this record permits — his own eleven percent, the depreciation put back, Harvest at full carrying value, sixteen percent on the new capital, selling expense at the bottom — and it makes nine dollars and eighty cents. The stock is eleven-o-eight. There is no honest road to the price. Sit on your ass.

THE REAL WARREN BUFFETT'S TAKE

Charlie's got the arithmetic, so let me take the part about how a man gets to a right answer by a wrong road, because I think that's the whole lesson in this file.

First, the credit, and it's real. We told him three times he'd charged the debt twice and each time he patched the line we pointed at. This time he went and understood the frame, and I went looking in six different places for the old habit and couldn't find it once. That's the difference between implementing a correction and learning something. And he did the harder thing too — he took his two builds, did the algebra, discovered they were one build, and said so himself. Most people defend the second witness. He shot it.

Now the trouble, and it's the same trouble in two places. He quotes a footnote and stops reading. On Harvest he quoted the fourth clause of a sentence whose first clause said the opposite, and he withdrew that — properly, in writing. One revision later he does it again on the one number that matters more than any other. He wants to know what this company pays for money, which is exactly the right question, and it's the question I'd have asked first. He finds a mortgage note and a promissory note and he stops. Sitting a few pages away is two hundred million dollars of ten-and-a-half-percent senior secured paper that people bought at par eight months ago. That's not a textbook. That's not a judgment. That's real money, from real lenders, on this entity, this year, in the most protected seat at the table. And the common stock sits underneath all of it, underneath a hundred and twenty million of construction finance and sixty million of leases besides, with one quarter of history and the tax man across the table.

Eleven percent for that. Fifty basis points over the senior paper. You cannot buy the bottom of a capital structure at the price of the top of it.

And then the number in the plants. Charlie's explained it. I'll only add the part that's about temperament. When your two methods disagree by twenty percent on the biggest number in your model, you have two choices. You can write a paragraph saying the record doesn't settle it and print the low one — which is what he did, and it reads as scrupulous, and it is not, because printing the low end of a gap you've declared unresolvable is choosing. Or you can go and find out why they disagree. He wrote the paragraph. And I'd rather a man print the wrong number and show his work than print the careful number and call the gap unknowable, because the second one is much harder to catch — I nearly didn't.

Here's what troubles me most, and it isn't any single error. He was too low on the earnings by a dollar sixty. He was too low on the discount rate by a dollar twenty-four the other way. They cancel. His answer is about right and not one of his reasons is. And then he writes that his corrections split and the band narrowed and that's what convergence looks like and the bottom has been found. Well — the last fellow said that too, one revision ago, about a different pair of errors that also cancelled. Twice now this memo has mistaken two large mistakes pointing away from each other for the absence of mistakes. That's not humility, and it's not rigour either; it's a man taking comfort from a coincidence.

On Rule Number One, which doesn't move and never did. Two billion one hundred thirty million dollars. One state. A moat that is a count of licences, granted by a board, under a statute that publishes the formula for doubling that count — twenty-eight becoming fifty. A largest liability of five hundred and ninety-eight million that the Internal Revenue Service has said in public is not valid, that the company itself reserves in full, and that compounds interest at fifteen million a quarter. Cash up seventy million while it borrowed fifty-nine at ten and a half percent — Charlie's right, the cushion isn't earned, and now we know what it costs. A chief executive with zero purchases and one point seven million shares sold. And on the most generous arithmetic anybody has produced in four passes, it earns about a hundred and forty-two million dollars. That's a six-and-a-half percent owner yield, no dividend, on a licence, against a four percent Treasury.

Would I buy it? No. And I want to be precise about why, because the memo's why has changed four times and mine hasn't. Not because I can't value it — I can, it's about five sixty, somewhere between three and eight. Because eight is not eleven, and because the whole of that three-to-eight still rests on a selling-expense line that no human being has ever printed.

Would I short it? No. No borrow cost, no short interest, no days-to-cover in the record, and a live DEA proceeding on somebody else's calendar. He says that's a judgment rather than a calculation. It is, and it's the right one.

Too hard? No — and he's earned this point twice now. The deconsolidation closed the third of June, Harvest is equity-method, so the third-quarter income statement is the medical-only income statement, mechanically, about ten weeks out. That's a known resolution date, and it's what makes WAIT the honest posture instead of the Too Hard pile. He argued it too weakly in Revision 3 and he's argued it correctly now.

THE "OLD SCHOOL" FINAL SCORECARD

  • Circle of Competence: OUT. Unchanged and unchallenged across four passes. One partial quarter of medical-only revenue and gross margin with no cost line beneath it. A tax regime, corporate structure and listing venue rewritten in seven weeks. A moat with a statutory dissolution formula. Section M empty, Section H contaminated, no verbatim management Q&A. And the marker for this pass: the memo's own EBITDA definition was wrong and its cost of equity was anchored fifty basis points over senior secured paper. When a business requires four revisions before somebody notices that the depreciation is hiding in the inventory, you are not inside the circle — you are describing its circumference.

  • Moat Integrity: NARROW AND ON A LEGISLATED CLOCK. Unchanged. §381.986 Fla. Stat. mints four MMTCs per 100,000 new patients; 22 queued; 28 becoming ~50. The scale evidence is genuine — 35% flower share on 22% of doors, 78% medical retention, 2x the next competitor's oil volume — and it is a cost position, not pricing power, settled by 60/50/40% off the first three visits into three straight years of falling flower prices with 24.4% of operators profitable. The Ohio Attorney General's antitrust action names Trulieve among nine MSOs and attacks precisely the shelf access that scale was supposed to buy.

  • Management Character: PROMOTERS. Unchanged. $177M of shareholder money into two failed ballot initiatives, one beaten 56% against a 60% bar. "Adjusted EBITDA" ~$97M/yr above GAAP on charges that recur annually. CEO: 0 purchases, 7 sales, 1,699,007 shares, ~$14.7M, under a disposition plan adopted eleven weeks before the restructuring was announced. I add one for this pass: the cash cushion the market is paying for was borrowed at 10.5% senior secured, and no participant in four revisions mentioned the coupon.

  • Berkshire Buy? NO — and the reason is now narrower and better sourced than in any prior pass. Not "it's worth $3 and costs $11." Not "I can't tell whether it's $3 or $10." "On corrected arithmetic — depreciation restored to EBITDA and the discount rate anchored to the company's own senior secured paper — it is worth $3.28 to $7.73, central $5.62, the price is $11.08, and no coherent construction in this record reaches the price."

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the SECURITY: F. Unchanged. No earnings, no dividend, no carry, 1.6%/yr dilution, ≥2.79x book, largest liability disputed, fully reserved by the company itself and >25% of market cap, three load-bearing inputs never disclosed, price 1.4–3.4x every corrected intrinsic estimate.

  • Graham-Dodd Grade — the MEMO'S WORK: B. Up from pass 3's B−, and the half-step is earned rather than given. The equity-side convention is applied consistently in every one of six places I tested — the recurring structural defect of three revisions is genuinely gone. The two builds were collapsed into one by the author's own algebra. The Harvest half-quotation was withdrawn in full and the fuller quotation printed. The private-buyer double standard was dropped rather than defended. The §0 confession that "backfilling and conceding are the same disease" is the most useful sentence in four drafts. It does not reach A− because the single largest earnings input is computed from a definition that omits the depreciation inside cost of goods sold, and the memo published the resulting gap as unresolvable rather than resolving it; because the single most load-bearing judgment is anchored to a $970 thousand note assumed in 2021 while $200M of 10.5% senior secured paper issued at par sits unopened in the same file; because the demonstration that the valuation convention is immaterial is tuned by an undisclosed 65%; because the Executive Summary's Load-Bearing Factor #2 states a ratio comparison its own §4 contradicts; and because the stress-test parenthetical is wrong in sign for the fourth revision running. Right destination, fourth trip, and this time it reached the kerb by stepping off both sides of the road at once.

ONE-LINE VERDICT ON THE VERDICT

Five FATAL findings, and for the second consecutive pass the two largest point away from each other — which is the finding, not a coincidence to be celebrated. The memo's "EBITDA" is gross profit less SG&A, which omits the depreciation of a 750,000-square-foot grow house because that depreciation is capitalised into inventory and released through cost of goods sold; restoring it takes EBITDA from $227M to $277M, owner earnings from $105M to $142M, and resolves definitionally the very cross-check the memo published as unresolvable before printing its low side as the central case. Against that, the cost of equity is anchored to a $970 thousand promissory note acquired in the Harvest acquisition in October 2021 and to legacy mortgages, described as "market-priced, this-year debt," while $200M of 10.5% Senior Secured Notes sold at par in December 2025 and January 2026 — the largest and newest instrument in the stack, and the memo's own "the cushion is financed, not earned" — sits unopened in the same file; an 11% cost of equity is fifty basis points over the company's own senior secured coupon and the whole adopted band sits below the dossier's mandated 13–18% floor without acknowledgment. The demonstration that the valuation convention is immaterial is tuned by an underived 65% settlement probability at which the two conventions happen to agree — they agree exactly at 60%, and at the company's own full reserve the gap is 89 cents, not 12. The headline "40% above even the bull corner," graded High conviction, is 13% if only the EBITDA is fixed. And the stress-test parenthetical is inverted in sign for the fourth revision running: exhausting the tax shield makes the buffer smaller, not larger. Correct all of it and the band is $3.28–$7.73, central $5.62, a factor of 2.36 — the width the memo published, at endpoints it did not — the tripwires still do not fire, and the price is 43% above the ceiling. WAIT / 0% / no long, no short stands, and I could not break it in either direction: the most bullish rule-compliant machine this record permits reaches $9.80. The memo's real error in Revision 4 is not bearishness and it is not generosity. It is that it arrived at approximately the right answer by walking off both sides of the road, and then cited the fact that it landed on the kerb as proof that it had learned to walk.

REALITY CHECK — RED TEAM, PASS 5 · TRULIEVE CANNABIS CORP. (NYSE: TRLV)

Date: 2026-08-26 · Memo under review: Munger Synthesis Revision 5, WAIT, trigger $3.40–$5.10, position 0%, band $3.42–$8.37, central $5.11 Price: $11.08 (24-Aug) / $11.32 [CALC] · Reviewers: The Ghost of Charlie Munger · The Living Warren Buffett Scope: the REPLACEMENT reasoning in Revision 5 only. HEADLINE FINDING

Four FATAL findings — and for the first time in three passes they do NOT cancel. Six of the seven material errors I can price run in the same direction, and that direction is toward the price.

That is the finding. Pass 3 found two errors pointing away from each other. Pass 4 found two more, the same size, inside the replacement argument. Revision 5 correctly stopped calling that convergence. But it has now replaced a cancelling error pair with one-directional bearish drift, which is a different and in some ways worse condition: cancellation leaves the number right for wrong reasons; drift leaves the number wrong.

Memo, Revision 5:        band $3.42 – $8.37    central $5.11    price 32% above the bull corner
Corrected (conservative): band $2.95 – $8.74    central $5.41    price 27% above the bull corner

The two claims that do not survive this pass are the two the memo is proudest of:

  1. "The most telling fact in the file is that those notes replaced 8.0% paper redeemed the same month — this company's own senior secured cost of money rose 250 basis points." It did not. The 8.0% notes were due 2026 and were redeemed out of cash on 5 December 2025, twelve days before the new notes closed; the filing states the new proceeds are for "capital expenditures and general corporate purposes," not refinancing; and the company net-deleveraged by $184M in the same month. Comparing a coupon set years earlier on paper months from maturity with a new 5-year private placement is a level-vs-spread and a term mismatch — the same class of error the memo convicted itself of on the §280E-era ROIC one page earlier.

  2. "No coherent construction in this record reaches $11.08." One does, and Revision 5's own headline correction is what created it. At the memo's own restated EBITDA of $277M, its own capital structure and its own $598.2M charge at p = 100%, the price is 7.7x EV/EBITDA excluding the tax claim and 9.2x including it — inside the dossier's own peer set. Run it backwards at Curaleaf's 9.6x and you get $11.73, above the price. At Revision 4's $227M the same computation gave $9.23 and the claim was true. The memo fixed the metric in the buyer's favour and hardened the claim against him in the same draft.

CHECK 0 — TAUTOLOGY GATE

One violation, and three clean passes I want on the record first.

Clean — the convergence claim was withdrawn outright, not softened. §0 now says the verdict is stable "not because the arithmetic converged on it, but because the gap… is larger than five revisions' worth of $1–$3 errors could close." That is the sentence pass 4 demanded, written without hedging. It is also testable, and I test it in (h). It half-passes.

Clean — R = r is correctly identified as an identity and used as a neutral, not banked as a witness. I verified it: at the central cell, E = 135, I = 25, b = 18.52%, g = 0.1852 × 0.15 = 2.778%, and (135 − 25)/(0.15 − 0.02778) = 110/0.12222 = $900.0M ≡ 135/0.15 = $900.0M. Identical. Which means the central grid is completely insensitive to the [MEDIA]-sourced $95M capex ceiling — a genuine structural improvement the memo does not claim credit for.

Clean — the dossier's 13–18% is used as a boundary check, not as a second witness for 15%. §2 says the rate "now sits inside the dossier's mandated 13–18% rather than below its floor." That is compliance language, not corroboration language. Pass 4 offered the memo a "two independent constructions converge" framing and the memo declined to take it. Correct.

VIOLATION — §3, and it is the Check-0 worked example in its fifth costume. The sensitivity varies p across 0/50/75/100% and publishes the result — good. But the central case sets p = 100%, which is simultaneously the bear corner, and the stated justification (deference to the company's own accounting) does not support the value chosen. See FATAL 3. This is the fifth consecutive revision in which the single input that decides the answer is held at a value the memo has not derived; the difference this time is that the memo shows its work, which is why it is FATAL rather than fatal-and-concealed.

FATAL FINDINGS

FATAL 1 — The 250bp cost-of-money discovery does not survive contact with the filing. The redemption came first, was paid in cash, and the company deleveraged.

Parent question (b). The memo calls this "the single most informative price in this record" and puts it in the thesis sentence, in §2, in §9 and in the Executive Summary. I tested it from the filings and it fails on four independent grounds.

What the record actually says

initial_dossier.txt:1011 and :1015, consecutive paragraphs in "Recent Developments":

> "In December 2025, the Company completed the full redemption of its outstanding 8.0% Senior Secured Notes due 2026, retiring the entire $368.0 million principal amount. Total cash used for the redemption was approximately $373.0 million, reflecting the principal repaid along with accrued and unpaid interest through the December 5, 2025 redemption date."

> "In December 2025, the Company fully repaid its Blue Ridge Bank mortgage note, originally due December 22, 2032. Total cash paid was approximately $15.8 million…"

> "In December 2025, the Company closed a private placement of 10.5% Senior Secured Notes due 2030, issuing the notes at par for aggregate gross proceeds of approximately $140.0 million. In January 2026… a second tranche… approximately $60.0 million. …mature on December 17, 2030… redeemable… on or after December 17, 2027… The Company intends to use the net proceeds for capital expenditures and general corporate purposes."

And raw_forensic.txt:18: "Trulieve reported that it used cash to redeem these notes and that the notes ceased trading and were delisted from the Canadian Securities Exchange."

Four things the memo's reading requires, and none of them are true

(i) The new notes did not replace the old ones. The sequence runs the other way. The redemption settled 5 December 2025, funded with cash. The new notes mature 17 December 2030 and are non-call until 17 December 2027, dating the issue to on or about 17 December 2025 — twelve days after the redemption. The filing states the use of proceeds is capex and general corporate purposes. A refinancing is a transaction in which the new money retires the old debt. This is not that. It is a cash redemption followed by an unrelated new-money raise, and the memo's word "replaced" — and the whole "refinanced from 8.0% to 10.5%" frame — is not supported by the document it cites.

(ii) The direction of travel is deleveraging, not distress.

December 2025 debt retired:   8.0% notes        $368.0M
                              Blue Ridge mortgage  15.8M
                                                 ───────
                                                 $383.8M
New money raised (Dec-25 + Jan-26)               $200.0M
                                                 ───────
NET DEBT REDUCTION                               $183.8M

The company cut its funded debt by nearly half in the same month the memo says the market repriced its risk upward by 250 basis points. Those two facts sit badly together. A smaller principal at a higher coupon on a longer tenor is the ordinary signature of a borrower terming out and shrinking, which is precisely what a company does when it stops needing the money — not what a distressed issuer is forced into.

(iii) The 250bp is a maturity difference and a rate-cycle difference, not a spread difference — and this is the one that guts the finding. The retired notes were due 2026. On 5 December 2025 they had at most twelve months to run. The new notes are five-year paper. For a sub-investment-grade issuer the 1-year-to-5-year credit curve routinely carries 150–300bp. That alone accounts for the entire step-up.

Worse: the 8.0% coupon was not a December-2025 price at all. It was struck when the notes were issued — CSE-listed paper due 2026, on Trulieve's Harvest-acquisition-era balance sheet. If it was priced in the 2021 vintage, when the US risk-free rate was roughly 0.5–1.5%, then:

8.0%  coupon − ~1.0% risk-free (issue date)  =  ~700bp credit spread
10.5% coupon − ~4.0% risk-free (Dec-2025, the dossier&#x27;s own figure)  =  ~650bp credit spread

On a spread basis this issuer's cost of money did not rise. It was flat to fifty basis points tighter. The 250bp the memo has built its most load-bearing input on is, on the most natural reading, the move in the risk-free rate between two issuance dates four years apart. Comparing two nominal coupons struck in different rate regimes and attributing the whole difference to the market's view of the borrower is a level-vs-spread error — and it is the identical basis mismatch the memo convicts itself of one page earlier, when it withdraws the "best ROIC in five years was 8.3%" justification because that figure was struck under a 228% §280E effective tax rate.

(iv) Public listed paper versus an unlisted private placement. The retired notes traded on the CSE. The new ones are a private placement to undisclosed buyers, non-call for two years. An illiquidity and structure premium of 50–150bp is conventional. Add that to the term premium and there is nothing left of 250bp to attribute to the issuer's risk.

And the load-bearing question the parent asked

Yes: the memo's single largest input rests on one data point. §8's own decomposition puts the discount rate at 29% of the entire published range — the largest contributor of the seven. That 29% is derived from one privately placed tranche, to unnamed buyers, at a stated coupon with no yield-to-worst, no rating, no comparables, no stated spread, and no disclosed market backdrop, plus a "generic 3.5–6.5 point equity spread" that is a textbook number.

What survives, and what must be deleted

The 10.5% coupon itself survives as a real, current, entity-specific price for a five-year senior secured claim, and using it as the base of a cost-of-equity build is legitimate — that part of §2 is right and is an improvement on the $970 thousand note. What must be deleted is every sentence that reads the 250bp as information about how capital views this business, because the record supports at least four innocent readings and rules out none of them. That includes the thesis sentence, the "most telling fact in the file" claim in §9, and §2's assertion that it "argues against every downward revision the red team and I have made to the discount rate across three passes." It argues nothing of the sort.

Direction: neutral on the level of r (10.5% is still the anchor), bullish on the reasoning — it removes the memo's stated ground for refusing a lower rate, and it removes the finding it was proudest of.

FATAL 2 — The 15% cost of equity double-charges the IRS claim and the licence formula, both of which are then charged again explicitly. The sentence denying this is false.

Parent question (a), and it is the most likely new defect, exactly as the parent suspected.

The claim under test

§2: "I adopt 14–17%, central 15% — the marginal senior secured coupon plus a 3.5–6.5 point equity spread… It is built from one observed price plus one generic spread, so it does not re-charge the tax claim, the non-disclosure, Harvest or the licence formula — all of which pass 3 correctly caught me pricing four times over."

Decompose the 15%

r_e  =  10.5%  (observed senior secured coupon, Dec-25/Jan-26, at par)
     +   4.5%  (generic equity spread, midpoint of the stated 3.5–6.5)
     = 15.0%

and the 10.5% itself decomposes:
10.5%  =  ~4.0%  US risk-free (the dossier&#x27;s own figure for Dec-2025)
       +  ~6.5%  CREDIT SPREAD

Now ask what is inside that 650bp credit spread for a senior secured lender to this issuer in December 2025. From the record:

Component of the Dec-2025 credit spread Charged again elsewhere in the memo?
The uncertain tax position — the balance sheet showed $668.4M at 31-Dec-2025 (raw_verify.txt:203), larger than the $598.2M the memo charges, against $255.5M of cash YES — $598.2M at p = 100%, −$2.08/share
Florida licence dissolution formula — §381.986 is a statute, in force and known YES — via R and the g apparatus, plus the "no floor adjustment" ruling
Federal illegality / no access to federal bankruptcy for a secured cannabis lender No — legitimate, and large
§280E on all activity at the issue date No — legitimate
Single-state concentration, small private issue, illiquidity No — legitimate
Medical-only cost-line non-disclosure No — medical-only reporting did not exist until Q2'26. The memo is right about this one
Harvest illiquidity No — Harvest did not exist until June 2026. The memo is right about this one too

Two of the memo's four claimed non-overlaps are correct. Two are false, and one of them is the largest explicit charge in the model.

Why it is worse than an ordinary double-charge

A discount-rate premium for a contingent liability compensates for the risk that it crystallises. The memo has set p = 100% — it has charged the crystallisation as a certainty. Once you have charged the worst case in full, the residual risk in that variable is not merely double-counted, it is negative: the only remaining surprises are favourable. Charging the maximum outcome explicitly while also paying a risk premium for the possibility of that outcome is the most punitive pairing available, and the memo executes it while asserting in terms that it has done the opposite.

Quantifying the overlap in dollars per share

The memo's own §8 sensitivity: r 17% → 14% is worth +$1.46/share, i.e. $0.487 per 100bp at the centre.

The IRS claim was, at issuance, a $668.4M contingent government claim against a company holding $255.5M of cash — the single largest identifiable credit risk in the file. It is not credible that a secured lender attributed zero basis points to it. A conservative attribution of 100–200bp of the 650bp spread implies a claim-clean secured rate of 8.5–9.5% and a claim-clean cost of equity of 13.0–14.0%.

Overlap  =  100–200bp × $0.487/100bp  =  +$0.49 to +$0.97 per share
As a fraction of the $2.08 explicit charge:  24% to 47%

Required fix: either state that r contains a tax-claim premium and reduce the explicit charge accordingly, or strip the premium from r and keep the explicit charge whole — but not both, and not while printing a sentence that says neither is happening. The licence formula needs the same treatment against the R/g apparatus.

FATAL 3 — p = 100% is not "the company's own probability." It is a recognition threshold being read as an expected cash outflow, and the memo has taken the top of a range instead of a point inside it.

Parent question (c), and the parent's hypothesis is correct.

What ASC 740-10 actually says

Under ASC 740-10-25-6, a tax position is recognised only when it is more likely than not (>50%) that it will be sustained upon examination, based on the technical merits. Under ASC 740-10-25-7 the assessment must presume examination by a taxing authority with full knowledge of all relevant information — it is a technical-merits test, deliberately blind to the practical reality that large tax disputes settle. Under ASC 740-10-30-7, if a position clears the threshold it is measured at the largest amount greater than 50% likely of being realised; if it fails the threshold, no benefit is recognised at all and the full amount is reserved.

A full reserve therefore means exactly one thing: management concluded P(sustained on technical merits) ≤ 50%. It does not mean management expects to pay 100%. It is consistent with management believing it will pay 60%, or 40%, or settling at 30 cents. The standard is a binary switch, not an expectation.

What the memo does with it

§3: "Under ASC 740-10 that is management's own judgment that the position is not more-likely-than-not sustainable — the company reserves essentially 100%. …I have no basis for assuming a more favourable outcome than management's own accounting. I adopt p = 100% as the base case."

The first clause is correct. The second does not follow from it. The memo states the threshold accurately and then treats the reserve amount as management's probability. "Not more likely than not to be sustained" bounds p to the interval (50%, 100%]. The memo takes the endpoint and calls the choice deference.

It is also not deference. It is the bear corner. §4's own corner definitions put p = 100% in the bear row and p = 50% in the bull row. The central case adopts the bear corner on the input the memo's own §8 says is 20% of the entire range — the second-largest contributor after the discount rate. Every other input in the central case sits at or near its midpoint; this one sits at its extreme.

Direction and magnitude

The error is BEARISH. The memo's own published sensitivity prices it:

p = 100%  →  $5.11    (memo&#x27;s central, = the bear corner)
p =  75%  →  $5.63    ← the neutral midpoint of the (50%, 100%] interval the standard actually implies
p =  50%  →  $6.15
p =   0%  →  $7.19

Correction at the neutral midpoint:  +$0.52/share  =  10% of the central estimate

$2.08/share is 41% of the $5.11 central case, and it rests on a reading of an accounting standard that the memo states correctly and then applies backwards.

The offset the memo owed itself and did not pay — and it is on the same line

§3 cites the compounding as evidence: "Interest compounds at $15.2M/quarter, up 69% year over year." It then charges $598.2M flat over five years and never accretes a dollar of that interest. If p = 100% and the claim is settled over five years, the balance accrues over those five years:

Balance $598.2M accreting at ~10% (IRS underpayment rate), paid in five level instalments:
   payment  =  598.2 × 0.10 / (1 − 1.10⁻⁵)  =  598.2 × 0.26380  =  $157.80M/yr
   PV at 15% =  157.80 × 3.3522  =  $529.0M      vs the memo&#x27;s $401.1M
   Understatement:  $127.9M  =  −$0.66/share

And it appears nowhere else either: the owner-earnings build charges $45M of debt interest and zero UTP interest, while raw_verify.txt:1 shows FY2025 "Total other expense, net" of $52.0M — a line that must contain both. Roughly $60M a year of accruing interest on the largest liability in the file is charged in neither the cash flow nor the claim PV.

Net effect on this line item: +$0.52 (p) − $0.52 to −$0.66 (accretion) ≈ zero. The memo arrives at approximately the right charge on the tax claim by making two errors on the same line that offset. Sixth time in three passes. It is why FATAL 3 is graded fatal on the reasoning and near-neutral in dollars.

FATAL 4 — "No coherent construction in this record reaches $11.08" is false, and Revision 5's own correction is what made it false.

Parent questions (g) and (h) converge here, and this is the finding that matters most for whether the memo can publish as written.

§5 and the Executive Summary: "Price $11.08 against a bull corner of $8.37: 32% above. Erase the IRS claim entirely (p = 0)… and the corner reaches $9.44 — still 17% below the price. No coherent construction in this record reaches $11.08." Conviction is graded High on that sentence.

The construction the memo did not run — on its own numbers

The memo's §9 concedes the frame in terms: "a marginal buyer pricing EV/EBITDA and takeout optionality… That buyer is not wrong about the multiple; he is wrong about the metric." Revision 5 then corrected the metric by $50M in the buyer's favour. Trace the consequence:

Against the dossier's own peer table (refined_dossier.md line 220): Green Thumb 7.3x, Curaleaf 9.6x, Tilray 20.0x. Against the dossier's conflicting current source (Motley Fool, 1-Jun-2026): Green Thumb ~3.5x, Curaleaf ~15.5x.

Run it backwards, holding every one of the memo's own inputs — including the $598.2M charged at p = 100%:
