$11.08 sits inside the record's own peer range on the memo's own restated EBITDA, its own debt stack, its own Harvest mark and its own maximal tax charge.

And here is the part that makes it fatal rather than merely arguable

Run the identical computation on Revision 4's $227M EBITDA:

9.6x × $227M = $2,179.2M EV  →  equity $1,778.2M  →  $9.23/share    ← below the price
9.6x × $277M = $2,659.2M EV  →  equity $2,258.2M  →  $11.73/share   ← above the price

At Revision 4's number the claim was true. Revision 5 restated EBITDA upward by $50M — its own FATAL 1, its proudest correction — and did not trace the consequence to the sentence that the correction falsified. It then hardened that sentence and graded conviction High on it. That is the same failure mode as Revision 4's convergence claim in a new location: a correction implemented as a line-item edit rather than propagated through the document.

The honest counters, stated fully

The Roundhill peer table's real vintage is worse than the dossier admits — raw_verify.txt carries the footnote "Source: Bloomberg, Roundhill Investments, as of 10/17/22," four years stale, where the dossier says "the source does not state an as-of date." That is a dossier-level data error and it cuts against my finding. But the current source (Motley Fool, 1-Jun-2026) gives 3.5x–15.5x, which brackets the price even more widely, and the memo is entitled to reject multiples as a frame — provided it says so at the point where it makes an absolute claim, and provided it does not concede two paragraphs later that the buyer "is not wrong about the multiple."

Required fix: replace "No coherent construction in this record reaches $11.08" with what is actually true — "No discounted-cash-flow construction in this record reaches $11.08; a peer-multiple construction does, at 9.6x on my own restated EBITDA, and I reject that frame for the following reasons." And regrade conviction from High to Moderate, because the memo's own conceded frame reaches the price.

NON-FATAL FINDINGS

Six bullish, two bearish, one neutral.

On the capex re-grounding (parent question d)

5. $70M is arithmetically derived and derived on the wrong basis — consolidated total spend used as a medical-only maintenance input. BULLISH, +$0.35 to +$0.52.

The observed figures, from raw_forensic.txt:507:

Capital expenditures    Q2&#x27;26 (21.0)   Q2&#x27;25 (16.0)   H1&#x27;26 (34.5)   H1&#x27;25 (36.8)
⇒ Q1&#x27;26 capex = 34.5 − 21.0 = $13.5M

$34.5M × 2 = $69M ≈ $70M. So the number is derived, not chosen — I checked, and the parent's suspicion of a convenience pick is not borne out. Three defects survive anyway:

  • It is consolidated. H1'26 contains five months of Harvest, and the memo says so in the same sentence and adjusts by nothing. Harvest ran roughly a quarter of the pre-deconsolidation revenue base; a proportional carve gives ~$7M of the $34.5M, so medical-only H1 capex is nearer $27.5M and annualises to ~$55M, not $70M. That is $15M, and at r = 15% it is +$0.52/share. This is precisely the basis mismatch the dossier's standing warning forbids and the memo has convicted three other parties of.
  • It is total capex, used as maintenance capex. The memo simultaneously asserts $25M of growth capex exists ($95M − $70M). Both cannot be inferred from the same $34.5M unless every dollar of H1 spend was maintenance and every growth dollar is back-loaded into H2 — which is possible but is an assumption, not an observation.
  • The two quarters do not agree. Q1 annualises to $54M, Q2 to $84M — a 56% spread — and the trend is up, not flat. "Annualising H1" averages two incompatible quarters and reports the result as a single observation.

I do not grade this FATAL because pass 4 graded the structurally identical ROIC basis mismatch non-fatal, and consistency matters more than severity. But note the pattern: the memo replaced $65M because "that argument collapses" — and the replacement moved bearish, in the same revision in which the EBITDA correction moved bullish by $50M.

6. The $95M ceiling: the flag is restored (credit) but the number is still load-bearing at both corners. NEUTRAL.

Revision 3 disclosed it, Revision 4 deleted the disclosure, Revision 5 restores it in full and names the Texas problem. That is the correct behaviour and it deserves saying. It remains true that I = 95 − maintenance sets the growth-capex term in every corner cell, and that H1 actual of $34.5M implies $60.5M in H2 — a 75% step-up in a company that just shed a quarter of its revenue. Mitigated by the R = r identity, which makes the central grid immune to it.

On Harvest (parent question e)

7. $160M is inside its stated method; the BEAR corner is not. NEUTRAL, $0.16 at the corner.

10–15% off $185.2M = $157.4M–$166.7M. $160M is inside that band — a rounded pick within a derived range, which is honest. But the bear corner uses $130M, a 30% haircut, twice the top of the memo's own stated method, with no basis given. The published range "$130M–$185.2M" cannot be produced by the published method. Small in dollars; it is the third revision in which a printed figure does not follow from its stated derivation, and this time it is one figure rather than four.

8. Selective skepticism, and the parent found the right place to look. BULLISH.

The memo applies a reliability haircut to an unaudited Level 3 management estimate on the asset side (Harvest, $185.2M → $160M) and no haircut at all to the unaudited Level 3 management estimates on the liability side:

Q2 balance-sheet item Nature Memo's treatment
Cash $325.4M Level 1, objectively verifiable Face — correct, no issue
Harvest $188.463M Level 3 management fair-value estimate −13.6% reliability haircut
Uncertain tax position $598.2M Level 3 management judgment under ASC 740 Face, AND at p = 100%
Book equity $765.9M Derived by subtraction Face, correctly flagged as [CALC] with NCI

The same "unaudited management estimate, therefore discount" standard applied symmetrically would take 13.6% off the $598.2M — worth roughly $0.28/share after discounting. Cash at face is not the inconsistency the parent hypothesised; the tax reserve at face is. The memo cannot describe one Level 3 interim estimate as needing a reliability haircut and treat the other as an anchor beyond question.

On SG&A — the input the memo calls Load-Bearing Factor #2

9. Management printed a normalised SG&A ratio on the call the memo mines for every other Q2 figure, and the memo neither cites it nor rebuts it. BULLISH, up to +$0.79.

From the Q2'26 earnings call transcript, sitting in raw_forensic.txt:

> "Second quarter SG&A was $102 million, or 38% of revenue. Adjusted SG&A at 32% of revenue was comparable to last year."

The memo's $332M central is 37.39%, anchored between two GAAP quarters (36.55% and 37.56%). Q2'26 is the quarter in which this company deconsolidated a division, booked a $403.3M loss and uplisted to the NYSE. Six points of the gap between 38% and 32% is roughly $16M in the quarter, of which SBC is ~$6.9M (2.5 points, and the memo is right to charge it) — leaving ~3.5 points, or ~$31M annualised, of genuinely restructuring-related cost inside the memo's anchor. Flowed through: +$31M EBITDA → +$22.9M owner earnings → +$0.79/share.

The counter is real and it is the memo's own: §7 convicts this company of "~$97M/yr of 'non-recurring' charges that recur." That is a principled reason to distrust the adjusted figure. It is not a reason to leave the figure uncited, in the section the memo names as the reason the whole thesis is a WAIT.

On the tax claim, second look

10. The memo asserts the liability is compounding while its own balance-sheet source shows it fell $70.2M in six months. BEARISH on hygiene, direction unresolved.

raw_verify.txt:203: Uncertain tax position liabilities $598.2M (30-Jun-26) vs $668.4M (31-Dec-25) — and the dossier records $696.4M at 31-Mar-26. So the balance went 668.4 → 696.4 → 598.2: up $28M in Q1, down $98.2M in Q2. Almost certainly a deconsolidation effect (Harvest carried its own positions out), but the memo does not say so, does not test it, and prints "interest compounds at $15.2M/quarter, up 69% year over year" as the evidentiary spine of p = 100% without mentioning that the balance it charges is $70.2M lower than it was six months ago.

11. The $45M of gross interest may be light. BEARISH, flagged not established, ≈ −$0.26.

FY2025 "Total other expense, net" is $52.0M and H1'26 shows the same line at $52.0M against H1'25 $55.1M (raw_verify.txt:1). That line nets interest expense, the $5.7M of H1 interest income and the UTP interest accrual, and it cannot be decomposed from the retrieved record. Built up instrument by instrument the memo's $45M is defensible ($200M @ 10.5% = $21.0M; $93.6M @ 8.22% = $7.7M; $180M of construction finance and finance leases at ~9% = $16.2M). I flag it as unverified rather than wrong, and note that if the true figure is nearer $55M the correction is −$0.26/share.

On the shape of the answer (parent question f)

12. The band asymmetry is roughly half genuine curvature and half a bear-cornered central. NEUTRAL — disclosed, not concealed.

Central $5.11 against a midpoint of $5.90 is a 79-cent gap. I priced the two candidate explanations:

All seven inputs at their range midpoints
(SG&amp;A 330, D_cogs 6%, capex 72.5, r 15.5%, Harvest 157.6, R 14%, p 75%):

EBITDA $282.7M · D&amp;A $130.3M · tax $27.9M · OE $137.3M · I $22.5M · g 2.295%
(137.3 − 22.5)/(0.155 − 0.02295) = $869.4M  + 325.4 + 157.6 − 297.3  =  $1,055.1M
÷ 192.57  =  $5.48

$5.48, not $5.90. So ~42 cents of the 79-cent gap is genuine concavity — the value function is concave in the inputs and the arithmetic midpoint of a band overstates. The remaining ~37 cents is the central leaning bear, and essentially all of it is p = 100% (the memo's own sensitivity: p 100% → 75% is worth $0.52). Six inputs sit at or near their midpoints; one sits at its extreme.

The "central" is not the bear case relabelled — but on the input that carries 20% of the range, it is. The memo publishes the p-sensitivity, so nothing is hidden; what is missing is the sentence acknowledging that its central sets one input at a corner.

13. Two credits I am obliged to give, because I went looking to break them and could not.

  • The EBITDA restatement survives a third, independent test. Pass 4 derived D&A-in-COGS two ways. Here is a third, from the FY2025 income statement rather than from EBITDA margins: pre-tax income $91.5M + total other expense $52.0M = EBIT $143.5M; the dossier's GAAP EBITDA of $330M therefore implies total D&A of $186.5M; the operating D&A line runs ~$117.6M annualised (H1'25 $58.8M × 2); residual D&A inside COGS ≈ $68.9M = 5.8% of FY2025 revenue — inside the memo's 5–7% and just above its 5.6% central. (Assumes the pipeline's EBITDA is struck before impairments; if not, the residual is smaller.) The single largest change in Revision 5 is correct, and if anything conservative — the Q4'25 disclosure (revenue $293M, GP $175M, adjusted EBITDA $105M) points to 7–8%.
  • §6 is arithmetically clean for the first time in five revisions. I reproduced every line: contribution 68.6c, variable SG&A 13.1c, pre-tax drag $0.555, post-tax $0.411, naive 135/0.411 = $328.5M = 37.0%, piecewise 105/0.555 = 21.3% with OE of exactly +$57M at that point (172 − 45 − 0 − 70), then 57/0.555 = 11.6%, total 32.9%. The sign is correct — the drag rises to $0.555 when the shield exhausts and cash goes negative sooner. Five revisions, five wrong numbers, and this one is right.

POINTING THE GATE IN BOTH DIRECTIONS (parent question g) — FIFTH TIME, AND THE ANSWER HAS CHANGED

Where did the ~50 cents go? I traced it, and it is fully accounted for.

Pass 4's corrected central was $5.62 (OE $142M ÷ 0.15 + Harvest $135M, no cash line, no tax charge). Revision 5 prints $5.11. The walk:

Step Δ value/share Running
Pass 4 corrected central — $5.62
Owner earnings $142M → $135M (gross interest −$21M; larger D&A tax shield +$19M; capex −$5M) at r = 15% −$0.24 $5.38
Cash added at face $325.4M (interest income now excluded from OE) +$1.69 $7.07
Harvest $135M → $160M +$0.13 $7.20
PV of the tax claim at p = 100%, five years, 15% −$2.08 $5.12 ≈ $5.11 ✓

The frame switch is legitimate and I verified it. Carrying cash at face while deducting gross interest and excluding interest income is internally consistent; pass 4's frame netted interest and omitted cash, which is the other consistent convention. Neither is a double count. The 51 cents is not a leak — it is the arithmetic consequence of doing exactly what pass 4 asked (charge the claim explicitly) inside a frame that also had to add cash back. No objection.

Every other number I could reproduce, I did:

Central grid: 135/0.15 + 325.4 + 160 − 401.1 = $984.3M ÷ 192.57 = $5.11 ✓
              r = 17% → $4.66 ✓        r = 14% → $5.40 ✓
Bull corner:  (169.8 − 35)/(0.14 − 0.03710) + 325.4 + 185.2 − 205.4  =  $8.38   ✓ ($8.37)
Bear corner:  (104.8 − 10)/(0.17 − 0.00954) + 325.4 + 130 − 382.8    =  $3.45   ✓ ($3.42)
p-sensitivity: 0% $7.19 ✓ · 50% $6.15 ✓ · 75% $5.63 ✓ · 100% $5.11 ✓
Width table:  all seven steps reproduce to the cent, and they sum to the bull corner ✓
Back-solve:   1.4x–2.3x corner-dependent ✓

The arithmetic of Revision 5 is the cleanest in five drafts. The defects are conceptual, and they are one-directional.

Have the corrections cancelled again? No — and that is the finding.

Finding Direction $/share at the centre
FATAL 2 — tax claim and licence formula priced in r and again explicitly Bull +$0.49 to +$0.97
FATAL 3a — p = 100% is the top of (50%, 100%], not an expectation Bull +$0.52
FATAL 3b — five years of accreting interest omitted from the claim PV Bear −$0.52 to −$0.66
NF 5 — maintenance capex from consolidated total spend Bull +$0.35 to +$0.52
NF 8 — reliability haircut on the Level 3 asset only, not the Level 3 liability Bull +$0.28
NF 9 — SG&A anchored to two GAAP quarters, one restructuring-contaminated Bull $0 to +$0.79
NF 11 — gross interest possibly light Bear −$0.26 (unverified)

Six bullish, two bearish, and the largest bearish item sits on the same line as the largest bullish one and offsets only that one. Net at the centre: +$0.85 to +$1.55.

The corrected record

Conservative restatement — taking only the two clearest corrections (r reduced 100bp for the tax-claim overlap; p at the neutral 75% with interest accreted) and leaving every other memo input untouched:

CENTRAL  r 14%, p 75% w/ accretion:  134.7/0.14 + 325.4 + 160 − 406.3   =  $1,041.2M  →  $5.41
BULL     r 13%, memo&#x27;s other bull inputs, p 50% w/ accretion            =  $1,684.1M  →  $8.74
BEAR     r 16%, memo&#x27;s other bear inputs, p 100% w/ accretion           =  $  568.8M  →  $2.95

Band $2.95 – $8.74 (2.96x).   Price 27% above the bull corner.
Bull corner with the claim erased (p = 0):  $10.19  — 8% below the price, not 17%.

Full restatement, adding the capex basis correction: central $5.95, bull corner $9.95, bull-corner-with-claim-erased $10.89 — which is the price.

Three consequences the memo must absorb:

  1. The "$9.44 even with the entire claim erased" line does not survive. Corrected it is $10.19–$10.89, and the last of those is $11.08 within rounding. Combined with FATAL 4's peer construction at $11.73, the memo's central rhetorical device — that the price is unreachable from every direction — is gone.
  2. The band widens rather than narrows. 2.45x → 2.96x conservative, 3.20x–3.28x on the full correction. §8 says "2.45x is well inside a factor of three (#4)." On the corrected record tripwire #4 is marginal and on the full correction it fires. That is an uncomfortable result and I report it because it is what the arithmetic gives.
  3. The verdict does not move. $5.41–$5.95 against $11.08. I tried to break WAIT in the bull's direction with every correction in this pass applied at once and could not get a defensible central above $6. To reach $11.08 you still need $237M–$307M of owner earnings against a corrected $145M.

And the diagnosis, which is new

Passes 3 and 4 found errors that pointed away from each other; the memo mistook that for convergence, was corrected, and stopped claiming it. Pass 5 finds errors that nearly all point the same way. That is not the same disease. Cancellation produces a right number from wrong reasons. Drift produces a wrong number — and here the drift is uniformly bearish, in a document whose author has spent five revisions being told his bear case was too harsh at the floor. The memo has internalised the criticism as a habit of severity rather than as a method.

(h) THE STOPPING QUESTION

Five revisions. Five passes. FATAL counts 4, 4, 5, 5, 4. The verdict has never changed. Is §0's argument sound, and should this publish?

§0's argument, tested

> "The verdict has been stable across five revisions not because the arithmetic converged on it, but because the gap between $11.08 and any defensible number is larger than five revisions' worth of $1–$3 errors could close."

The structure is sound and it is the right kind of claim — falsifiable, and about the size of the gap rather than the quality of the work. But the memo states it qualitatively when it could have stated it quantitatively, and quantifying it is what makes it hold. Here is the version it should have written:

Central estimate by revision:   $4.45 → $5.26 → $5.62 → $5.11 → $5.95 (pass 5 corrected)
Mean absolute move per pass:    ~$0.60
Gap remaining at the centre:    $11.08 − $5.95  =  $5.13
Passes required to close it at the observed rate, all moving one way:  ~8.5
Gap at the corrected bull corner: $11.08 − $9.95 = $1.13  →  ~2 passes

The refusal at the centre is safe by roughly eight passes' worth of error. The refusal at the bull corner is safe by about two. That is the honest shape of it, and it is why the decision is publishable and the conviction grade is not.

Would a sixth pass find a sixth set of FATALs?

Probably yes — and that is not, by itself, an argument against publishing. But the character of the findings has changed and that matters more than the count:

Pass Nature of the largest finding Directions
1–2 Arithmetic errors (SBC double-count, smuggled g) One way
3 Frame errors (net debt twice, rule suspended at the corner) Opposed, cancelling
4 Definitional error + wrong anchor instrument Opposed, cancelling
5 Interpretive errors — a misread price, a misread accounting standard, an unpropagated correction Same way

The arithmetic has stopped being the problem. I could not find a single reproduction failure in Revision 5 — the grid, the corners, the width table, the p-sensitivity and the stress test all tie. What is left is judgment about what observed facts mean, and no number of further passes settles that, because it is not the kind of thing a pass settles. A sixth pass would find interpretive disagreements, not errors, and that is the signal to stop.

Plainly, then

PUBLISH THE DECISION. DO NOT PUBLISH THE GRADE OR THE FOUR HEADLINES.

  • WAIT / 0% / no long, no short is right and is now supported by five independent attempts to break it, three of which were mine. Publish it.
  • Delete or rewrite four sentences: the 250bp "most telling fact"; "no coherent construction in this record reaches $11.08"; "$9.44 even with the claim erased"; and "it does not re-charge the tax claim… or the licence formula."
  • Regrade conviction from High to Moderate, on the ground that the memo's own conceded frame (EV/EBITDA at the peer set) reaches the price on the memo's own restated metric.
  • Restate the band as $2.95–$8.74, central ~$5.40–$5.95, and say plainly that the correction moved the estimate up and the width out.
  • Then stop. Not because five feels like enough, but because the marginal pass is now moving the estimate by less than a dollar against a five-dollar gap, and because what a sixth pass would find is a disagreement about what a bond price means — which is exactly the thing the memo should say it cannot resolve, rather than resolve wrongly for a sixth time.

THE REAL CHARLIE MUNGER'S TAKE

He fixed the stress test. Five revisions, five wrong numbers on one line, and this time I sat down with a pencil and went through it — the contribution margin, the variable selling expense, the pre-tax drag, the post-tax drag, the point where the tax shield runs out and the fifty-seven million dollars still sitting there — and every single figure ties. He got the sign right. He recomputed from the new base instead of patching it a fifth time. I'd been waiting four passes to write that sentence and there it is.

And he put the depreciation back in the plants. That's the biggest thing in the draft and I went and tested it a third way he didn't think of — took last year's pre-tax income, added back the other expense to get operating income, backed into total depreciation, took off the operating line — and it lands at five point eight cents on the revenue dollar, right where he put it. The man's largest correction is correct and it's if anything too small. Say it out loud before the rest.

Now.

He found a thing. Two hundred million dollars of ten-and-a-half-percent paper, and one line above it, three hundred sixty-eight million of eight percent paper redeemed the same month. And he wrote — and I'm quoting the thesis sentence — that this company's own cost of money rose two hundred fifty basis points while the regulatory news was getting better, and he called it the single most informative price in this record. Beautiful finding. It's wrong four different ways and every one of them is in the same paragraph he quoted from.

The old notes were due 2026. On the fifth of December 2025 they had months to live. The new ones run to 2030. You cannot take the coupon on a bond that's nearly matured, set against a five-year new issue, and call the difference a repricing of the borrower — that's a term premium, and for paper like this it's two hundred basis points on its own. And the eight percent wasn't a December-2025 price at all; it was struck years earlier when the Treasury paid one percent. Eight over one is seven hundred basis points of spread. Ten and a half over four is six hundred fifty. His borrower didn't get more expensive. His borrower got fifty basis points cheaper and the government got dearer. That's the same trick he caught himself doing with the eight-point-three percent return on capital — comparing a number from one tax regime to a number from another — one page earlier, in his own handwriting.

And read the rest of it. He says the new notes replaced the old ones. They didn't. The redemption settled on the fifth, paid in cash. The new paper closed on the seventeenth, and the filing says the money's for capital expenditures and general corporate purposes. That's not a refinancing, that's a company paying off a bond with its own money and then going out for fresh money twelve days later. And while it was at it, it paid off the Blue Ridge mortgage too. Three hundred eighty-four million retired, two hundred raised. It cut its borrowings nearly in half. Charlie — a fellow the market has just repriced two and a half points wider does not get to halve his debt in the same month. Those two facts won't sit in the same room.

Then the discount rate, and here's where he does something he expressly promises he isn't doing. He writes: built from one observed price plus one generic spread, so it does not re-charge the tax claim or the licence formula. Well — go and look at what was on the balance sheet the day those lenders priced that paper. Six hundred sixty-eight million dollars of uncertain tax position. More than he charges. Against two hundred fifty-five million of cash. And you're telling me the secured lender who demanded ten and a half at par put nothing in the price for a government claim two and a half times the company's cash? Of course he did. So the tax man is in the rate, and then he's taken out again at a hundred percent, two dollars and eight cents a share. That's the mortgage charged twice, in a new hat, in the very sentence that says it isn't.

And it's worse than ordinary double-counting, because he's charged the worst case. When you've already booked the disaster in full, the only surprises left are the good ones. Charging a risk premium on top of a certainty isn't conservatism — it's arithmetic run backwards.

Which brings me to the hundred percent, and this one he half knew. He writes, correctly, that ASC 740 means management judged the position not more likely than not to be sustained. That means the odds of losing are somewhere north of fifty. It does not mean a hundred. He states the rule right and then takes the top of the range and calls it deference to management. Deference would be the middle. And look where a hundred percent sits in his own table — it's the bear corner. Six of his inputs sit near their midpoints and the one worth a fifth of his whole range sits at the extreme, wearing a label that says central.

And then — he tells us interest on that claim compounds at fifteen million a quarter, uses it as the reason for the hundred percent, and never charges a penny of it. Not in the cash flow, not in the present value. Sixty million a year of accruing interest on the biggest liability in the file, cited as evidence and left out of the sum. So he's too harsh on the odds and too soft on the amount, on the same line, and the two nearly cancel. That's six times in three passes I've caught this document being right by accident.

Now the one that ought to sting most, because it's his own correction that did it to him. He puts the depreciation back and his EBITDA goes from two twenty-seven to two seventy-seven. Good. Then he writes, in his summary, graded High conviction: no coherent construction in this record reaches eleven-o-eight. Take his own two seventy-seven. Take his own debt, his own cash, his own Harvest mark, his own six-hundred-million charge at a hundred percent. Put the enterprise value over it. The stock at eleven dollars is seven-point-seven times, or nine-point-two with the tax man in it. And in his own dossier, Curaleaf is at nine-point-six. Run it back at Curaleaf's multiple and you get eleven seventy-three. That's above the price. At Revision Four's two twenty-seven the same sum gives nine twenty-three and the sentence was true. He fixed the number in the buyer's favour and then wrote a harder version of the sentence the fix had just killed. And two paragraphs later he concedes it himself — that buyer is not wrong about the multiple. Well then he's not wrong about the answer either.

The little ones. Seventy million of maintenance capital, annualised from thirty-four and a half in the first half — and the first half has five months of Harvest in it, and he says so in the same sentence and takes nothing off. That's the consolidated-versus-medical sin he's convicted three other men of. And the two quarters he averaged annualise to fifty-four and eighty-four. And Harvest: he takes thirteen and a half percent off a management estimate in an unaudited filing because it's a management estimate in an unaudited filing — and takes zero off the five hundred ninety-eight million, which is also a management estimate in the same unaudited filing. Sceptical about the asset, credulous about the liability, both times in the same direction.

The destination is right and I couldn't move it. I took every correction in this pass, applied them all at once in the bull's favour, and got five ninety-five, with a corner at nine ninety-five. The stock is eleven-o-eight. Sit on your ass. But the four sentences he's proudest of all have to come out, and he needs to notice that this time his mistakes didn't cancel — they all leaned the same way, and it was the harsh way. A man who's been told four times he was too rough on the floor eventually stops making that mistake by accident and starts making it on purpose.

THE REAL WARREN BUFFETT'S TAKE

Charlie's got the arithmetic. I want to talk about what happens to a man's judgment after five drafts, because that's the whole lesson in this file and it isn't the one anybody expected.

First the credit, and there's more of it than in any previous pass. He withdrew the convergence claim outright — didn't soften it, didn't restate it, wrote I withdraw that sentence. He put the media flag back on the ninety-five-million capital budget that the last draft had quietly promoted to load-bearing. He corrected "audited" to "unaudited," twice, against his own interest. He caught his own growth rule being an identity and refused to bank it as a second witness. And the stress test — I checked every line and it's right, for the first time in five tries. This is the best-executed draft of the five and it isn't close.

Now the thing that troubles me, and it's new. In the first two passes he made arithmetic mistakes. In the third and fourth he made mistakes that pointed opposite ways and cancelled — Charlie and I both told him so and he took it. In this one, six of his seven mistakes point the same direction, and it's the harsh direction. He is too high on the discount rate because he's charged the tax man inside it and again outside it. He's too high on the probability because he read an accounting threshold as a forecast. He's too high on the maintenance capital because he used a number that has a business he no longer owns inside it. He discounted the asset for being an estimate and took the liability at face. Every one of those is defensible standing alone. Put them end to end and they're eighty-five cents to a dollar fifty-five, all in one direction, and that isn't a pattern of error any more. That's a temperament.

Here's what I think happened, and I say it with sympathy because I've watched it happen to good analysts. He got told four times that his bear case was too hard on the business, and he learned to be hard on the business in ways nobody would call him on. A discount rate you've anchored to a real bond price is very difficult to argue with. A probability you've borrowed from the company's own accountants sounds like humility. Neither one is, and the reason I can tell is that they both move the same way.

On the bond, which is the part he was proudest of. He asked exactly the right question — what does this company actually pay for money? — and it's the question I'd have asked first, and it's the question nobody asked for four revisions. Then he found two numbers, an eight and a ten and a half, put them next to each other, and read a story into the difference. You cannot do that with bonds. One of them was nearly due and one runs five years. One was priced when Treasuries paid nothing and one when they paid four. One traded on an exchange and one was placed privately with people whose names we don't have. When you take out the term and the rate cycle, this company's credit spread is a shade tighter than it was, not two and a half points wider. And the company halved its borrowings in the same month, which is not what a repriced borrower gets to do.

I want to be careful here, because the ten-and-a-half is still real and using it as the floor of a cost of equity is still the right instinct — that part of the draft is an improvement and it should stay. What has to go is the story. He called it the single most informative price in this record. It's an informative price. It is not informative about the thing he says it's informative about.

On the six hundred million. He says he has no basis for assuming a better outcome than the company's own accounting. But the company's own accounting doesn't say what he thinks it says. It says a bookkeeping test failed — that a lawyer couldn't get to fifty-one percent on the technical merits, presuming the taxman shows up knowing everything. It says nothing at all about what gets written on the cheque, and the reason the rule is built that way is precisely so it won't. Most large arguments with the Internal Revenue Service end in the middle. He took the far end and called it modesty. Fifty-two cents a share, and it's the one input where his central sits in the corner.

And then he tells us the interest on it compounds and never charges the interest. Charlie's right that those two nearly cancel. I'd rather be told he doesn't know than watch two errors hold hands and call the result an estimate.

On the sentence he'll mind losing most. No coherent construction in this record reaches eleven-o-eight. He put his depreciation back where it belonged, which raised his earnings measure by fifty million, and he did not go back and check what that did to the sentence. It killed it. On his own restated number, at his own capital structure, with the whole tax claim charged at a hundred percent, this stock is trading at about nine times — and there's a nine-point-six sitting in his own dossier with Curaleaf's name on it. He even concedes the buyer has the right multiple. Well, a man with the right multiple applied to your own corrected metric arrives at your price. That doesn't make the buyer right about the business. It makes the sentence wrong.

On Rule Number One, which has not moved and will not. Two billion one hundred thirty million dollars. One state that matters. A moat that is a count of licences, granted by a board, under a statute that publishes the formula for doubling the count — twenty-eight becoming fifty. A largest liability of five hundred ninety-eight million that the government has said in public is not valid, that the company reserves in full, that accrues interest nobody has charged. Cash up seventy million while it borrowed fifty-nine at ten and a half percent senior secured — Charlie's right, the cushion is rented. A chief executive with zero purchases and one point seven million shares sold. And on the most generous arithmetic anybody has produced in five passes, it earns about a hundred and forty-five million dollars. That's a six-point-eight percent owner yield, no dividend, on a licence, against a four percent Treasury.

Would I buy it? No. And after five passes I can finally say why in one sentence that doesn't need a footnote: because the most generous honest number anyone has reached is about six dollars, the most generous corner is about ten, and the price is eleven — and the whole of it still sits on a selling-expense line no human being has ever printed.

Would I short it? No. No borrow cost in the record, no short interest, no days-to-cover, and — he says this himself and it's the best sentence in the draft — after producing five different values for one company he shouldn't be shorting the sixth.

Too hard? No, and for the reason he's argued correctly three drafts running. The deconsolidation closed the third of June, Harvest is equity-method, so the third-quarter statement is the medical-only statement, mechanically, about ten weeks out. That's a real date on a real calendar.

Should he publish? Yes — the decision. Not the grade, and not the four sentences he likes best. And then he should put the pencil down. Not because five is enough, but because what a sixth pass would argue about is what a bond price means, and that's the sort of thing an honest man says he can't settle instead of settling wrongly for the sixth time.

ONE-LINE VERDICT ON THE VERDICT

Four FATAL findings, and for the first time in three passes they do not cancel — six of the seven priceable errors run the same way, and it is the harsh way, which is a worse condition than cancellation because cancellation leaves the number right for wrong reasons while drift leaves it wrong. The discovery the memo is proudest of fails on the face of the paragraph it quotes: the 8.0% notes were due 2026 and were redeemed for cash on 5 December, twelve days before the 10.5% notes closed, whose stated use of proceeds is capital expenditure rather than refinancing, and against which the company retired $383.8M and raised $200M — a $184M net deleveraging — so that comparing a years-old coupon on near-maturity paper with a new five-year unlisted private placement measures a term premium and four points of risk-free rate, not a 250bp repricing of this borrower, whose credit spread on the same arithmetic actually tightened. The 15% cost of equity is anchored to a coupon that was struck with $668.4M of uncertain tax position on the balance sheet against $255.5M of cash, so it already prices the claim the memo then charges again in full at p = 100%, worth $0.49–$0.97 a share, in the very sentence asserting it does not; and that p = 100% reads an ASC 740-10 recognition threshold — which says only that P(sustained) ≤ 50% — as an expected cash outflow, installing the bear corner as the central case on the input carrying a fifth of the range, worth $0.52, while the $60M a year of interest the memo cites as its evidence is charged neither in owner earnings nor in the claim's present value, worth $0.66 the other way. And the Executive Summary's High-conviction claim that no coherent construction in this record reaches $11.08 was falsified by this draft's own headline correction: at the restated $277M of EBITDA the price is 7.7x enterprise value ex-claim and 9.2x including it, against Curaleaf at 9.6x in the memo's own dossier, which runs back to $11.73 — where at Revision 4's $227M the same sum gave $9.23 and the claim was true. TRLV: The Trulieve Family Newsletter

Trulieve Cannabis Corp. (NYSE: TRLV) — dinner table edition, August 26, 2026

1. The "Napkin" Pitch

Trulieve grows marijuana, processes it, and sells it — mostly to registered medical patients in Florida — through its own chain of stores. Think of it less like a corner shop and more like a winery that owns its own vineyards, its own bottling plant, and its own wine shops. It controls the whole chain from dirt to doorstep, which is unusual in this business.

2. The Good News (Why we like it)

The Moat: a government-issued fence around the yard. Florida doesn't let just anyone open a medical marijuana shop — you need a license, and licenses are scarce. Trulieve has 170 of Florida's roughly 770 dispensaries (22% of all the doors in the state) and sells 35% of all the flower bought in the state. It sells more than twice as much cannabis oil as its nearest competitor. That's not a moat built on customers loving the brand — it's a moat built on the state government limiting how many competitors can even show up.

The Secret Sauce got a tax cut. In April 2026, the federal government reclassified medical marijuana to a less-restrictive drug category. That sounds like a technicality, but it matters enormously: cannabis companies have long paid a punishing, non-standard tax rate because of an old law. This change starts easing that burden — but only for the medical side of the business, not the recreational side.

3. The Bad News (What could go wrong)

Banana Peel #1: A tax bill that could wipe out a quarter of the company. The IRS is disputing roughly $598 million that Trulieve claims it's owed back in tax refunds. To put that in perspective, the entire company is worth about $2.1 billion — so this single argument with the IRS is worth more than a quarter of Trulieve's total value. And the IRS hasn't been polite about it: they've publicly said Trulieve's refund claims "are not valid."

Banana Peel #2: The state is about to let 22 new competitors into the yard. Remember that nice, scarce-license moat? Florida is preparing to roughly double the number of licensed operators, from about 28 up to around 50. A fence that's about to grow a lot more gates in it protects the yard a lot less.

Banana Peel #3: The company just spun off part of itself and took a $403 million loss doing it. In June 2026, Trulieve moved its recreational (non-medical) business into a separate company called Harvest. Trulieve still gets 90% of the profits from Harvest — but zero say in how it's run. Imagine handing your car keys to your teenager, keeping the car payment, and having no say over how fast they drive it.

Banana Peel #4: No paycheck while you wait. Trulieve pays no dividend. If you buy the stock and it just sits there for two years while the tax dispute and licensing questions get sorted out, nobody sends you a check in the meantime. You're paid only if the stock price eventually rises — and only then.

4. The "Price Tag" Logic

Think of buying this stock like buying a house in a neighborhood where the boundary lines are genuinely in dispute — you don't know yet whether the lot is a quarter-acre or an acre.

  • The Steal Price: $3.15 — below this, you'd be getting the house for scrap value, tax dispute and all.
  • The Walk-Away Price: $5.85 — above this, even in a reasonably generous read of the numbers, you're paying more than the house is worth.
  • The Asking Price today: about $11.08–$11.32.

That means the house is on the market for roughly double what a careful buyer thinks it's worth even under a generous appraisal. Somebody looking to flip it quickly on the strength of "well, similar houses on this block sold for more" might justify the price — but a buyer planning to actually live in it, collecting the rent (the profits) year after year, cannot get there with any honest math.

5. The Final Verdict

WAIT. Not a Buy, not a Sell — a "come back later."

The panel of experts who looked at this (twelve of them) split 7 Pass, 3 Wait, 1 Sell, 1 Buy — hardly a chorus of enthusiasm. The recommended position size right now is zero — no stake at all, not even a small toe in the water.

This isn't "this company is doomed." It's "the price is asking us to already know the answers to two enormous open questions" — how the IRS fight resolves, and how much the coming flood of new competitors dents Trulieve's market share — and right now, nobody does. There's a real, useful clue arriving soon: Trulieve's next quarterly report (due roughly ten weeks from now) will, for the first time ever, show the medical-only business on its own — cutting through a lot of the fog. Worth checking back on then. Until it prices closer to that $5.85 ceiling, this one stays on the watch list, not in the cart.
